Namo tassa bhagavato arahato sammāsambuddhassa

Homage to the Blessed One, the Arahant, the Perfectly Self-Enlightened One.

Khuddakanikāye

The Minor Collection

Nettippakaraṇa-ṭīkā

Nettippakaraṇa-ṭīkā

Ganthārambhakathāvaṇṇanā

The Explanation of the Introduction

Saṃvaṇṇanārambhe [V 0.0001][M 0.0001] (dī. ni. ṭī. 1.ganthārambhakathāvaṇṇanā; ma. ni. ṭī. 1.1 ganthārambhakathāvaṇṇanā; saṃ. ni. ṭī. 1.1.1 ganthārambhakathāvaṇṇanā) ratanattayavandanā saṃvaṇṇetabbassa dhammassa pabhavanissayavisuddhipaṭivedanatthaṃ, taṃ pana dhammasaṃvaṇṇanāsu viññūnaṃ bahumānuppādanatthaṃ, taṃ sammadeva tesaṃ uggahaṇadhāraṇādikkamaladdhabbāya sammāpaṭipattiyā sabbahitasukhanipphādanatthaṃ. Atha vā maṅgalabhāvato, sabbakiriyāsu pubbakiccabhāvato, paṇḍitehi sammācaritabhāvato, āyatiṃ paresaṃ diṭṭhānugatiāpajjanato ca saṃvaṇṇanāyaṃ ratanattayapaṇāmakiriyā. Atha vā ratanattayapaṇāmakaraṇaṃ pūjanīyapūjāpuññavisesanibbattanatthaṃ, taṃ attano yathāladdhasampattinimittassa kammassa balānuppadānatthaṃ, antarā ca tassa asaṅkocanatthaṃ, tadubhayaṃ anantarāyena aṭṭhakathāya parisamāpanatthaṃ. Idameva ca payojanaṃ ācariyena idhādhippetaṃ. Tathā [V 0.0002] hi vakkhati “vandanājanitaṃ…pe… tassa tejasā”ti. Vatthuttayapūjā hi niratisayapuññakkhettasambuddhiyā aparimeyyappabhāvo puññātisayoti bahuvidhantarāyepi lokasannivāse antarāyanibandhanasakalasaṃkilesaviddhaṃsanāya pahoti, bhayādiupaddavañca nivāreti. Yathāha –

At the beginning of the commentary (Dī. Ni. ṭī. 1. Ganthārambhakathāvaṇṇanā; Ma. Ni. ṭī. 1.1 Ganthārambhakathāvaṇṇanā; Saṃ. Ni. ṭī. 1.1.1 Ganthārambhakathāvaṇṇanā), the homage to the Triple Gem is for the purpose of revealing the origin, basis, purity, and realization of the Dhamma being commented upon. Moreover, it is for producing great respect among the wise for the Dhamma commentaries, and for their proper practice, leading to the attainment of all welfare and happiness through correct learning, retention, and so forth. Alternatively, due to its auspicious nature, as it serves as a preliminary duty in all activities, because it is rightly performed by the wise, and to set an example for others to follow in the future, the act of paying homage to the Triple Gem is included in the commentary. Or again, paying homage to the Triple Gem generates the special merit of honoring what is worthy of honor, strengthening the power of one’s own accumulated merit that is the cause for the acquired good fortune, preventing the diminishing of that merit, and accomplishing both for the completion of the commentary without hindrance. This is indeed the purpose intended by the teacher here. As he will say: "The homage generated... by its power..." For the worship of the Triple Gem, being an unsurpassed field of merit conducive to supreme enlightenment, possessing immeasurable power and extraordinary merit, even amidst various obstacles in the world, is capable of dispelling all defilements arising from hindrances and averting dangers such as fear. As it is said—

“Pūjārahe pūjayato, buddhe yadi va sāvake”ti. (dha. pa. 195; apa. thera 1.10.1) ca,

"When one honors those worthy of honor, whether Buddhas or their disciples," (Dhammapada 195; Apadāna, Thera 1.10.1) and,

Tathā [M 0.0002] –

Likewise –

“Ye, bhikkhave, buddhe pasannā, agge te pasannā, agge kho pana pasannānaṃ aggo vipāko hotī”ti (a. ni. 4.34; itivu. 90) ca,

"Monks, those who have faith in the Buddha have faith in the foremost. And for those who have faith in the foremost, the result is foremost." (A. Ni. 4.34; Itivu. 90) and,

Tathā –

Likewise –

“‘Buddho’ti kittayantassa, kāye bhavati yā pīti;

Varameva hi sā pīti, kasiṇenapi jambudīpassa;

“‘Dhammo’ti…pe… ‘saṅgho’ti…pe… dīpassā”ti. (dī. ni. aṭṭha. 1.6; itivu. aṭṭha 90; dī. ni. ṭī. 1.ganthārambhakathāvaṇṇanā; ma. ni. ṭī. 1.1; a. ni. ṭī. 2.4.34) ca,

"When one praises 'Buddha,' whatever joy arises in the body; indeed, that joy is far superior to mastering the entire Jambudīpa with a kasiṇa. Similarly, when one praises 'Dhamma,' ... and when one praises 'Sangha,' ... (that joy is far superior to mastering the entire Jambudīpa with a kasiṇa)." (dī. ni. aṭṭha. 1.6; itivu. aṭṭha 90; dī. ni. ṭī. 1.ganthārambhakathāvaṇṇanā; ma. ni. ṭī. 1.1; a. ni. ṭī. 2.4.34) and,

Tathā –

Likewise –

“Yasmiṃ, mahānāma, samaye ariyasāvako tathāgataṃ anussarati, nevassa tasmiṃ samaye rāgapariyuṭṭhitaṃ cittaṃ hoti, na dosa…pe… na mohapariyuṭṭhitaṃ cittaṃ hotī”ti (a. ni. 6.10; 11.11) ca,

"At the time, Mahānāma, when a noble disciple recollects the Tathāgata, his mind is not overcome by lust, not by hatred, not by delusion." (A. Ni. 6.10; 11.11) and,

Tathā –

Likewise –

“Araññe [V 0.0003] rukkhamūle vā…pe…;

Bhayaṃ vā chambhitattaṃ vā, lomahaṃso na hessatī”ti. (saṃ. ni. 1.249) ca;

"In the forest, at the foot of a tree...; Fear or dread, or hair-raising terror will not arise." (Saṃ. Ni. 1.249) and;

Tattha yassa ratanattayassa vandanaṃ kattukāmo, tassa guṇātisayayogasandassanatthaṃ “mahākāruṇika”ntiādinā gāthāttayamāha. Guṇātisayayogena hi vandanārahabhāvo, vandanārahe ca katā vandanā yathādhippetappayojanaṃ sādhetīti. Tattha yassā saṃvaṇṇanaṃ kattukāmo, sā netti visesato yathānulomasāsanasannissayā, tassa ca vicittākārappavattivibhāvinī. Tathā hi suttantadesanā na vinayadesanā viya karuṇāppadhānā, nāpi abhidhammadesanā viya paññāppadhānā, atha kho karuṇāpaññāppadhānāti tadubhayappadhānadesanāvisesavibhāvanaṃ tāva sammāsambuddhassa thomanaṃ kātuṃ tammūlakattā sesaratanānaṃ “mahākāruṇikaṃ nātha”ntiādi vuttaṃ.

Here, for one who wishes to pay homage to the Triple Gem, three verses beginning with "mahākāruṇika" are spoken to demonstrate the connection with the excellence of qualities. For it is through the connection with the excellence of qualities that one becomes worthy of homage, and homage paid to one who is worthy achieves its intended purpose. Here, that Netti, which one wishes to comment upon, is specifically based on the teaching that accords with the sequence, and reveals the diverse nature of its manifestation. For the Sutta teaching is not predominantly compassion like the Vinaya teaching, nor predominantly wisdom like the Abhidhamma teaching, but rather it is predominantly both compassion and wisdom. Therefore, the phrase 'the great compassionate refuge' and so forth is stated, in order to praise the Perfectly Enlightened One and to show that the other Gems have their root in him, thereby revealing the special teaching that is predominant in both compassion and wisdom.

Tattha kiratīti (dī. ni. ṭī. 1.ganthārambhakathāvaṇṇanā; ma. ni. ṭī. 1.1; saṃ. ni. ṭī. 1.1.1; a. ni. ṭī. 1.1.1) karuṇā, paradukkhaṃ vikkhipati apanetīti attho. Atha vā kiṇātīti karuṇā, paradukkhe sati kāruṇikaṃ hiṃsati vibādhatīti attho[M 0.0003]. Kampanaṃ karotīti vā karuṇā, paradukkhe sati sādhūnaṃ hadayakhedaṃ karotīti attho. Kamiti vā sukhaṃ, taṃ rundhatīti karuṇā. Esā hi paradukkhāpanayanakāmatālakkhaṇā, attasukhanirapekkhatāya kāruṇikānaṃ sukhaṃ rundhati vibandhatīti attho. Kiriyati dukkhitesu pasāriyatīti vā karuṇā, karuṇāya niyuttoti kāruṇiko yathā “dovāriko”ti (a. ni. 7.67). Yathā hi dvāraṭṭhānato aññattha vattamānopi dvārapaṭibaddhajīviko puriso dvārānativattavuttitāya dvāre niyuttoti “dovāriko”ti vuccati, evaṃ bhagavā mettādivasena karuṇāvihārato aññattha vattamānopi karuṇānativattavuttitāya karuṇāya niyuttoti “kāruṇiko”ti vuccati. Mahābhinīhārato paṭṭhāya hi yāva mahāparinibbānā lokahitatthameva lokanāthā tiṭṭhantīti. Mahanto kāruṇikoti mahākāruṇiko. Satipi bhagavato tadaññaguṇānampi vasena mahantabhāve kāruṇikasaddasannidhānena vuttattā karuṇāvasenevettha mahantabhāvo veditabbo yathā “mahāveyyākaraṇo”ti. Evañca katvā “mahākāruṇiko”ti iminā padena puggalādhiṭṭhānena satthu mahākaruṇā vuttā hoti.

Here, 'kiratīti' (as explained in Dī. Ni. Ṭī. 1. Ganthārambhakathāvaṇṇanā; Ma. Ni. Ṭī. 1.1; Saṃ. Ni. Ṭī. 1.1.1; A. Ni. Ṭī. 1.1.1) denotes compassion, meaning it dispels or removes the suffering of others. Alternatively, 'kiṇātīti' is compassion, meaning it afflicts or disturbs the compassionate one when others suffer. Or, 'kampanaṃ karotīti' is compassion, meaning it causes distress in the hearts of the good when others suffer. Or, 'kamiti vā sukhaṃ, taṃ rundhatīti karuṇā'—compassion is that which obstructs happiness. Characterized by the desire to remove the suffering of others, it obstructs and hinders the happiness of the compassionate due to their indifference to their own happiness. Or, 'kiriyati dukkhitesu pasāriyatīti vā karuṇā'—compassion is that which is spread among the suffering. One devoted to compassion is called 'kāruṇiko,' just as a gatekeeper is called 'dovāriko' (A. Ni. 7.67). For just as a man whose livelihood is tied to the gate, though engaged elsewhere, is called 'dovāriko' because his mode of life does not transgress the gate, so too the Blessed One, though engaged in other abodes such as loving-kindness, is called 'kāruṇiko' because his mode of life does not transgress compassion. From the commencement of his great aspiration until his final great Parinibbāna, the Lords of the World remained solely for the welfare of the world. Thus, he is called the Greatly Compassionate One. Even though the Blessed One possesses other great qualities, the term 'kāruṇika' here signifies his greatness in compassion, as in the case of 'mahāveyyākaraṇo' (great expounder). Thus, by the term 'mahākāruṇiko,' the Teacher's great compassion is conveyed through the designation of the person.

Aparo nayo – atthasādhanato karuṇaṃ karuṇāyanaṃ karuṇāsampavattanaṃ arahatīti kāruṇiko[V 0.0004]. Bhagavato hi sabbaññutāya anavasesato sattānaṃ hitaṃ, hitupāyañca jānato, tattha ca akilāsuno hitesitā satthikā, na tathā aññesanti. Atha vā karuṇā karuṇāyanaṃ sīlaṃ pakati sabhāvo etassāti kāruṇiko. Bhagavā hi pathavīphassādayo viya kakkhaḷaphusanādisabhāvā karuṇāsabhāvo sabhāvabhūtakaruṇoti attho. Sesaṃ purimasadisameva. Atha vā mahāvisayatāya, mahānubhāvatāya, mahapphalatāya ca mahatī karuṇāti mahākaruṇā. Bhagavato hi karuṇā niravasesesu sattesu pavattati, pavattamānā ca anaññasādhāraṇā pavattati, diṭṭhadhammikādibhedañca mahantameva sattānaṃ hitasukhaṃ ekantato nipphādeti, mahākaruṇāya niyuttoti mahākāruṇiko, taṃ mahākāruṇikaṃ. Sesaṃ sabbaṃ vuttanayeneva veditabbaṃ. Sumāgadhādipadānaṃ viya cettha saddasiddhi veditabbā.

Another approach: 'Kāruṇiko' is one who is worthy of compassion, as compassion accomplishes welfare, is the means of compassion, and brings compassion into being. For the Blessed One, with his omniscience, unwearyingly knows the welfare and means of welfare for all beings, and his desire for their welfare is effective, unlike that of others. Or, compassion is the disposition, the inherent nature of this one; thus he is the Compassionate One. The Blessed One, like the earth element and others, has compassion as his natural essence—his very being is compassion. The rest is as previously explained. Or, because of its vast scope, great power, and immense fruitfulness, compassion is called great compassion. The Blessed One’s compassion extends to all beings without exception, and while active, it operates in a way unmatched by others. It brings about exclusively the great welfare and happiness of beings, whether in this life or subsequent ones. Thus, he is devoted to great compassion—the Greatly Compassionate One. The rest should be understood in the same way as before. Here, the derivation of words like 'Sumāgadha' should be understood similarly.

Nāthatīti [M 0.0004] nātho, veneyyānaṃ hitasukhaṃ āsīsati patthetīti attho, mettāyanavasena cettha hitasukhāsīsanaṃ veditabbaṃ, na karuṇāyanavasena paṭhamapadena vuttattā. Atha vā nāthati veneyyagataṃ kilesabyasanaṃ upatāpetīti nātho, nāthatīti vā nātho, yācatīti attho. Bhagavā hi “sādhu, bhikkhave, bhikkhu kālena kālaṃ attasampattiṃ paccavekkhitā”tiādinā (a. ni. 8.7, 8) sattānaṃ taṃ taṃ hitappaṭipattiṃ yācitvāpi mahākaruṇāya samussāhito te tattha niyojeti. Paramena vā cittissariyena samannāgato, sabbasatte vā sīlādiguṇehi īsati abhibhavatīti paramissaro bhagavā “nātho”ti vuccati, taṃ nāthaṃ.

He is a Protector (Nātha) because he desires (`nāthati`) the welfare and happiness of those to be trained. Here, this desire for welfare and happiness should be understood as arising from loving-kindness, not from compassion, since that was stated by the first term. Alternatively, he is a Protector (Nātha) because he torments (`nāthati`) the disaster of defilements found in those to be trained. Or, he is a Protector because he requests (`nāthati`), meaning he asks. Indeed, the Blessed One, spurred on by great compassion, urges beings towards their respective paths of welfare, even after having requested it of them, as indicated by the passage, 'Good, monks! A monk should from time to time reflect on his own accomplishment...' (A. 8.7-8). Or, because he is endowed with supreme sovereignty of mind, or because he is master over and surpasses all beings in virtues such as morality, the Blessed One, the supreme lord, is called 'Nātha'. To that Protector.

Ñātabbanti ñeyyaṃ, atītādibhedabhinnaṃ sabbaṃ saṅkhataṃ, asaṅkhatañca. Saṅgaraṇaṭṭhena sāgaro, patitapatitānaṃ attano puthulagambhīrabhāvehi saṃsīdanaṃ nimmujjanaṃ karotīti attho. Saṃ-saddassa cettha “sābhāvo, sārāgo”tiādīsu (dha. sa. 389, 391) viya niruttinayena daṭṭhabbo. Saṅgaraṇaṭṭhenāti vā saṅgarakaraṇaṭṭhena, ṭhitadhammatāya “ayaṃ me mariyādā, imaṃ velaṃ nātikkamāmī”ti lokena saṅgaraṃ saṅketaṃ karonto viya hotīti attho. Saṅgaraṇaṃ vā samantato galanaṃ sandanaṃ udakena karotīti sāgaro. Kappavuṭṭhānakāle hi mahāsamuddo ito cito ca paggharitvā sakalaṃ lokadhātuṃ ekoghaṃ karotīti. Lokiyā pana vadanti “sāgarassa rañño puttehi sāgarehi nibbattito khatoti sāgaro, puratthimo samuddappadeso, taṃsambandhatāya ruḷhivasena sabbopi samuddo tathā voharīyatī”ti. Sāgarasadisattā sāgaro, ñeyyameva sāgaroti ñeyyasāgaro. Sadisatā cettha puthuladuttaragambhīrānādikālikatāhi veditabbā, nihīnaṃ cetamopammaṃ. Tathā hi ñeyyasseva sātisayā puthulatā aparimāṇalokadhātubyāpanato, sabbaññutaññāṇasseva taraṇīyatāya duttaratā, gambhīratā, ādikoṭirahitā ca pavatti, na itarassa [V 0.0005] paricchinnadesattā bāhirakavītarāgehipi ittarena khaṇena atikkamitabbattā, parimitagambhīrattā, parimitakālattā ca. Ñeyyasāgarassa pāraṃ pariyantaṃ gatoti ñeyyasāgarapāragū, taṃ ñeyyasāgarapāraguṃ.

That which is to be known (`ñātabba`) is the knowable (`ñeyyaṃ`), which includes all conditioned and unconditioned phenomena, divided into past, etc. The ocean (`sāgaro`) is so called because it swallows (`saṅgaraṇaṭṭhena`), meaning it causes those who fall into it to sink and drown due to its vastness and depth. Here, the transformation of the prefix `saṃ-` to `sā-` should be understood according to etymological principles, as in `sābhāvo` and `sārāgo`. Alternatively, `saṅgaraṇaṭṭhena` means by making an agreement (`saṅgarakaraṇaṭṭhena`), as if it makes an agreement with the world, saying, 'This is my boundary; I do not cross this shore.' Or, the ocean (`sāgaro`) is so called because it causes water to flow and stream all around. At the time of the aeon's end, the great ocean overflows from all sides, making the entire world-system into a single mass of water. Worldly people say: 'The ocean (`sāgara`) was dug (`khato`) by the sons of King Sāgara, the Sāgaras; this refers to the eastern sea, but by association, the entire ocean is referred to as such.' Because it is like an ocean, it is an ocean (`sāgara`). The knowable itself is the ocean; hence, the 'ocean of the knowable' (`ñeyyasāgaro`). This resemblance should be understood in terms of vastness, being difficult to cross, depth, and beginninglessness, though this analogy is inferior. For the knowable is exceedingly vast, pervading immeasurable world-systems; it is difficult to cross, being traversable only by the knowledge of omniscience; it is profound, and its process is without a first beginning—unlike the other, which is limited in scope, can be crossed in a mere moment even by outsiders free from passion, and is finite in depth and duration. One who has gone to the far shore (`pāraṃ`) and limit (`pariyantaṃ`) of the ocean of the knowable is a `ñeyyasāgarapāragū`. To that one who has gone to the far shore of the ocean of the knowable.

Gamanañcettha [M 0.0005] ñāṇagamanameva, na itaraṃ ñeyyaggahaṇato, taṃ pana ñāṇaṃ duvidhaṃ sammasanapaṭivedhabhedato, tathā hetuphalabhedato. Tattha “kicchaṃ vatāyaṃ loko āpanno”tiādinā (dī. ni. 2.57; saṃ. ni. 2.4, 10; peṭako. 23) karuṇāyanavaseneva abhinivisitvā anekākāravokāre saṅkhāre sammasantaṃ bhagavato sammasanañāṇaṃ chattiṃsakoṭisatasahassamukhena ñeyyasāgaraṃ ajjhogāhetvā tassa pāraṃ pariyantaṃ agamāsi, yaṃ “mahāvajirañāṇa”nti vuccati. Paṭivedhañāṇaṃ pana sabbaññutaññāṇapadaṭṭhānaṃ āsavakkhayañāṇaṃ, āsavakkhayañāṇapadaṭṭhānañca sabbaññutaññāṇaṃ, yaṃ “mahābodhī”ti vuccati. Pāragamanañca tassa kiccasiddhiyā, samatthatāya ca veditabbaṃ. Tathā yathāvuttaṃ sammasanañāṇaṃ hetu, itaraṃ phalaṃ. Saha sammasanañāṇena vā āsavakkhayañāṇaṃ hetu, sabbaññutaññāṇaṃ phalaṃ tadānisaṃsabhāvatoti veditabbaṃ.

Here, the 'going' is indeed the going of knowledge, not any other kind, as it is the grasping of the knowable. That knowledge, however, is twofold: by the distinction of comprehension and penetration, and likewise by the distinction of cause and result. Herein, the Blessed One’s knowledge of comprehension, which comprehends the formations in their manifold and diverse aspects by way of compassion, with the thought, 'Alas, this world has fallen into suffering,' and so forth (Dī. Ni. 2.57; Saṃ. Ni. 2.4, 10; Peṭako. 23), plunged into the ocean of the knowable through thirty-six trillion openings and reached its far shore, the limit. This is called the 'Great Adamantine Knowledge.' The knowledge of penetration, however, is the knowledge of the destruction of the taints, which is the proximate cause for the knowledge of omniscience, and the knowledge of omniscience is the proximate cause for the knowledge of the destruction of the taints. This is called the 'Great Enlightenment.' And the going to the far shore should be understood by the accomplishment of its task and its capability. Likewise, as stated, the knowledge of comprehension is the cause, and the other is the result. Or, it should be understood that the knowledge of the destruction of the taints together with the knowledge of comprehension is the cause, and the knowledge of omniscience is the result, being its benefit.

Vandeti namāmi, abhitthavāmi vā. Saṇhaṭṭhena nipuṇā, anupacitañāṇasambhārānaṃ agādhaṭṭhena gambhīrā, ekattādibhedato nandiyāvaṭṭādivibhāgato ca vicitrā visiṭṭhā nānāvidhā nayā etissāti nipuṇagambhīravicitranayā, nipuṇagambhīravicitranayā desanā assāti nipuṇagambhīravicitranayadesano, taṃ nipuṇa…pe… desanaṃ. Nayatīti vā nayo, pāḷigati, sā ca vuttanayena atthato nipuṇā, atthato byañjanato ca gambhīrā, saṅkhepavitthārānulomādippavattiyā nānāvidhatāya vicitrā. Tathā hi paññattianupaññattiādivasena, saṃkilesabhāgiyādilokiyāditadubhayavomissatādivasena, kusalādikhandhādisaṅgahādisamayavimuttādiṭhapanādikusalamūlāditikapaṭṭhānādivasena ca anekavidhā pāḷigatīti.

`Vande` means I salute, or I praise. The teaching is subtle (`nipuṇā`) by reason of its fineness; profound (`gambhīrā`) by reason of its unfathomable depth for those whose accumulations of knowledge are undeveloped; and it has diverse (`vicitrā`), distinguished, and manifold methods (`nayā`) due to distinctions such as unity, and divisions such as the Nandiyāvaṭṭa. He is one whose teaching has subtle, profound, and diverse methods (`nipuṇagambhīravicitranayadesano`). To that teaching (`desanaṃ`) which is subtle, profound, and has diverse methods. Or, a method (`nayo`) is that which leads (`nayati`); it is the course of the Pāli text. And that course is subtle in meaning; profound in both meaning and expression; and diverse in its various applications, such as the concise and the detailed, the concordant, and so on. For indeed, the course of the Pāli text is manifold: by way of designations and secondary designations, etc.; by way of what pertains to defilement, what is worldly, what is mixed with both, etc.; by way of the wholesome, the aggregates, the compendia, the establishment of the temporarily liberated, the wholesome roots, the triads of the Paṭṭhāna, etc.

Tattha (dī. ni. ṭī. 1.ganthārambhakathāvaṇṇanā) dvīhākārehi bhagavato thomanā veditabbā attahitasampattito, parahitappaṭipattito ca. Tesu attahitasampatti anāvaraṇañāṇādhigamato, savāsanānaṃ sabbesaṃ kilesānaṃ accantappahānato ca veditabbā, parahitappaṭipatti lābhasakkārādinirapekkhacittassa sabbadukkhaniyyānikadhammadesanato, paṭiviruddhesupi niccaṃ hitajjhāsayañāṇaparipākakālāgamanato ca veditabbā. Sā panettha payogato, āsayato ca duvidhā, parahitappaṭipatti, yathāvuttabhedā duvidhā ca attahitasampatti pakāsitā [V 0.0006] hoti. Kathaṃ? “Mahākāruṇika”nti iminā āsayato[M 0.0006], “nipuṇa…pe… desana”nti iminā payogato, “nātha”nti iminā pana ubhayathāpi bhagavato parahitappaṭipatti pakāsitā karuṇākiccadīpanato, “ñeyyasāgarapāragu”nti iminā sātisayaṃ attahitasampatti paramukkaṃsagatañāṇakiccadīpanato.

Therein, the praise of the Blessed One should be understood in two aspects: from the accomplishment of his own welfare, and from the practice for the welfare of others. Among these, the accomplishment of his own welfare should be understood from his attainment of unobstructed knowledge and from his utter abandonment of all defilements together with their latent tendencies. The practice for the welfare of others should be understood from his teaching of the Dhamma that leads out from all suffering with a mind unconcerned with gain and honor, and from his constant disposition for the welfare of even those who are hostile, awaiting the time for the maturing of their knowledge. Herein, that practice for the welfare of others is shown to be twofold, by way of application and intention, and the accomplishment of his own welfare, with its two aforementioned distinctions, is also shown. How so? By 'the greatly compassionate one,' the practice for the welfare of others is shown by way of intention. By 'the teaching which is subtle...,' it is shown by way of application. By 'the Protector,' however, the Blessed One's practice for the welfare of others is shown in both ways, as it reveals the function of compassion. By 'one who has gone to the far shore of the ocean of the knowable,' his surpassing accomplishment of his own welfare is shown, as it reveals the function of knowledge that has reached the supreme summit.

Atha vā tīhākārehi bhagavato thomanā veditabbā hetuto, phalato, upakārato ca. Tattha hetu mahākaruṇā, sā pana paṭhamapadena sarūpeneva dassitā. Phalaṃ catubbidhaṃ ñāṇasampadā pahānasampadā ānubhāvasampadā rūpakāyasampadā cāti. Tāsu padhānabhūtā ñāṇapahānasampadā “ñeyyasāgarapāragu”nti iminā padena pakāsitā. Padhāne hi dassite avinābhāvato itarampi dvayaṃ dassitameva hoti. Na hi buddhānaṃ ānubhāvarūpakāyasampattīhi vinā kadācipi dhammakāyasirī vattatīti. Upakāro anantaraṃ abāhiraṃ katvā tividhayānamukhena vimuttidhammadesanā, sā “nāthaṃ, nipuṇa…pe… desana”nti padadvayena pakāsitāti veditabbaṃ.

Alternatively, the praise of the Blessed One should be understood through three aspects: cause, fruit, and benefit. Here, the cause is great compassion, which is shown in the first term by its own nature. The fruit is fourfold: the accomplishment of knowledge, the accomplishment of abandoning, the accomplishment of power, and the accomplishment of the form-body. Among these, the accomplishments of knowledge and abandoning, being the principal ones, are indicated by the term, 'one who has gone to the far shore of the ocean of the knowable.' For when the principal aspects are shown, the other two are also shown, due to their inseparability. Indeed, the splendor of the Dhamma-body of the Buddhas never exists without the accomplishments of power and the form-body. The benefit is the teaching of the Dhamma of liberation through the gateway of the three vehicles, including all without exception. This is shown by the two terms, 'the Protector' and 'the teaching which is subtle...,' and should be understood as such.

Tattha (dī. ni. ṭī. 1.ganthārambhakathāvaṇṇanā) “mahākāruṇika”nti etena sammāsambodhiyā mūlaṃ dasseti. Mahākaruṇāsañcoditamānaso hi bhagavā saṃsārapaṅkato sattānaṃ samuddharaṇatthaṃ katābhinīhāro anupubbena pāramiyo pūretvā anuttaraṃ sammāsambodhiṃ adhigatoti karuṇā sammāsambodhiyā mūlaṃ. “Ñeyyasāgarapāragu”nti etena pubbabhāgappaṭipattiyā saddhiṃ sammāsambodhiṃ dasseti. Anāvaraṇañāṇapadaṭṭhānañhi maggañāṇaṃ, maggañāṇapadaṭṭhānañca anāvaraṇañāṇaṃ “sammāsambodhī”ti vuccati. Vuttappabhedaṃ pana sammasanañāṇaṃ saha paññāpāramiyā tassā pubbabhāgapaṭipadā. Tassā hi ānubhāvena līnuddhaccapatiṭṭhānāyūhanakāmasukhallikattakilamathānuyogasassatucchedādiantadvayavirahitā ukkaṃsapāramippattā majjhimā paṭipadā bhāvanāpāripūriṃ gatā. “Nātha”nti iminā sammāsambodhiyā phalaṃ dasseti lokattayanāyakabhāvadīpanato. Tathā hi sabbānatthaparihārapubbaṅgamāya niravasesahitasukhavidhānatapparāya niratisayāya payogasampattiyā, sadevamanussāya pajāya accantupakāritāya aparimitanirupamabhāvaguṇavisesasamaṅgitāya ca sabbasattuttamo bhagavā aparimāṇāsu lokadhātūsu aparimāṇānaṃ sattānaṃ anuttaragāravaṭṭhānabhūtatāya ca “nātho”ti vuccatīti. “Nipuṇa…pe… desana”nti iminā sammāsambodhiyā payojanaṃ [M 0.0007] dasseti. Saṃsāramahoghato sattasantāraṇatthañhi bhagavatā sammāsambodhi abhipatthitā, tañca sattasantāraṇaṃ yathāvuttadesanāsampattiyā samijjhati tadavinābhāvato. Iminā bhagavato sātisayā parahitappaṭipatti dassitā, itarehi attahitasampattīti tadubhayena attahitāya paṭipannādīsu catūsu puggalesu bhagavato catutthapuggalabhāvaṃ [V 0.0007] dīpeti, tena ca anuttaradakkhiṇeyyabhāvaṃ, uttamavandanīyabhāvaṃ, attano ca vandanakiriyāya khettaṅgatabhāvaṃ dīpeti.

Here, by "the Greatly Compassionate One," the root of perfect enlightenment is shown. For the Blessed One, his mind stirred by great compassion, having made the aspiration to lift beings from the swamp of saṃsāra, fulfilled the perfections step by step and attained unsurpassed perfect enlightenment—thus, compassion is the root of perfect enlightenment. By "the One Who Has Crossed to the Far Shore of the Ocean of Knowables," perfect enlightenment along with the preliminary practice is shown. For path-knowledge has unobstructed knowledge as its proximate cause, and unobstructed knowledge has path-knowledge as its proximate cause—this is called "perfect enlightenment." Moreover, the knowledge of comprehension, along with the perfection of wisdom, is its preliminary practice. By its power, the middle path of practice, free from the two extremes such as indulgence in sensual pleasures, the practice of self-mortification, eternalism, and annihilationism, and from states of sloth, restlessness, inertia, and striving, having reached the highest perfection, attains the complete fulfillment of development. By "Protector," the fruit of perfect enlightenment is shown, by revealing his state as leader of the three worlds. Indeed, the Blessed One, because of the unsurpassed accomplishment of his efforts which, preceded by the removal of all that is harmful, are intent on providing complete welfare and happiness; due to his being extremely beneficial to all people, including gods and humans; and due to the possession of immeasurable and incomparable special qualities, is the supreme being among all beings, and is called "Protector" because he is the basis of unsurpassed reverence for immeasurable beings in immeasurable world-systems. By "Skillful… in teaching," the purpose of perfect enlightenment is shown. For the Blessed One aspired to perfect enlightenment to rescue beings from the great flood of saṃsāra, and this rescue is accomplished through the excellence of teaching as described, as it is inseparable therefrom. By this, the Blessed One’s pre-eminent practice for the welfare of others is shown, while by the others, the accomplishment of his own welfare is shown. Thus, among the four types of persons practicing for their own welfare and for the welfare of others, the Blessed One is revealed as being the fourth type. Thereby, his state as an unsurpassed recipient of offerings, his supreme worthiness of veneration, and his status as a field for one's act of veneration are revealed.

Ettha ca yathā “mahākāruṇika”nti iminā padena bhagavato mahākaruṇā dassitā, evaṃ “ñeyyasāgarapāragu”nti etena mahāpaññā dassitā. Tesu karuṇāggahaṇena lokiyesu mahaggatabhāvappattāsādhāraṇaguṇadīpanato bhagavato sabbalokiyaguṇasampatti dassitā hoti, paññāggahaṇena sabbaññutaññāṇapadaṭṭhānamaggañāṇadīpanato sabbalokuttaraguṇasampatti. Tadubhayaggahaṇasiddho eva cattho nāthasaddena pakāsīyati. Karuṇāvacanena upagamanaṃ nirupakkilesaṃ dasseti, paññāvacanena apagamanaṃ. Tathā karuṇāggahaṇena lokasamaññānurūpaṃ bhagavato pavattiṃ dasseti lokavohāravisayattā karuṇāya, paññāggahaṇena samaññāya anatidhāvanaṃ. Sabhāvānavabodhena hi dhammānaṃ samaññaṃ atidhāvitvā sattādisammasanaṃ hotīti. Tathā karuṇāggahaṇena mahākaruṇāsamāpattivihāraṃ dasseti, paññāggahaṇena tīsu kālesu appaṭihatañāṇaṃ, catusaccañāṇaṃ, catupaṭisambhidāñāṇaṃ, catuvesārajjañāṇaṃ. Karuṇāggahaṇena mahākaruṇāsamāpattiñāṇassa gahitattā sesāsādhāraṇañāṇāni, cha abhiññā, aṭṭhasu parisāsu (ma. ni. 1.151, 178) akampanañāṇāni, dasa balāni, cuddasa buddhañāṇāni, soḷasa ñāṇacariyā, aṭṭhārasa buddhadhammā (mahāni. 69, 156; cūḷani. mogharājamāṇavapucchāniddesa 85; paṭi. ma. 3.5; dī. ni. aṭṭha. 3.305; vibha. mūlaṭī. suttantabhājanīyavaṇṇanā; dī. ni. ṭī. 3.141), catucattālīsa ñāṇavatthūni sattasattati ñāṇavatthūnīti (saṃ. ni. 2.34) evamādīnaṃ anekesaṃ paññāppabhedānaṃ vasena ñāṇacāraṃ dasseti.

Here, just as by the term 'the Greatly Compassionate One' the Blessed One's great compassion is shown, so too by 'the One Who Has Crossed to the Far Shore of the Ocean of Knowables' his great wisdom is shown. By the mention of compassion, the Blessed One's accomplishment of all worldly virtues is shown, since it reveals the special qualities attained by those in the world who have reached a sublime state. By the mention of wisdom, his accomplishment of all supramundane virtues is shown, since it reveals the path-knowledge which is the proximate cause of the knowledge of omniscience. The meaning established by mentioning both is clarified by the word 'Protector.' The word 'compassion' shows approaching without defilement, while the word 'wisdom' shows departing. Similarly, by the mention of compassion, the Blessed One's conduct conforming to worldly convention is shown, because compassion is in the domain of worldly parlance; by the mention of wisdom, non-transgression of convention is shown, for by not understanding the inherent nature of phenomena, convention is transgressed, and grasping at the notion of a 'being,' etc., occurs. Similarly, by the mention of compassion, dwelling in the attainment of great compassion is shown; by the mention of wisdom, unimpeded knowledge in the three times, knowledge of the four truths, knowledge of the four analytical knowledges, and knowledge of the four kinds of confidence are shown. And since the knowledge of the attainment of great compassion is included by the mention of compassion, by the mention of wisdom the range of knowledge is shown through its many divisions, by way of the remaining special knowledges such as the six higher knowledges, unshakable knowledge in the eight assemblies (Majjhima Nikāya 1.151, 178), the ten powers, the fourteen Buddha-knowledges, the sixteen modes of knowing, the eighteen unique qualities of a Buddha (Mahāniddesa 69, 156; Cūḷaniddesa, Mogharājamāṇavapucchāniddesa 85; Paṭisambhidāmagga 3.5; Dīgha Nikāya Aṭṭhakathā 3.305; Vibhaṅga Mūlaṭīkā, Suttantabhājanīyavaṇṇanā; Dīgha Nikāya Ṭīkā 3.141), the forty-four bases of knowledge, and the seventy-seven bases of knowledge (Saṃyutta Nikāya 2.34).

Tathā karuṇāggahaṇena caraṇasampatti, paññāggahaṇena vijjāsampatti. Karuṇāggahaṇena sattādhipatitā, paññāggahaṇena dhammādhipatitā. Karuṇāggahaṇena [M 0.0008] lokanāthabhāvo, paññāggahaṇena attanāthabhāvo. Tathā karuṇāggahaṇena pubbakāribhāvo, paññāggahaṇena kataññutā. Karuṇāggahaṇena aparantapatā, paññāggahaṇena anattantapatā. Karuṇāggahaṇena vā buddhakaradhammasiddhi, paññāggahaṇena buddhabhāvasiddhi. Tathā karuṇāggahaṇena paresaṃ tāraṇaṃ, paññāggahaṇena sayaṃ tāraṇaṃ. Tathā karuṇāggahaṇena sabbasattesu anuggahacittatā, paññāggahaṇena sabbadhammesu virattacittatā dassitā hoti. Sabbesañca buddhaguṇānaṃ karuṇā ādi taṃnidānabhāvato, paññā pariyosānaṃ tato uttari karaṇīyābhāvato, iti ādipariyosānadassanena sabbe buddhaguṇā dassitā honti. Tathā karuṇāvacanena sīlakkhandhapubbaṅgamo samādhikkhandho dassito hoti. Karuṇānidānañhi sīlaṃ tato pāṇātipātādiviratippavattito, sā [V 0.0008] ca jhānattayasampayoginīti. Paññāvacanena paññākkhandho. Sīlañca sabbabuddhaguṇānaṃ ādi, samādhi majjhe, paññā pariyosānanti evampi ādimajjhapariyosānakalyāṇā sabbe buddhaguṇā dassitā honti nayato dassitattā. Eso eva hi niravasesato buddhaguṇānaṃ dassanupāyo, yadidaṃ nayaggāhaṇaṃ, aññathā ko nāma samattho bhagavato guṇe anupadaṃ niravasesato dassetuṃ. Tenevāha –

Similarly, by the mention of compassion, the accomplishment of conduct is shown; by the mention of wisdom, the accomplishment of knowledge is shown. By the mention of compassion, lordship over beings is shown; by the mention of wisdom, lordship over the Dhamma is shown. By the mention of compassion, the state of being a protector of the world is shown; by the mention of wisdom, the state of being a protector of oneself is shown. Similarly, by the mention of compassion, the state of being a benefactor is shown; by the mention of wisdom, gratitude is shown. By the mention of compassion, the state of not afflicting others is shown; by the mention of wisdom, the state of not afflicting oneself is shown. Or, by the mention of compassion, the accomplishment of the qualities that make one a Buddha is shown; by the mention of wisdom, the accomplishment of Buddhahood is shown. Similarly, by the mention of compassion, the saving of others is shown; by the mention of wisdom, the saving of oneself is shown. Similarly, by the mention of compassion, a mind of benevolence toward all beings is shown; by the mention of wisdom, a mind of dispassion toward all phenomena is shown. And compassion is the beginning of all the Buddha's qualities, as it is their root cause; wisdom is their culmination, as there is nothing further to be done beyond it. Thus, by showing the beginning and the culmination, all the Buddha's qualities are revealed. Similarly, by the word 'compassion,' the aggregate of concentration, which is preceded by the aggregate of virtue, is shown. For virtue has compassion as its origin, as it proceeds from the abstinence from killing living beings and so on, and that compassion is connected with the three jhānas. By the word 'wisdom,' the aggregate of wisdom is shown. And since virtue is the beginning of all the Buddha's qualities, concentration the middle, and wisdom the culmination, in this way too all the Buddha's qualities are shown as beautiful in the beginning, the middle, and the end, because they are shown by this method. This indeed is the method for revealing all the Buddha's qualities without omission, namely, the adoption of this method. Otherwise, who indeed would be capable of revealing all the virtues of the Blessed One, one by one, without omission? Therefore, it is said—

“Buddhopi buddhassa bhaṇeyya vaṇṇaṃ, kappampi ce aññamabhāsamāno;

Khīyetha kappo ciradīghamantare, vaṇṇo na khīyetha tathāgatassā”ti. (dī. ni. aṭṭha. 1.304; 3.141; ma. ni. aṭṭha. 3.425; udā. aṭṭha. 53; apa. aṭṭha. 2.7.20; bu. vaṃ. aṭṭha. 4.4; cariyā. aṭṭha. nidānakathā, pakiṇṇakakathā; dī. ni. ṭī. 1.ganthārambhakathāvaṇṇanā; ma. ni. ṭī. 1.1; saṃ. ni. ṭī. 1.1.1; a. ni. ṭī. 1.1.1; vajira. ṭī. ganthārambhakathā; sārattha. ṭī. 1.ganthārambhakathāvaṇṇanā);

'Even a Buddha might speak the praise of a Buddha, / If he were to speak for an entire aeon, not speaking of anything else; / That aeon would come to an end, long though it be, / But the praise of the Tathāgata would never be exhausted.' (Dīgha Nikāya Aṭṭhakathā 1.304, 3.141; Majjhima Nikāya Aṭṭhakathā 3.425; Udāna Aṭṭhakathā 53; Apadāna Aṭṭhakathā 2.7.20; Buddhavaṃsa Aṭṭhakathā 4.4; Cariyāpiṭaka Aṭṭhakathā, Nidānakathā, Pakiṇṇakakathā; Dīgha Nikāya Ṭīkā 1, Ganthārambhakathāvaṇṇanā; Majjhima Nikāya Ṭīkā 1.1; Saṃyutta Nikāya Ṭīkā 1.1.1; Aṅguttara Nikāya Ṭīkā 1.1.1; Vajirabuddhi Ṭīkā, Ganthārambhakathā; Sāratthappakāsinī Ṭīkā 1, Ganthārambhakathāvaṇṇanā)

Teneva ca āyasmatā sāriputtattherenāpi buddhaguṇaparicchedanaṃ pati anuyuttena “no hetaṃ, bhante”ti (dī. ni. 2.145) paṭikkhipitvā “apica me, bhante, dhammanvayo vidito”ti (dī. ni. 2.146) vuttaṃ.

For that very reason, when the Venerable Elder Sāriputta was questioned concerning the delimitation of the Buddha's qualities, he rejected the proposition, saying, "No, indeed not, venerable sir," (Dīgha Nikāya 2.145) and added, "However, venerable sir, the course of the Dhamma is known to me." (Dīgha Nikāya 2.146).

Evaṃ saṅkhepena sakalasabbaññuguṇehi bhagavantaṃ abhitthavitvā idāni saddhammaṃ thometuṃ “vijjācaraṇasampannā”tiādimāha. Tattha vijjācaraṇasampannā hutvāti vacanaseso. Vindiyaṃ dhammānaṃ salakkhaṇaṃ, sāmaññalakkhaṇañca [M 0.0009] vindatīti vijjā, lobhakkhandhādīni vā vijjhanaṭṭhena vijjā, catunnaṃ vā ariyasaccānaṃ viditakaraṇaṭṭhena vijjāti evaṃ tāvettha vacanatthato vijjā veditabbā. Pabhedato pana tissopi vijjā vijjā bhayabheravasutte āgataniyāmeneva, aṭṭhapi vijjā vijjā ambaṭṭhasuttādīsu (dī. ni. 1.278 ādayo) āgataniyāmeneva. Caranti tehīti caraṇāni, sīlasaṃvarādayo pañcadasa dhammā, iti imāhi vijjāhi, imehi ca caraṇehi sampannā sampannāgatāti vijjācaraṇasampannā.

Having thus briefly extolled the Blessed One with all the qualities of omniscience, now, to praise the True Dhamma, he begins with 'endowed with knowledge and conduct.' Herein, 'having become endowed with knowledge and conduct' is the remainder of the statement. Knowledge (vijjā) is so called because it discerns the specific characteristics and general characteristics of phenomena; or because it pierces the aggregates of greed and other defilements; or because it makes known the four noble truths. Thus, the meaning of the term 'knowledge' should be understood in this way from its etymology. In terms of classification, there are the three kinds of knowledge, as mentioned in the Bhayabherava Sutta, and the eight kinds of knowledge, as found in the Ambaṭṭha Sutta and other texts (Dī. Ni. 1.278 ff.). Conduct (caraṇa) is so called because one proceeds by means of them. These are the fifteen qualities beginning with moral restraint. Thus, those who are endowed with these kinds of knowledge and these kinds of conduct are called 'endowed with knowledge and conduct.'

Yenāti yena dhammena karaṇabhūtena, hetubhūtena ca. Tattha maggadhammassa karaṇattho veditabbo niyyānakiriyāsādhakatamabhāvato, nibbānadhammassa hetuattho ārammaṇapaccayabhāvato. Paccayattho hi [V 0.0009] ayaṃ hetvattho. Pariyattidhammassapi hetuattho yujjateva paramparāya hetubhāvato. Phaladhamme pana ubhayampi sambhavati. Kathaṃ? “Tāya saddhāya avūpasantāyā”ti vacanato maggena samucchinnānaṃ kilesānaṃ paṭippassaddhippahānakiccatāya phalassa niyyānānuguṇatā, niyyānapariyosānatā cāti iminā pariyāyena siyā karaṇattho niyyānakiriyāya. Sakadāgāmimaggavipassanādīnaṃ pana upanissayapaccayabhāvato siyā hetuattho. Evañca katvā aggappasādasuttādīsu (itivu. 90) aggādibhāvena aggahitāpi phalapariyattidhammā chattamāṇavakavimānādīsu (vi. va. 886 ādayo) saraṇīyabhāvena gahitāti tesaṃ magganibbānānaṃ viya mahāaṭṭhakathāyaṃ saraṇabhāvo uddhaṭo. Visesato cettha maggapariyāpannā eva vijjācaraṇadhammā veditabbā. Te hi nippariyāyena niyyānakiriyāya sādhakatamabhūtā, na itare. Itaresaṃ pana niyyānatthatāya niyyānatā. Yadi evaṃ kasmā “vijjācaraṇasampannā hutvā”ti vuttaṃ, niyyānasamakālameva hi yathāvuttavijjācaraṇasampattisamadhigamoti? Nāyaṃ virodho samānakālatāya eva adhippetattā yathā “cakkhuñca paṭicca rūpe ca uppajjati cakkhuviññāṇa”nti (ma. ni. 1.204, 400; 3.421, 425, 426; saṃ. ni. 2.43-45; 4.60; kathā. 465, 467). Sampannāti vā padassa vattamānakālatthatā veditabbā “uppannā dhammā”ti (dha. sa. tikamātikā 17) ettha uppannasaddassa viya. Evañca katvā vacanasesamantareneva padayojanā siddhā hoti. “Yenā”ti [M 0.0010] ca padaṃ ubhayattha sambandhitabbaṃ “yena dhammena vijjācaraṇasampannā, yena dhammena niyyantī”ti.

By which: by which Dhamma, as instrumental and as causal. Therein, the instrumental sense for the path-Dhamma is to be understood from its being the most effective means for the act of liberation; the causal sense for the Nibbāna-Dhamma is from its being an object-condition. For this causal sense is indeed the sense of a condition. The causal sense also applies to the scriptural Dhamma, from its being a cause in succession. In the case of the fruit-Dhamma, both are possible. How? From the statement, 'By that unwaning faith,' the fruit's conformity to liberation and its culmination in liberation—by this mode, the instrumental sense in the act of liberation may apply due to the fruit's function of calming and abandoning the defilements cut off by the path. Moreover, for the insight leading to the once-returner's path, etc., the causal sense may apply due to their being decisive-support conditions. And so, even though the Dhamma of the fruit and the scriptures are taken as supreme, etc., in suttas like the Aggappasāda Sutta (Itivu. 90), and as objects of recollection in stories like the Chattamāṇavaka Vimāna (Vi. Va. 886 ff.), their nature as a refuge is explained in the Great Commentary, just like the path and Nibbāna. Here, specifically, the dhammas of knowledge and conduct included in the path should be understood. For they are unambiguously the most effective means in accomplishing the act of liberation, not the others. For the others, however, their nature of leading out is because their purpose is leading out. If so, why is it said, 'having become endowed with knowledge and conduct'? Because the attainment of the aforesaid knowledge and conduct is simultaneous with liberation. There is no contradiction here, since simultaneity is intended, just as in the statement, 'Dependent on the eye and forms, eye-consciousness arises' (M. i. 111, etc.). Alternatively, the word 'endowed' should be understood as referring to the present time, like the word 'arisen' in 'arisen phenomena' (Dhs. Tika. 17). And by doing so, the construction of the words is established without a remainder to the statement. And the word 'by which' should be connected in both places: 'by which Dhamma they are endowed with knowledge and conduct, and by which Dhamma they are led out.'

Lokatoti khandhādilokato, vaṭṭatoti attho. Nti taṃ magganibbānaphalapariyattibhedaṃ dhammaṃ. Uttamanti seṭṭhaṃ. Tathā hesa attanā uttaritarassa abhāvena “anuttaro”ti vuccati. Tattha maggassa niyyānahetuādiatthena, nibbānassa nissaraṇavivekādiatthena, phalassa ariyasantabhāvādiatthena ca seṭṭhatā veditabbā. Svāyamattho “yāvatā, bhikkhave, dhammā saṅkhatā, ariyo aṭṭhaṅgiko maggo tesaṃ aggamakkhāyatī”ti (itivu. 90; a. ni. 4.34) ādisuttapadānusārena vibhāvetabbo.

From the world: from the world of aggregates, etc.; the meaning is 'from the round of existence.' That Dhamma, which is divided into path, Nibbāna, fruition, and scripture, is supreme. 'Uttama' means the best. Thus, because there is nothing superior to it, it is called 'anuttara' (unsurpassed). Here, the excellence of the path should be understood in terms of its being the cause of deliverance, etc.; the excellence of Nibbāna in terms of its being escape and seclusion, etc.; and the excellence of the fruition in terms of its being the state of noble peace, etc. This meaning should be clarified in accordance with such sutta passages as: 'Bhikkhus, as far as conditioned things extend, the Noble Eightfold Path is declared the foremost among them' (Itivu. 90; A. Ni. 4.34).

Dhammanti yathānusiṭṭhaṃ paṭipajjamāne apāyato, saṃsārato ca apatamāne katvā dhāretīti dhammo. Sammā, sāmañca sabbadhammānaṃ buddhattā sammāsambuddho, sabbaññutāanāvaraṇañāṇo samantacakkhu bhagavā, tena yathā sammāsambodhisamadhigameneva sabbe buddhaguṇā sampāpuṇīyanti, evaṃ sammadeva āsevanāya bhāvanāya bahulīkiriyāya sammāpaṭipattiyā sammadeva paccavekkhaṇāya sakkaccaṃ dhammadesanāya veneyyasantānesu patiṭṭhāpanena –

Dhamma: it is called 'dhamma' because it upholds those who practice it as instructed, preventing them from falling into the lower realms and saṃsāra. The Perfectly Self-Enlightened One (Sammāsambuddho), because he has awakened perfectly (sammā) and by himself (sāmaṃ) to all things (sabbadhammānaṃ); the Blessed One, the All-Knowing One with unobstructed knowledge, the one with the universal eye. Just as all the qualities of a Buddha are attained through the attainment of perfect self-awakening, so too, through right cultivation, development, and repeated practice, through right conduct, right reflection, earnest teaching of the Dhamma, and establishing it in the mental continuities of those to be trained—

“Ariyaṃ[V 0.0010], vo bhikkhave, sammāsamādhiṃ desessāmi (ma. ni. 3.136; peṭako. 24). Maggānaṭṭhaṅgiko seṭṭho (dha. pa. 273; netti. 170; peṭako. 30). Yāvatā, bhikkhave, dhammā saṅkhatā vā asaṅkhatā vā, virāgo tesaṃ aggamakkhāyati (itivu. 90; a. ni. 4.34). Ekāyano ayaṃ, bhikkhave, maggo sattānaṃ visuddhiyā (dī. ni. 2.373; ma. ni. 1.106; saṃ. ni. 5.367, 384). Dhammaṃ, vo bhikkhave, desessāmi ādikalyāṇa”nti (ma. ni. 3.420; netti. 5) –

“Bhikkhus, I will teach you noble right concentration” (Majjhima Nikāya 3.136; Peṭakopadesa 24). “Of paths, the eightfold is the best” (Dhammapada 273; Nettippakaraṇa 170; Peṭakopadesa 30). “Bhikkhus, as far as there are conditioned or unconditioned phenomena, dispassion is declared the foremost of them” (Itivuttaka 90; Aṅguttara Nikāya 4.34). “Bhikkhus, this is the direct path for the purification of beings” (Dīgha Nikāya 2.373; Majjhima Nikāya 1.106; Saṃyutta Nikāya 5.367, 384). “Bhikkhus, I will teach you the Dhamma that is good in the beginning” (Majjhima Nikāya 3.420; Nettippakaraṇa 5).

Ādivacanehi, abhitthavanena ca pūjito mānito apacitoti sammāsambuddhapūjito, taṃ sammāsambuddhapūjitaṃ dhammaṃ vandeti sambandho.

By these opening words and by praise, it is honored, esteemed, and venerated; thus, it is “honored by the Perfectly Self-Enlightened One” (sammāsambuddhapūjita). The connection is that one pays homage to that Dhamma which is honored by the Perfectly Self-Enlightened One.

Ayaṃ panettha saṅkhepattho – yassa dhammassa adhigamane vijjāsampannā ceva honti caraṇasampannā ca, sabbavaṭṭadukkhato ca niyyanti, tameva ariyānaṃ sakalaguṇasamaṅgibhāvanimittaṃ, anavasesadukkhanissaraṇahetubhūtañca uttamaṃ [M 0.0011] pavaraṃ saddhiṃ pariyattidhammena navavidhaṃ lokuttaradhammaṃ bhagavatāpi sammāpaṭipattiādividhinā pūjitaṃ namāmi, abhitthavāmi vāti.

Here, the concise meaning is this: I pay homage to and praise that supreme, excellent ninefold supramundane Dhamma, together with the scriptural Dhamma, through the attainment of which beings become endowed with knowledge and conduct and are led out from all the suffering of the round; which is the basis for the full endowment with all the qualities of the noble ones and is the cause for the complete escape from suffering; and which is honored by the Blessed One himself through the method of right practice and other means.

Ettha ca “yena lokato niyyanti, vijjācaraṇasampannā ca hontī”ti padadvayena yathākkamaṃ dhammassa bhāvetabbabhāvo, sacchikātabbabhāvo ca vutto. Tesu paṭhamena vijjāsampattiyā dhammaṃ thometi, dutiyena vimuttisampattiyā. Tathā paṭhamena jhānasampadāya, dutiyena vimokkhasampadāya. Paṭhamena vā samādhisampadāya, dutiyena samāpattisampadāya. Paṭhamena vā khayañāṇabhāvena, dutiyena anuppādañāṇabhāvena. Atha vā purimena vijjūpamatāya, dutiyena vajirūpamatāya. Purimena vā virāgasampattiyā, dutiyena nirodhasampattiyā. Tathā paṭhamena niyyānabhāvena, dutiyena nissaraṇabhāvena. Paṭhamena vā hetubhāvena, dutiyena asaṅkhatabhāvena. Paṭhamena vā dassanabhāvena, dutiyena vivekabhāvena. Paṭhamena vā adhipatibhāvena, dutiyena amatabhāvena dhammaṃ thometi. Atha vā paṭhamena niyyānikabhāvadassanato svākkhātatāya dhammaṃ thometi, dutiyena sacchikātabbabhāvato sandiṭṭhikatāya. Tathā purimena akālikatāya, pacchimena ehipassikatāya. Purimena vā opaneyyikatāya, pacchimena paccattaṃ veditabbatāya dhammaṃ thometi.

Here, the two phrases, “by which they are led out from the world” and “endowed with knowledge and conduct,” indicate, in order, the nature of the Dhamma as something to be developed and the nature of it as something to be realized. By the first, the Dhamma is praised for the accomplishment of knowledge; by the second, for the accomplishment of liberation. Similarly, by the first, for the accomplishment of jhāna; by the second, for the accomplishment of deliverance. Or by the first, for the accomplishment of concentration; by the second, for the accomplishment of attainment. Or by the first, for the nature of knowledge of destruction; by the second, for the nature of knowledge of non-arising. Alternatively, by the former, for its likeness to lightning; by the latter, for its likeness to a diamond. Or by the former, for the accomplishment of dispassion; by the latter, for the accomplishment of cessation. Similarly, by the first, for the nature of leading out; by the second, for the nature of escape. Or by the first, for the nature of a cause; by the second, for the unconditioned nature. Or by the first, for the nature of seeing; by the second, for the nature of seclusion. Or by the first, for the nature of mastery; by the second, for the deathless nature—the Dhamma is praised. Alternatively, by the first, the Dhamma is praised for being well-proclaimed due to its quality of leading out; by the second, for being directly visible due to its quality of being realizable. Similarly, by the former, for its timelessness; by the latter, for its quality of inviting one to come and see. Or by the former, for its conduciveness; by the latter, for its quality of being personally experienced—the Dhamma is praised.

“Uttama”nti ca etena aññassa visiṭṭhassa abhāvadīpanena paripuṇṇatāya dhammaṃ thometi[V 0.0011], “sammāsambuddhapūjita”nti etena parisuddhatāya. Sabbadosāpagamena hissa pūjanīyatā. Parisuddhatāya cassa pahānasampadā, paripuṇṇatāya pabhavasampadā. Pahānasampattiyā ca bhāvanāpāripūrī anavasesadosasamugghāṭanato, pabhavasampattiyā sacchikiriyanibbatti tatuttari karaṇīyābhāvato. Anaññasādhāraṇatāya hi uttamoti. Tathā bhāvetabbabhāvenassa saha pubbabhāgasīlādīhi sekkhā sīlasamādhipaññākkhandhā, sacchikātabbabhāvena saha asaṅkhatāya dhātuyā asekkhā sīlasamādhipaññākkhandhā dassitā hontīti.

“Uttama” (Supreme) signifies the absence of any other superior, thus extolling the Dhamma as complete. “Sammāsambuddhapūjita” (venerated by the Perfectly Self-Enlightened One) indicates its purity. Its venerability is because of the removal of all faults. Its purity is the accomplishment of abandonment, and its completeness is the accomplishment of arising. Through the accomplishment of abandonment, the complete perfection of development is due to the eradication of all defilements without remainder; through the accomplishment of arising, the realization of Nibbāna occurs because there is nothing further to be done beyond that. Indeed, “uttama” is because it is unique, not shared with another. Thus, the aggregates of virtue, concentration, and wisdom of a trainee, along with their preliminary stages such as virtue, are shown as what should be developed; and the aggregates of virtue, concentration, and wisdom of one beyond training, along with the unconditioned element, are shown as what should be realized.

Evaṃ saṅkhepena sabbasaddhammaguṇehi saddhammaṃ thometvā idāni ariyasaṅghaṃ thometuṃ “sīlādiguṇasampanno”tiādi vuttaṃ. Tattha sīlādiguṇasampannoti sīlasamādhipaññāvimuttiyādiguṇehi sampanno samannāgato, sampannasīlādiguṇo vā. Ariyānañhi taṃtaṃmaggavajjhakilesappahānena hatapaṭipakkhā suvisuddhā sīlādayo “sampannā”ti vattabbataṃ arahanti[M 0.0012], na puthūjjanānaṃ, yato “suppaṭipanno”tiādinā (ma. ni. 1.74; a. ni. 6.10; udā. 18) ariyasaṅgho thomīyati. Atha vā sīlādiguṇasampannoti paripuṇṇasīlādiguṇo. Ariyapuggalānañhi ariyasaccappaṭivedhena saheva yathārahaṃ sekkhāsekkhā sīlādidhammakkhandhā pāripūriṃ gacchantīti. Ṭhito maggaphalesūti maggesu, phalesu ca ṭhito, maggaṭṭho, phalaṭṭho cāti attho. Yoti aniyamato ariyasaṅghaṃ niddisati, tassa “ta”nti iminā niyamaṃ veditabbaṃ.

Having briefly extolled the Dhamma with all the virtues of the true Dhamma, now, to extol the noble Sangha, the phrase “endowed with the qualities of virtue, etc.” is stated. Herein, “endowed with the qualities of virtue, etc.” means one who is endowed or possessed with the qualities of virtue, concentration, wisdom, liberation, and so on, or one whose qualities of virtue, etc., are perfected. For the noble ones, whose virtue and other qualities are supremely purified by abandoning the defilements overcome by the respective paths, are worthy of being called “perfected,” not ordinary people, because the noble Sangha is praised with terms like “practicing well” (MN 1.74; AN 6.10; Ud. 18). Alternatively, “endowed with the qualities of virtue, etc.” means one whose virtues and other qualities are fully perfected. For the aggregates of virtue and other qualities of noble individuals, whether trainees or perfected ones, attain perfection appropriately with the realization of the noble truths. “Established in the paths and fruits” means established in the paths and in the fruits, that is, one who is established in the path and one established in the fruit. The word “yo” (who) indicates the noble Sangha without restriction, and “ta” (that) should be understood as specifying it.

Nanu ca ariyasaṅghe na sabbe ariyapuggalā maggaṭṭhā, nāpi sabbe phalaṭṭhāti? Saccametaṃ, avayavadhammena pana samudāyaṃ niddisanto evamāha yathā “samaṃ cuṇṇa”nti. Yathā hi yogacuṇṇassa avayavesu labbhamāno samabhāvo samudāye apadisīyati “samaṃ cuṇṇa”nti, evaṃ ariyasaṅghassa avayavabhūtesu ariyapuggalesu labbhamāno maggaṭṭhaphalaṭṭhabhāvo samudāyabhūte ariyasaṅghe ṭhito “maggaphalesū”ti apadiṭṭhoti veditabbaṃ.

But is it not true that not all noble individuals in the Noble Saṅgha are established in the path, nor are all established in the fruition? This is indeed true. However, when indicating the whole by its parts, it is said thus, as in “equal powder.” Just as the equal proportion found in the components of medicinal powder is attributed to the whole as “equal powder,” so too the state of being established in the path or fruition, found in the noble individuals who are the components of the Noble Saṅgha, is designated in the collective Noble Saṅgha as “in the paths and fruits.” This should be understood.

Ārakattā kilesehi, anaye na iriyanato, aye ca iriyanato, sadevakena ca lokena “saraṇa”nti araṇīyato ariyo, diṭṭhisīlasāmaññena saṃhatattā saṅgho, ariyo ca so saṅgho cāti ariyasaṅgho, taṃ ariyasaṅghaṃ. Pujjabhavaphalanibbattanato attano santānaṃ punātīti vā puññaṃ, khittaṃ vuttaṃ bījaṃ viruhanaṭṭhānatāya tāyati rakkhatīti khettaṃ kedārādi, khettaṃ viyāti khettaṃ, sattānaṃ puññassa mahapphalabhāvakaraṇena viruhanaṭṭhānatāya khettanti puññakkhettaṃ. Anuttaraṃ vandeti sambandho.

Because he is remote from defilements, because he does not move in wrong ways but moves in right ways, and because he is worthy of being resorted to as a refuge by the world with its gods, he is called ‘noble’ (ariya). The Sangha is so called because it is unified by commonality of view and virtue; and that Sangha is noble, thus it is the Noble Sangha. The connection is that one pays homage to that unsurpassed Noble Sangha, which is a field of merit. (Merit (puñña) is so called because it purifies one’s own continuum by producing the fruit of a state worthy of veneration. A field (khetta) is so called because it protects and shelters the sown seeds, being a place for their growth, like a cultivated field; and because it is a place for the growth of beings’ merit, making it greatly fruitful, it is called a ‘field of merit’ (puññakkhetta).)

Ettha [V 0.0012] ca “sīlādiguṇasampanno”ti etena ariyasaṅghassa bhagavato anujātaputtataṃ dasseti, tenassa pabhavasampadā dīpitā hoti. “Ṭhito maggaphalesū”ti etena pahānasampadaṃ, ñāṇasampadañca dasseti kilesānaṃ samucchedappaṭippassaddhippahānadīpanato, maggaphalañāṇādhigamadīpanato ca. “Ariyasaṅgha”nti etena pabhavasampadaṃ sabbasaṅghānaṃ aggabhāvadīpanato, sadevakena ca lokena araṇīyabhāvadīpanato. “Puññakkhettaṃ anuttara”nti etena lokassa bahūpakārataṃ dasseti aggadakkhiṇeyyabhāvadīpanato.

Here, “endowed with virtues such as morality” indicates that the noble Sangha are the true sons of the Blessed One, thus revealing their excellence of origin. “Established in the paths and fruits” reveals their excellence in abandonment and wisdom, by showing the eradication and tranquillization-abandonment of the defilements, as well as showing the attainment of path and fruit knowledge. “Noble Sangha” reveals their excellence of origin by demonstrating their supreme status among all assemblies and their state of being worthy of being resorted to as a refuge by the world, including the devas. “An unsurpassed field of merit” reveals their great benefit to the world by showing their status as the foremost worthy recipients of offerings.

Tathā [M 0.0013] “sīlādiguṇasampanno”ti idaṃ ariyasaṅghassa sammāujuñāyasāmīcippaṭipannabhāvadīpanaṃ. “Ṭhito maggaphalesū”ti idaṃ satipi santānavibhāgena anekabhāve catupurisayugaaṭṭhapurisapuggalabhāvadīpanaṃ. “Ariyasaṅgha”nti idaṃ āhuneyyādibhāvadīpanaṃ. “Puññakkhettaṃ anuttara”nti idaṃ lokassa hitasukhāya paṭipannatādīpanaṃ. Tathā “ṭhito maggaphalesū”ti idaṃ ariyasaṅghassa lokuttarasaraṇagamanasabbhāvadīpanaṃ, tenassa bhagavato orasaputtabhāvo dassito hoti. “Sīlādiguṇasampanno”ti iminā panassa vihatavidhastakilesā anavasesā sekkhāsekkhā sīlādidhammakkhandhā dassitā. “Ariyasaṅghaṃ puññakkhettaṃ anuttara”nti iminā tesaṃ tesaññeva yathāvuttaguṇavisesānaṃ suparisuddhataṃ dīpeti. Tenassa mahānubhāvataṃ, anuttaradakkhiṇeyyabhāvaṃ, vandanārahabhāvaṃ, attano ca vandanākiriyāya khettaṅgatabhāvaṃ dīpeti. Saraṇagamanañca sāvakānaṃ sabbaguṇānaṃ ādi, sapubbabhāgappaṭipadā sekkhā sīlakkhandhādayo majjhe, asekkhā sīlakkhandhādayo pariyosānanti ādimajjhapariyosānakalyāṇā sabbe ariyasaṅghaguṇā imāya gāthāya pakāsitāti veditabbaṃ.

Thus, 'endowed with virtues such as morality' illuminates the Noble Sangha's proper, upright, methodical, and harmonious practice. 'Established in the paths and fruits' reveals that although they are diverse as individuals, they are the four pairs and eight types of persons. 'Noble Sangha' signifies their worthiness of offerings and other honors. 'An unsurpassed field of merit' demonstrates their practice for the welfare and happiness of the world. Likewise, 'established in the paths and fruits' reveals the Noble Sangha's nature as the supramundane refuge, thereby showing them as the Blessed One’s true spiritual offspring. Through 'endowed with virtues such as morality,' their defilements, eradicated and destroyed without remainder, and their aggregates of virtue and so forth, both of learners and perfected ones, are shown. Through 'Noble Sangha, an unsurpassed field of merit,' the supreme purity of those very distinguished qualities is illuminated. Thus, their great power, unsurpassed worthiness of offerings, worthiness of veneration, and their role as a field for performing acts of veneration are revealed. Taking refuge is the beginning of all virtues for disciples; the preliminary practice, the aggregates of virtue and so forth of the learners, are the middle; and the aggregates of virtue and so forth of the perfected ones are the culmination. All the Noble Sangha’s virtues, beautiful in the beginning, middle, and end, are thus made clear in this verse.

Evaṃ gāthāttayena saṅkhepato sakalaguṇasaṃkittanamukhena ratanattayassa paṇāmaṃ katvā idāni taṃ nipaccakāraṃ yathādhippete payojane pariṇāmento “vandanājanita”nti gāthamāha. Tattha vandanājanitanti vandanākārena nibbattitaṃ, ratanattayaguṇābhitthavanavasena, nipaccakāravasena vā uppāditanti attho. Itīti evaṃ “mahākāruṇika”ntiādippakārena. Ratijananaṭṭhena ratanaṃ, buddhadhammasaṅghā, cittīkatādibhāvo vā ratanaṭṭho. Vuttañhetaṃ –

Having thus paid homage to the Three Jewels in brief by means of three verses, through the proclamation of all their virtues, now directing that act of reverence toward its intended purpose, he speaks the verse beginning with 'vandanājanita.' Therein, 'vandanājanita' means 'generated by homage,' that is, produced by way of praising the virtues of the Three Jewels or by way of the act of reverence. 'Thus' means in this way, in the manner beginning with 'the greatly compassionate one,' and so forth. A 'ratana' (jewel) is so called because it generates delight, namely the Buddha, the Dhamma, and the Sangha; or the meaning of 'ratana' is the state of being esteemed, and so forth. For it has been said—

“Cittīkataṃ mahagghañca, atulaṃ dullabhadassanaṃ;

Anomasattaparibhogaṃ, ratanaṃ tena vuccatī”ti. (khu. pā. aṭṭha. 6.3; dī. ni. aṭṭha. 2.33; saṃ. ni. 5.223; su. ni. aṭṭha. 1.226; mahāni. aṭṭha. 50; dī. ni. ṭī. 1.ganthārambhakathā; ma. ni. ṭī. 1.4; saṃ. ni. ṭī. 1.1.4; a. ni. ṭī. 1.1.4; sārattha. ṭī. 1.ganthārambhakathāvaṇṇanā);

'Highly esteemed, greatly valuable, incomparable, rarely seen, enjoyed by beings of superior quality; therefore it is called a jewel.'

Cittīkatabhāvādayo [V 0.0013] ca anaññasādhāraṇā buddhādīsu eva labbhanti, ratanānaṃ tayaṃ ratanattayaṃ, tasmiṃ ratanattaye. Hatantarāyoti vidhastaupaddavo hutvāti sambandho, etena attano pasādasampattiyā, ratanattayassa ca khettabhāvasampattiyā tassa puññassa atthasaṃvaṇṇanāya upaghātakaupaddavānaṃ [M 0.0014] vihanane samatthataṃ dasseti. Sabbatthāti sabbasmiṃ anto ceva bahi ca, ajjhattikabāhiravatthūsūti attho. Sabbatthāti vā sabbasmiṃ kāle, saṃvaṇṇanāya ādimajjhapariyosānakālesūti vuttaṃ hoti. Hutvāti pubbakālakiriyā, tassa “karissāmatthavaṇṇana”nti etena sambandho. Tassāti yaṃ ratanattaye vandanājanitaṃ puññaṃ, tassa. Tejasāti ānubhāvena balena.

The qualities such as being highly esteemed, which are unshared by others, are found only in the Buddhas and so forth; the triad of jewels is the Three Jewels; in those Three Jewels. 'Having dispelled dangers' means having destroyed obstacles—this is the connection. By this, it shows the ability to eliminate destructive obstacles for the exposition of the meaning of that merit, owing to the perfection of one's own faith and the perfection of the Three Jewels as a field. 'Everywhere' means in all places, both inside and outside, that is, in internal and external things. Or, 'everywhere' means at all times—thus, it means at the beginning, middle, and end times of the exposition. 'Having been' is a prior action; the connection is with 'I will make an exposition of the meaning.' 'Of that' refers to the merit generated by venerating the Three Jewels. 'By its power' means by influence, by strength.

Evaṃ ratanattayavandanāya payojanaṃ dassetvā idāni nettippakaraṇassa gambhīratthattā atthasaṃvaṇṇanāya dukkarabhāvaṃ dassetuṃ “ṭhiti”ntiādimāha. Tattha ṭhitinti ṭhānaṃ anantaradhānaṃ avicchedappavattiṃ. Ākaṅkhamānenāti icchamānena patthayantena, “ahovatāyaṃ saddhammanetti ciraṃ tiṭṭheyyā”ti evaṃ patthayantenāti vuttaṃ hoti. Ciranti dīghakālaṃ, pañcavassasahassaparimāṇaṃ kālanti attho. Saddhammanettiyāti saddhammasaṅkhātāya nettiyā. Saddhammo hi veneyyasantānesu ariyaguṇānaṃ nayanato netti, saddhammassa vā netti saddhammanetti, tassā saddhammanettiyā, svāyamattho aṭṭhakathāyaṃ vicārito eva. Therenāti thiraguṇayuttena. Abhiyācitoti ādaragāravena yācito. Abhimukhaṃ vā yācito, anuttaraṃ katvā yācitoti attho. Uddissa vā yācito, garutaraṃ katvā yācitoti attho, “karotu āyasmā nettippakaraṇassa kañci atthasaṃvaṇṇana”nti evaṃ nettiyā atthasaṃvaṇṇanaṃ pati ajjhesitoti vuttaṃ hoti. Ettha ca saddhammassa ciraṃ ṭhitikāmena ajjhāsayasampannena sāsane thiraguṇayuttena sabrahmacārinā ādaragāravena, abhimukhaṃ vā yācitena me na sakkā tassa abhiyācanaṃ paṭikkhipitunti dasseti “ṭhitiṃ ākaṅkhamānenā”ti gāthāya.

Having shown the purpose of venerating the Three Jewels, now, to illustrate the difficulty of explaining the profound meaning of the Nettippakaraṇa, he begins with 'ṭhiti.' Therein, 'ṭhiti' means stability, non-disappearance, uninterrupted continuity. 'Ākaṅkhamānena' means by one who desires, who longs, thinking, 'May this Guide to the Good Dhamma endure for a long time'—thus it is said of one who so aspires. 'Ciraṃ' means for a long time, a period of five thousand years. 'Saddhammanettiyā' refers to the Netti known as the Saddhamma. For the Saddhamma is a Netti (guide) because it leads noble qualities into the minds of those to be trained. Alternatively, the Netti of the Saddhamma is Saddhammanetti; this very meaning is examined in the commentary. 'Therena' means by an elder endowed with steadfast virtues. 'Abhiyācito' means respectfully requested with reverence. Alternatively, it means requested face-to-face, asked in an unsurpassed manner. Or, it means requested for a purpose, asked with great gravity—'May the venerable one compose an exposition of the meaning of the Nettippakaraṇa'—thus he was entreated for an exposition of the Netti's meaning. Here, the verse beginning with 'ṭhitiṃ ākaṅkhamānenā' shows that it was impossible for me to refuse his request, having been asked by a fellow monastic—one who desires the long stability of the Saddhamma, is endowed with good intention, and is steadfast in virtue within the Dispensation—with reverence, or face-to-face.

Padumuttaranāthassāti padumuttarassa sammāsambuddhassa. Passatāti pubbenivāsacakkhunā, samantacakkhunā eva vā hatthatale ṭhapitaāmalakaṃ viya abhinīhāraṃ passantena. Tādināti tādibhāvayuttena[V 0.0014], sabbattha vā nibbikārena, “amhākaṃ bhagavatā”ti vacanaseso. Yassāti āyasmato mahākaccānattherassa. Ṭhapitoti –

'Of the Lord Padumuttara' means of Padumuttara, the Perfectly Enlightened One. 'Seeing' means seeing with the eye of past lives or even with the universal eye, seeing the aspiration like an āmalaka fruit placed on the palm of the hand. 'By such a one' means by one possessing such a quality, or by one who is imperturbable in all things; the remainder of the phrase, 'by our Blessed One,' is understood. 'Of whom' refers to the Venerable Elder Mahākaccāna. 'Established' means—

“Etadaggaṃ, bhikkhave, mama sāvakānaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ saṃkhittena bhāsitassa vitthārena atthaṃ vibhajantānaṃ yadidaṃ mahākaccāno”ti (a. ni. 1.188, 197) –

'Monks, among my bhikkhu disciples, the foremost of those who analyze in detail the meaning of what has been stated in brief is Mahākaccāna.'

Evaṃ [M 0.0015] ṭhapito. Sīlādiguṇavisesehi mahantā sāvakāti mahāsāvakā (theragā. aṭṭha. 2.1288; a. ni. ṭī. 2.3.59), mahākassapādayo, tesu ayamāyasmā aññataroti, mahāsāvako ca so guṇavisesayogato uttamo cāti mahāsāvakuttamo.

Thus he was established. Great disciples (mahāsāvaka) are those who are great by virtue of special qualities such as morality and so forth, like Mahākassapa and others. Among them, this venerable one is one; and being a great disciple and supreme by virtue of his connection with special qualities, he is a supreme great disciple (mahāsāvakuttama).

Jhānādīsu sātisayānaṃ āvajjanādivasībhāvānaṃ, ariyiddhivasena paramassa ca cetovasībhāvassa adhigatattā vasippatto. Atthādīsu savisesabhedagatapaṭisambhidāñāṇattā pabhinnapaṭisambhido. “Paṇḍito, bhikkhave, mahākaccāno, mahāpañño, bhikkhave, mahākaccāno”tiādinā (ma. ni. 1.205) anekesu ṭhānesu bhagavatā pasaṃsitattā sambuddhena pasaṃsito. Tena vuttaṃ “satthu ceva saṃvaṇṇito saṃbhāvito, viññūnañca sabrahmacārina”nti.

Because of having attained the states of mastery, such as mastery of adverting, which are excellent in the jhānas and so forth, and because of having attained supreme mastery of mind by way of noble spiritual power, he is 'one who has attained mastery' (vasippatto). Because of possessing the knowledge of the analytical discriminations which have reached specific distinctions in regard to meaning and so forth, he is 'one with analytical discriminations' (pabhinnapaṭisambhido). Because he was praised by the Blessed One in many places with phrases such as 'Wise, monks, is Mahākaccāna; of great wisdom, monks, is Mahākaccāna,' he is 'praised by the Perfectly Enlightened One' (sambuddhena pasaṃsito). Therefore, it is said, 'Praised and esteemed by the Teacher, and by his wise fellows in the holy life.'

Anumoditāti “sādhu sādhu, kaccāna, sādhu kho, tvaṃ kaccāna, imaṃ dhammasaṃvaṇṇanaṃ abhāsī”ti evaṃ anumoditā. Ekasmiṃ kira samaye ayaṃ mahāthero jambuvanasaṇḍe viharanto attano santikāvacarānaṃ bhikkhūnaṃ imaṃ hāranayapaṭimaṇḍitaṃ pakaraṇaṃ abhāsi. Bhāsitvā ca bhagavato santikaṃ upasaṅkamitvā bhagavantaṃ abhivādetvā ekamantaṃ nisinno yathābhāsitaṃ imaṃ pakaraṇaṃ bhagavato nivedesi. Taṃ sutvā bhagavā “sādhu sādhū”tiādinā anumoditvā “tasmātiha, tvaṃ kaccāna, imaṃ dhammasaṃvaṇṇanaṃ dhammanettitveva dhārehī”ti nāmaggahaṇaṃ akāsīti vadanti. Desanāhārādinandiyāvaṭṭanayādihāranayānusāreneva sabbadhammasaṃvaṇṇanānaṃ gatiyoti āha “sāsanassa sadāyattā, navaṅgassatthavaṇṇanā”ti.

'Approved' means approved thus: 'Good, good, Kaccāna! Well have you spoken, Kaccāna, this exposition of the Dhamma.' It is said that once, while dwelling in the Jambu Grove, this great elder taught this treatise, adorned with its methods of conveyance, to the monks who were his close attendants. After speaking, he approached the Blessed One, paid homage, sat to one side, and reported this treatise to the Blessed One as it had been spoken. Hearing it, the Blessed One expressed approval with 'Good, good,' and so forth, and then said, 'Therefore, Kaccāna, you should bear in mind this exposition of the Dhamma as the very Guide to the Dhamma'—thus, they say, he bestowed its name. He said that the course of all expositions of the Dhamma is precisely in accordance with the methods of conveyance, such as the method of teaching and the method of revolving joy. Hence, it is said: 'The exposition of the meaning of the nine-limbed teaching is always dependent on the Dispensation.'

Gambhīrañāṇehīhi gambhīrehi ñāṇehi, na saddhāmattakena, gambhīrañāṇehi vā mahāpaññehi ariyehi. Pakaraṇassa gambhīratthataṃ, attano ca ñāṇassa nātivisayataṃ viditvā saṃvaṇṇanārambhe saṃsīdantampi maṃ sāsanaguṇādiupanissayasampadā ussāhesīti imamatthaṃ dasseti “kiñcāpī”tiādinā.

'By those with profound knowledge' means by profound knowledges, not merely by faith; or by noble ones of great wisdom endowed with profound knowledge. By the phrase beginning with 'kiñcāpi,' he shows this meaning: 'Although I was faltering at the start of the exposition, having recognized the profound meaning of the treatise and the limited scope of my own knowledge, the perfection of supporting conditions, such as the virtues of the Dispensation, encouraged me.'

“Pañcapi [V 0.0015] nikāye ogāhetvā”ti iminā nettiyā pañcapi mahānikāye anupavisitvā avaṭṭhānaṃ, tesaṃ saṃvaṇṇanābhāvañca dīpeti. Tattha [M 0.0016] “katamo assādo ca ādīnavo cā”tiādipeṭakopadesapāḷiṃ (peṭako. 23) ānetvā idha desanāhārādīnaṃ padatthavinicchayo peṭakena saṃsandanaṃ nāma. “yathābala”nti iminā sabbathā sabbabhāgenāpi nettiyā saṃvaṇṇanā mayā na sukarā kātuṃ, attano pana ñāṇabalānurūpaṃ karissāmīti niratimānataṃ dīpeti.

By 'having plunged into the five Nikāyas,' he indicates the Netti's establishment through its entry into the five great Nikāyas, and its nature as an exposition of them. Herein, bringing the Pāli text from the Peṭakopadesa, such as 'What is the gratification and what is the danger?', the determination of the meaning of the terms for the modes of conveyance, etc., is called 'comparison with the Peṭaka.' By 'according to my ability,' he shows his humility, indicating that 'an exposition of the Netti that is complete in every respect is not easy for me to make, but I will do it in accordance with the strength of my own knowledge.'

Suvisuddhanti suṭṭhu visuddhaṃ, nikāyantaraladdhidosehi antarantarā anuppavesitehi asammissanti adhippāyo. Asaṃkiṇṇanti sanikāyepi padatthantaraparikappanādinā asaṃkiṇṇaṃ tādisasaṅkararahitaṃ anākulaṃ suparicchinnaṃ. Vividhehi ākārehi nicchinotīti vinicchayo. Atthānaṃ vinicchayo atthavinicchayo. Gaṇṭhiṭṭhānabhūtesu atthesu khilamaddanākārena pavattā vimaticchedakathā, nipuṇo sukhumo saṇho atthavinicchayo etassāti nipuṇatthavinicchayo. Atha vā atthe vinicchinotīti atthavinicchayo, yathāvuttaatthavisayañāṇaṃ, nipuṇo cheko atthavinicchayo etassāti nipuṇatthavinicchayo, taṃ nipuṇatthavinicchayaṃ. Samayanti siddhantaṃ. Idaṃ vuttaṃ hoti – mahāvihāravāsīnaṃ siddhanto vuttanayena suparisuddho, anākulo, saṇhasukhumavinicchayo ca, siddhantaṃ taṃ avilomento anukūlato tattha siddhaṃyeva dhammanettiṃ pakāsayanto nettippakaraṇassa atthasaṃvaṇṇanaṃ karissāmīti.

By 'thoroughly purified' (suvisuddhaṃ) is meant well purified, unmixed with the faults of the doctrines of other schools that have been introduced here and there—this is the intention. By 'unmixed' (asaṃkiṇṇaṃ) is meant unmixed even within its own school by such things as the conceptualization of other meanings of words, free from such mixture, unconfused, well-defined. Because it determines in various ways, it is a 'determination' (vinicchayo). The determination of meanings is 'atthavinicchayo.' A discourse that cuts off doubt, proceeding in the manner of crushing a hard spot in meanings that are like knots, is a subtle, refined, and delicate determination of meaning; hence, 'subtle determination of meaning' (nipuṇatthavinicchayo). Alternatively, `atthavinicchayo` is that which determines meanings, that is, knowledge concerning the sphere of meaning as stated; one who has a skilled and expert determination of meaning is `nipuṇatthavinicchayo`. That subtle determination of meaning. By `samaya` is meant tenet (`siddhanta`). This is what is said: The tenet of the residents of the Mahāvihāra is, in the manner stated, thoroughly purified, unconfused, and possesses a subtle and refined determination of meaning. Without opposing that tenet, in accordance with it, revealing the Dhamma-guidance established therein, I will make the meaning-commentary of the Nettippakaraṇa.

Pamādalekhanti aparabhāge potthakāruḷhakāle pamajjitvā likhanavasena pavattappamādapāṭhaṃ. Vajjetvāti apanetvā. Pāḷiṃ sammā niyojayanti taṃ taṃ nettipāḷiṃ tattha tattha udāharaṇabhāvena ānītasutte sammadeva niyojento, atthasaṃvaṇṇanāya vā taṃ taṃ udāharaṇasuttasaṅkhātaṃ pāḷiṃ tasmiṃ tasmiṃ lakkhaṇabhūte nettiganthe sammadeva niyojento. Upadesanti nettiupanisaṃ nettihadayaṃ. Yvāyaṃ sapaṭṭhānavibhāgassa tettiṃsavidhassa nettipadatthassa saha nimittavibhāgena asaṅkarato vavatthito visayo, taṃ. Vibhāvento pakāsento. Tassā nettiyā karissāmi atthavaṇṇananti sambandho.

By 'careless writing' (pamādalekhaṃ) is meant an erroneous reading that has arisen through careless writing at the time of being entered into books in a later period. By 'having avoided' (vajjetvā) is meant having removed. By 'properly applying the text' (pāḷiṃ sammā niyojayanti) is meant properly applying that particular Netti text to the Sutta brought in as an example here and there, or properly applying that text, which is reckoned as an example-Sutta, to that particular Netti treatise which serves as the definition. By 'instruction' (upadesaṃ) is meant the essence of the Netti, the heart of the Netti. That is, that sphere which is established without confusion: the thirty-threefold meaning of the Netti terms, with the division of its foundation and together with the division of its sign. By 'illuminating' (vibhāvento) is meant making clear. The connection is: 'I will make a meaning-commentary for that Netti.'

Ettha ca “abhiyācito”ti iminā atthasaṃvaṇṇanāya nimittaṃ dasseti, “ṭhitiṃ ākaṅkhamānena ciraṃ saddhammanettiyā”ti iminā payojanaṃ, “karissāmatthavaṇṇana”nti [M 0.0017] iminā piṇḍatthaṃ. Saṃvaṇṇiyamānā hi pakaraṇatthā saṃvaṇṇanāya piṇḍattho. “Tamupanissāyā”tiādinā karaṇappakāraṃ.

Here, by 'having been requested' (abhiyācito) he shows the reason for the meaning-commentary; by 'desiring the long continuance of the Netti of the True Dhamma' (ṭhitiṃ ākaṅkhamānena ciraṃ saddhammanettiyā) he shows the purpose; and by 'I will make a meaning-commentary' (karissāmatthavaṇṇanaṃ) he shows the summarized meaning. For the summarized meaning of the exposition is the meanings of the treatise that are being expounded. By 'relying on that' (tamupanissāya) and so on, he explains the method of doing it.

Idāni [V 0.0016] saṃvaṇṇanāya savane niyojento “iti attha”nti osānagāthamāha. Tattha “sakkacca”nti padaṃ ubhayattha yojetabbaṃ “sakkaccaṃ vibhajantassa, sakkaccaṃ nisāmayathā”ti.

Now, engaging the audience in listening to the exposition, he speaks the concluding verse, 'Thus the meaning' (iti atthaṃ). Here, the word 'carefully' (sakkaccaṃ) should be connected to both places: 'for one who carefully analyzes' (sakkaccaṃ vibhajantassa) and 'listen carefully' (sakkaccaṃ nisāmayathā).

Ganthārambhakathāvaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The exposition of the discourse on the commencement of the treatise is concluded.

Nidānakathāvaṇṇanā

The Exposition of the Discourse on the Introduction

Vacanatthajānanena viditappakaraṇatthasāmaññatthassa pakaraṇakathā vuccamānā sobheyyāti nettipadatthaparijānanameva ādimhi yuttarūpanti tadatthaṃ pucchati “tattha kenaṭṭhena nettī”ti. Tattha tatthāti “tassā nettiyā karissāmatthavaṇṇana”nti yadidaṃ vuttaṃ, tasmiṃ; yassā karissāmatthavaṇṇananti paṭiññātaṃ, sā netti kenaṭṭhena nettīti attho. Tatthāti vā “nettippakaraṇassā”ti etasmiṃ vacane yā netti vuttā, sā kenaṭṭhena nettīti attho. “Nayanaṭṭhenā”ti idaṃ kattukaraṇādhikaraṇasādhanānaṃ sādhāraṇavacananti “ariyadhammaṃ nayatī”ti kattusādhanavasena tāva nettisaddassa atthaṃ vatvā idāni karaṇādhikaraṇasādhanavasena vattuṃ “nayanti tāyā”tiādi vuttaṃ.

Since the discourse on the treatise would be excellent when spoken, once the general meaning of the treatise's content is known through understanding the meaning of its words, it is fitting at the outset to fully comprehend the meaning of the Netti terms. Therefore, he asks concerning that: 'In what sense is it Netti?' (tattha kenaṭṭhena nettī). Here, 'here' (tattha) means in that which was stated: 'I will make a meaning-commentary for that Netti'; the meaning is: 'In what sense is that Netti, for which a meaning-commentary has been declared, called Netti?' Or, 'here' (tattha) means in this statement, 'of the Nettippakaraṇa'; the meaning is: 'In what sense is the Netti mentioned called Netti?' 'In the sense of guiding' (nayanaṭṭhena): this is a common expression for agentive, instrumental, and locative functions. First, having stated the meaning of the word 'Netti' in the agentive sense, 'it guides the noble Dhamma,' now, to state it in the instrumental and locative senses, 'by it one guides' and so on, is said.

Tathā hi vuttanti nettiupadesādhīnattā eva suttāvabodhassa vuttaṃ. Peṭake “tasmā nibbāyitukāmena sutamayena atthā pariyesitabbā, tattha pariyesanāya ayaṃ anupubbī bhavati soḷasa hārā pañca nayā aṭṭhārasa mūlapadānī”tiādi (peṭako. 3). Hāranayavicāraṇā vinimutto atthasaṃvaṇṇanāviseso natthīti āha “suttassa atthasaṃvaṇṇanā nettiupadesāyattā”ti. Svāyamattho parato pakiṇṇakakathāyaṃ āvi bhavissati. Evaṃ mahāvisayā cāyaṃ netti kuto pabhavāti āha “suttappabhavā”ti, etena nettiyā pamāṇabhūtataṃ dasseti. Idañca suttassa nettisannissayatāparidīpanaparaṃ, na therappabhavatāpaṭikkhepaparaṃ. Thero hi pañca mahānikāye ogāhetvā taṃsannissayeneva [M 0.0018] tesaṃ saṃvaṇṇanābhūtaṃ imaṃ pakaraṇaṃ abhāsi, tasmā ayameva saṃvaṇṇanādhammo, yadidaṃ saṃvaṇṇetabbadhammasannissayatā.

By 'for thus it is said' (tathā hi vuttaṃ) is meant that the understanding of the Suttas is dependent on the instruction of the Netti.
