(Objection:) Of the word ‘iddhi,’ the first meaning is that of agent, the second is said to be that of instrument. Of the word ‘pāda,’ there is only the one meaning of instrument. And the ‘iddhi’ to be achieved is spoken of, but they are not achieved by themselves. The ‘iddhi’ to be achieved is not co-referential with the ‘pāda’ which is the instrument of achievement, so how can it be said, ‘in the first sense, iddhi itself is the pāda’? Similarly, since the iddhi called ‘wisdom’ is to be accomplished by the instrument which is the act of making iddhi, and is to be achieved by the instrument which is the act of achieving, the co-referentiality of the two instruments is not possible, so how can it be said, ‘in the second sense, the pāda of iddhi’? (Reply:) It is possible, because the pāda is a component of what is being achieved and is the means and instrument for achieving it. Or else, it is by this construction: ‘in the first sense, it is the pāda of iddhi; in the second sense, iddhi itself is the pāda; thus iddhipāda.’ How so? Here, by the principle of proximity, the immediately following meaning is intended as the first, and the one preceding that as the second.

“Chandaṃ ce bhikkhu adhipatiṃ karitvā labhati samādhi”ntiādi (vibha. 432) vacanato chandasamādhippadhānasaṅkhārasamannāgataṃ iddhipādaṃ bhāvetīti etthāpi chandādhipati samādhi chandasamādhīti adhipatisaddalopaṃ katvā samāso vuttoti viññāyati. Adhipatisaddatthadassanavaseneva pana “chandahetuko, chandādhiko vā samādhī”ti sammohavinodaniyaṃ (vibha. aṭṭha. 431) vuttaṃ, tasmā idhāpi chandādhipati samādhi chandasamādhīti veditabbo. Taṃ pana chandaṃ vuttanayena saddhāsīsena dassento “saddhādhipateyyā cittekaggatā”ti vuttaṃ. “Idaṃ padhāna”nti vā vīriyaṃ vuttaṃ. Vīriyasaddāpekkhāsahitaṃ ekavacanena vatvā catubbidhassapi vīriyassa adhippetattā nibbattetabbadhammavibhāgena ca “ime saṅkhārā”ti vuttaṃ. Tena padhānabhūtā saṅkhārāti evaṃ samāso veditabbo. Saṅkhatasaṅkhārādinivattanatthañcettha padhānaggahaṇaṃ. Atha vā taṃ taṃ visesaṃ saṅkharotīti saṅkhāro, sabbampi vīriyaṃ. Tattha catukiccasādhakato aññassa nivattanatthaṃ padhānaggahaṇanti padhānabhūtā seṭṭhabhūtāti attho.

From the statement, ‘If a bhikkhu, having made desire the dominant factor, obtains concentration,’ etc. (Vibh 432), it is understood that one develops the basis of psychic power endowed with concentration-of-desire and striving-formations. Here too, it is understood that the compound ‘concentration-of-desire’ (chandasamādhi) is stated by eliding the word ‘dominant’ from ‘concentration that has desire as its dominant factor.’ And because of seeing the meaning of the word ‘dominant,’ it is said in the Sammohavinodanī (Vibha-a 431): ‘concentration that has desire as its cause, or that has desire as pre-eminent.’ Therefore, here too, ‘concentration-of-desire’ should be understood as ‘concentration that has desire as its dominant factor.’ Showing that desire with faith as its head in the way stated, it is said: ‘unification of mind with faith as its dominant factor.’ Or ‘this is striving’ refers to energy. Having spoken in the singular with reference to the word 'energy', and because the fourfold energy is intended and because of the division of the phenomena to be produced, it is said: ‘these formations.’ Thus the compound should be understood as ‘formations that are principal.’ And here the taking up of ‘principal’ is for the purpose of excluding conditioned formations, etc. Or else, ‘it forms this or that distinction,’ thus it is a ‘formation’; this applies to all energy. Therein, the taking up of ‘principal’ is for the purpose of excluding what is other than the accomplisher of the four tasks. Thus the meaning is ‘principal,’ ‘foremost.’

Vīriyiddhipādaniddese “vīriyasamādhippadhānasaṅkhārasamannāgata”nti (vibha. 435) dvikkhattuṃ vīriyaṃ āgataṃ. Tattha purimaṃ samādhivisesanaṃ “vīriyādhipati samādhi vīriyasamādhī”ti, dutiyaṃ samannāgamaṅgadassanatthaṃ. Dve eva hi sabbattha samannāgamaṅgāni samādhi, padhānasaṅkhāro ca. Chandādayo [V 0.0050] samādhivisesanāni. Padhānasaṅkhāro pana padhānavacaneneva visesito, na chandādīhīti na idha vīriyādhipatitā padhānasaṅkhārassa vuttā hoti. Vīriyañca samādhiṃ visesetvā ṭhitameva samannāgamaṅgavasena [M 0.0053] padhānasaṅkhāravacanena vuttanti nāpi dvīhi vīriyehi samannāgamo vutto hoti. Yasmā pana chandādīhi visiṭṭho samādhi tathā visiṭṭheneva tena sampayutto padhānasaṅkhāro, sesadhammā ca, tasmā samādhivisesanānaṃ vasena “cattāro iddhipādā”ti vuttā. Visesanabhāvo ca chandādīnaṃ taṃtaṃapassayanavasena hotīti chandasamādhi…pe… iddhipādanti ettha nissayatthepi pādasaddena upāyatthena chandādīnaṃ iddhipādatā vuttā hoti. Tathā hi abhidhamme uttaracūḷabhājanīye “cattāro iddhipādā chandiddhipādo”tiādinā (vibha. 457) chandādīnameva iddhipādatā vuttā. Pañhāpucchake ca “cattāro iddhipādā – idha bhikkhu chandasamādhī”ti (vibha. 462) ārabhitvāpi puna chandādīnaṃyeva kusalādibhāvo vibhatto. Upāyiddhipādadassanatthameva hi nissayiddhipādadassanaṃ kataṃ. Aññathā catubbidhatāva na hotīti. Ayamettha pāḷivasena atthavinicchayo veditabbo.

In the exposition of the basis of psychic power of energy, in the phrase ‘endowed with energy-concentration and striving-formations’ (Vibh 435), energy appears twice. Therein, the first is a qualifier of concentration—‘concentration that has energy as its dominant factor is energy-concentration’—while the second is for the purpose of showing a factor of endowment. For everywhere there are just two factors of endowment: concentration and striving-formation. Desire and the others are qualifiers of concentration. But the striving-formation is qualified only by the word ‘striving,’ not by desire and the others. Thus the dominance of energy is not stated here of the striving-formation. And energy, having qualified concentration, just stands so; it is also stated by the term ‘striving-formation’ by way of a factor of endowment. Thus endowment with two energies is not stated. But since concentration is qualified by desire, etc., and the striving-formation and the remaining states are associated with that concentration thus qualified, therefore the ‘four bases of psychic power’ are spoken of by way of the qualifiers of concentration. And the status of desire, etc., as qualifiers is due to their being the respective supports. Thus in ‘concentration-of-desire…pe… is a basis of psychic power,’ the status of desire, etc., as bases of psychic power is stated by the word ‘pāda’ in the sense of support, and also in the sense of means. For in the Abhidhamma, in the Uttara-Cūḷabhājanīya, the status of desire, etc., themselves as bases of psychic power is stated thus: ‘The four bases of psychic power are: the basis of psychic power of desire,’ etc. (Vibh 457). And in the Question Section, though beginning, ‘The four bases of psychic power: here a bhikkhu develops concentration-of-desire’ (Vibh 462), the wholesome and other qualities of desire, etc., themselves are then analyzed. For the showing of the bases of psychic power as support is done for the very purpose of showing them as means. Otherwise, there would be no fourfold division. This is how the determination of the meaning according to the Pāli should be understood here.

Tadaṅgasamucchedanissaraṇavivekanissitattaṃ vatvā paṭippassaddhivivekanissitassa avacanaṃ “chandasamādhi…pe… iddhipādaṃ bhāvetī”ti (vibha. 432) bhāvetabbānaṃ iddhipādānaṃ vuttattā. Bhāvitiddhipādassa hi sacchikātabbā phalapariyāpannā iddhipādāti.

Having stated its dependence on seclusion by substitution of opposites, by cutting off, and by escape, the non-statement of its dependence on seclusion by tranquillization is because the bases of psychic power to be developed have been stated in the passage, ‘He develops concentration-of-desire…pe… the basis of psychic power’ (Vibh 432). For the bases of psychic power included in the fruit are to be realized by one who has already developed the bases of psychic power.

Vossaggasaddo pariccāgattho, pakkhandanattho cāti vossaggassa duvidhatā vuttā. Yathāvuttena pakārenāti tadaṅgasamucchedappakārena, tanninnabhāvārammaṇappakārena ca. Pariṇamantaṃ vipassanakkhaṇe.

The word ‘relinquishment’ has the meaning of abandoning and the meaning of leaping forward; thus the twofold nature of relinquishment is stated. ‘In the manner stated’ means: by the mode of substitution of opposites and cutting off, and by the mode of having an object with a nature inclined towards that. ‘Inclining towards’: at the moment of insight.

14. Pubbabhāgapaññāyāti ekāvajjananānāvajjanavīthīsu pavattaupacārapaññāya. Adhigamapaññāyāti appanāpaññāya. Puna pubbabhāgapaññāyāti nānāvajjanupacārapaññāya, paṭisandhipaññāya vā. Upacārapaññāyāti ekāvajjane, sabbattha vā pavattaupacārapaññāya.

14. ‘By preliminary-part wisdom’ means: by the access wisdom occurring in the processes of single adverting and multiple adverting. ‘By attainment wisdom’ means: by the wisdom of absorption. Again, ‘by preliminary-part wisdom’ means: by the access wisdom of multiple adverting, or by the wisdom of rebirth-linking. ‘By access wisdom’ means: by the access wisdom occurring in single adverting, or occurring everywhere.

Pucchāvissajjanavicayopīti yathāvuttāya pucchāya vissajjanavicayopi. Vuttanayānusārenāti adiṭṭhajotanā, vimaticchedanā cāti heṭṭhā vuttanayānugamanena.

‘Also the investigation by question and answer’ means: also the investigation of the answer to the question as stated. ‘In accordance with the stated method’ means: by following the method stated previously, namely, illuminating the unseen and cutting off doubt.

15. Sekhe [M 0.0054] asekheti sekkhe ariyapuggale, asekkhe ariyapuggale. Vipassanāpubbaṅgamappahāneti vipassanaṃ purecārikaṃ katvā pavattakilesappahāne, pahānābhisamayeti attho.

15. ‘In the trainee and the non-trainee’ means: in the noble person who is a trainee and in the noble person who is a non-trainee. ‘In the abandoning preceded by insight’ means: in the abandoning of defilements that proceeds having made insight the forerunner; the meaning is, in the abandoning and realization.

“Yaṃ [V 0.0051] aniccaṃ dukkhaṃ anattā”ti pāḷiṃ dassetvā puna “yaṃ anicce dukkhe anattanī”ti vacanaṃ evampettha paṭhantīti dassetuṃ.

Having shown the Pāli text, ‘Whatever is impermanent, suffering, non-self,’ this is to show that they also recite here the statement, ‘Whatever is in the impermanent, in suffering, in the non-self.’

Sesasaṃkilesavodānadhammāti gedhato avasiṭṭhasaṃkilesadhammā ca sabbavodānadhammā ca. Abhāvenāti abhāvanena abhāvakaraṇena.

‘The remaining defiling and cleansing states’ means: the defiling states remaining after greed, and all cleansing states. ‘Through absence’ means: through non-development, through causing non-existence.

Payogaparakkamanti bhusaṃ yogo payogo, payogova parakkamo payogaparakkamo, cittaṃ. Ukkhipatīti kosajjapakkhe patituṃ adento kusalapakkhe uddhaṃ khipento viya pavattati. Padhānavīriyanti akusalānaṃ anuppādanaṭṭhena uttamavīriyaṃ. Yojetabbānīti “āsevamāno vāyamatī”tiādinā yojetabbāni. Anuppannāti avattabbataṃ āpannānanti bhūmiladdhārammaṇādhiggahitāvikkhambhitāsamugghāṭituppannānaṃ.

`Payogaparakkamaṃ`: Intense application is payoga; application itself is exertion, payogaparakkama; this means the mind. `It lifts up` means: not allowing it to fall to the side of laziness, it proceeds as if flinging it upwards to the side of the wholesome. `Supreme energy` means the highest energy in the sense of not allowing unwholesome states to arise. `They should be yoked` means they should be yoked with such phrases as 'cultivating, he strives…' `Not arisen` means: of those that have not reached a state where they can be spoken of, that is, of those that have not arisen by having obtained a plane, been taken up as an object, been suppressed, or been eradicated.

16. “Aṭṭhamakassa indriyānī”ti vuttattā “paṭhamamagge saddhādayo”tiādi vuttaṃ. Indriyaggahaṇañca pāḷiyaṃ nidassanamattaṃ daṭṭhabbaṃ.

16. Because it is said, 'the faculties of the eighth person,' it is therefore stated, 'on the first path, faith and so on…' And the taking up of 'faculties' in the Pāli text should be seen as a mere indication.

Asubhānupassanā kāyānupassanāsatipaṭṭhānanti āha “satipaṭṭhānabhāvanāya suniggahito kāmavitakko”ti. Samādhi uppajjamāno kāmavitakkampi niggahetvā eva uppajjatīti dassento “anavajjasukhapadaṭṭhānenā”tiādimāha. “Kusalesu dhammesu āraddhavīriyo”tiādinā dhammacchandato uppajjamāno vīriyacchando khantiṃ paribrūhetīti dasseti. Anavajjadhammānaṃ upakārakadhammāsevanaṃ viya anupakārakadhammaparivajjanampi paññānisevaneneva hotīti āha “samādhiādīna”ntiādi.

He says that the contemplation of the foul is the establishment of mindfulness as contemplation of the body, citing: 'A thought of sensual desire is well restrained by the development of the establishments of mindfulness.' Showing that concentration, when arising, arises only after having restrained a thought of sensual desire, he says, 'by means of the proximate cause of blameless happiness…' By means of the phrase 'one with aroused energy in regard to wholesome states…' he shows that the desire for energy, arising from desire for the Dhamma, enhances patience. He says, 'of concentration, etc…' to show that just as there is the cultivation of helpful states for blameless states, so too the avoidance of unhelpful states occurs only through the cultivation of wisdom.

17. Sabbadhammādhiṭṭhānaṃ desanaṃ puggalādhiṭṭhānena vibhajituṃ “loko nāmā”tiādi vuttanti dassento “sabbadhammānanti…pe… dassetu”nti āha. Mahaggatadhammesu ṭhānaṃ taṃsampādanāva. Tathā sesesu. Vaḍḍhiyamānesūti yathā vimuttiṃ paripācayanti, evaṃ brūhiyamānesu.

17. Showing that the phrase 'the world, namely…' etc., was stated in order to analyze the teaching with its basis in all phenomena by way of a basis in a person, he says, 'of all phenomena… to show.' In the case of sublime states, their 'standing' is simply their accomplishment. So too for the rest. `When they are being increased` means: as they bring liberation to maturity, so when they are being augmented.

Dassanapariññāti rūpārūpadhammānaṃ salakkhaṇato, paccayato ca parijānanā. Tenāha “ñātapariññā”ti. Paṭipakkhavidhamanena saddhiṃ lakkhaṇattayavibhāvanā idha “bhāvanāpariññā”ti adhippetāti āha “bhāvanā…pe… pariññā cā”ti[M 0.0055]. Dassanatthā pariññā dassanapariññā, bhāvanatthā pariññā bhāvanāpariññāti evaṃ vā ettha attho daṭṭhabbo.

Full understanding by seeing is the thorough knowing of material and immaterial phenomena by way of their individual characteristics and their conditions. For that reason, it is called 'full understanding as knowledge.' It is said, 'development… and full understanding…' to show that here the demonstrating of the three characteristics, together with the dispelling of the opposing side, is intended as 'full understanding by development.' Or the meaning here should be seen thus: full understanding for the purpose of seeing is full understanding by seeing; full understanding for the purpose of development is full understanding by development.

Kakkhaḷaphusanādīti [V 0.0052] kakkhaḷādiphusanādi. Abhijānitvāti abhiññāya paññāya jānitvā, ṭhitassa abhijānanahetu vāti attho. Atthoti phalaṃ. Nayoti vuttanayo.

`Contact with the hard, etc.` means contact with the hard and so on. `Having directly known` means: having known with the wisdom of direct knowledge; or the meaning is, it is a cause for the direct knowing of one who is established. `Meaning` is fruit. `Method` is the method that has been stated.

“Yaṃ asaṅkhata”ntipi paṭhanti. Catunayakovidoti ekattanānattādinayacatukke nipuṇo. Desanāyuttikusaloti dhammānaṃ desanāvidhimhi kusalo.

Some also read, 'that which is unconditioned.' `Skilled in the four methods` means skilled in the fourfold method concerning unity, diversity, and so on. `Skilled in the suitability of the teaching` means skilled in the method of teaching the Dhamma.

Sadisī kātabbā saṃsandanavasenāti adhippāyo. Ānetabbā “ayaṃ desanā imāya desanāya evaṃ saṃsandatī”ti. Atthato apetanti ayuttatthaṃ. Asambandhatthanti aññamaññaṃ asambandhapadatthaṃ. Nanu paṭṭhānavicāro nayavicāro viya hārehi asammisso vicāraṇantaroti codanaṃ manasi katvā āha “yasmā panā”tiādi. Idha nikkhittoti idha suttavicaye suttatthavicārabhāvato nikkhitto, etena vā paṭṭhānassa hārantogadhabhāvadassaneneva mūlapadānaṃ viya paṭṭhānassa padatthantarābhāvo dassitoti veditabbaṃ.

The intention is that it should be made similar by way of collation. It should be adduced thus: 'This teaching collates with that teaching in this way.' `Devoid of meaning` means an unsuitable meaning. `Unconnected meaning` means the meaning of terms unconnected with each other. Having kept in mind the objection, 'Surely the investigation of the point of departure, like the investigation of the method, is a separate investigation, not mixed with the modes of exegesis?' he says, 'But since…' etc. `Laid down here` means laid down here in the investigation of the sutta because it is an investigation of the sutta's meaning. Or, it should be understood that by this—by showing that the point of departure is not included within the modes of exegesis, just like the root terms—it is shown that the point of departure has no other meaning of a term.

Imassa suttassāti saṃvaṇṇiyamānasuttaṃ sandhāyāha. Kasmiṃ vā padeti saṃvaṇṇiyamānaṃ gāthaṃ sandhāyāha. Tabbicayenāti pucchādivicayena, assādādivicayena ca.

`Of this sutta`: he speaks referring to the sutta that is being commented upon. `In which verse?`: he speaks referring to the verse that is being commented upon. `By its investigation` means by investigation through questions, etc., and by investigation of gratification, etc.

Vicayahāravibhaṅgavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Explanation of the Analysis of the Mode of Investigation is concluded.

3. Yuttihāravibhaṅgavaṇṇanā

3. The Explanation of the Analysis of the Mode of Reasoning

18. Evametassa suttassa attho na gahetabbo, evaṃ pana gahetabboti aggahetabbagahetabbānaṃ atthānaṃ vijahanaggahaṇatthāya yuttāyuttivicāraṇāyaṃ vajjetabbesu tāva paṭhamaṃ paṭipattīti dassento āha “atathākārena gayhamānā suttatthā visayo”ti yathā “vāmaṃ muñca, dakkhiṇaṃ gaṇhā”ti (dha. sa. aṭṭha. 498; visuddhi. mahāṭī. 1.14; saṃ. ni. ṭī. 1.1.213). Vajjetabbabhāvato hi suttapadehi suttatthe [M 0.0056] vivecite gahetabbabhāvo ca avasiṭṭho hoti. Tathā hi vakkhati “mettāvihārassa sato byāpādo cittaṃ pariyādāya ṭhassatī’ti na yujjati desanā, ‘byāpādo pahānaṃ abbhatthaṃ gacchatī’ti yujjati desanā”ti (netti. 21).

18. Showing that, for the purpose of abandoning meanings that should not be grasped and grasping those that should be grasped—by saying, 'The meaning of this sutta should not be taken in this way, but should be taken in this way'—in the investigation of the suitable and unsuitable, the first practice among things to be rejected is this, he says: 'The meanings of the sutta, when grasped in an incorrect manner, are the object,' as in 'Release the left, take the right.' For, because the wrong meaning is to be rejected, when the meaning of the sutta has been distinguished from the words of the sutta, the part that is to be grasped is what remains. For so it will be said: 'It is not suitable to teach that "for one dwelling in loving-kindness, ill will will continue to obsess the mind," but it is suitable to teach that "ill will is abandoned and comes to an end."

Yuttiniddhāraṇena [V 0.0053] ayathāsabhāvato vivecitvā yathāsabhāvato dhammassa gahaṇakāraṇāni kathento “mahantā apadisitabbā etesanti mahāpadesā”ti imamatthamāha “buddhādayo”tiādinā. Patiṭṭhānānīti patiṭṭhānasādhanāni. Sesesūti saṅghāpadesādīsu. Paṭhamattho eva hi idha pāḷi āgato, vinicchayane kāraṇaṃ mahāpadesoti adhippāyo. Suttotaraṇādīti ādisaddena suttānotaraṇādipi saṅgayhati. Suttotaraṇavinayasandassanāni hi kenaci yathābhatassa ganthassa “dhammo”ti vinicchayane kāraṇaṃ. Suttānotaraṇavinayāsandassanāni “adhammo”ti. Yadi evanti yadi yathābhatassa ganthassa suttavinayehi saṃsandanaṃ “dhammo”ti, asaṃsandanaṃ “adhammo”ti vinicchayakāraṇaṃ, evaṃ santeti attho. Sampadīyati ñāpīyati dhammo etehīti sampadāyā, akkhātāro.

Explaining the reasons for grasping the Dhamma according to its true nature, having distinguished it from its untrue nature by the determination of what is suitable, he states this meaning of 'great references'—'great ones are to be referred to by these'—with the words 'the Buddhas, etc.' `Foundations` means the means of establishing. `In the remaining cases` refers to the reference to the Sangha, etc. Indeed, the first meaning has come down here in the Pāli; the intention is that the great reference is the reason in making a decision. `Bringing a sutta to bear, etc.`: by the word 'etc.,' not bringing a sutta to bear, etc., is also included. For bringing a sutta to bear and seeing how it fits with the Vinaya are the reason for deciding 'this is Dhamma' in the case of some text as it has been brought. Not bringing a sutta to bear and not seeing how it fits with the Vinaya are the reason for deciding 'this is not Dhamma.' `If so` means: if the collation of a text as it has been brought with the Sutta and Vinaya is the reason for deciding 'this is Dhamma,' and its non-collation is the reason for deciding 'this is not Dhamma,' this being so is the meaning. `The Dhamma is handed down, is made known, by them,' thus they are the transmitters, the declarers.

Vinīyanti rāgādayo etenāti vinayo, kāraṇaṃ. Tenāha “rāgādivūpasamanimitta”nti. Kiṃ pana taṃ? Sādhiṭṭhānasamathavipassanādidhammā. Ye parato “tecattālīsaṃ bodhaṅgamā dhammā”ti (netti. 24) vakkhati.

`Lust, etc., are disciplined by this,' thus it is the 'Discipline' (Vinaya); the cause. Therefore he says, 'the sign of the calming of lust, etc.' But what is that? The states of serenity, insight, etc., with their foundation, which he will speak of later as 'the forty-three states that are wings to awakening.'

Vinayamahāpadesā kappiyānulomato anulomakappiyaṃ nāma, taṃsadisatāya suttantamahāpadesāpi anulomakappiyanti aṭṭhakathāvohāro. Tena vuttaṃ “yaṃ anulomakappiyanti vuccatī”ti.

The great reference of the Vinaya is called 'allowable by conformity' from 'conformity with what is allowable.' Due to similarity with that, the great references of the Suttanta are also called 'allowable by conformity'—this is the usage in the commentary. Hence it is said: 'What is called "allowable by conformity."

Yadipi tattha tattha pavattā bhagavato pakiṇṇakadesanā aṭṭhakathā, sā pana dhammasaṅgāhakehi tepiṭakaṃ buddhavacanaṃ saṅgāyitvā tassa atthasaṃvaṇṇanānurūpena vācanāmaggaṃ āropitattā ācariyavādo nāma. Tena vuttaṃ “ācariyavādo nāma aṭṭhakathā”ti. Tissopi saṅgītiyo āruḷho eva hi buddhavacanassa atthasaṃvaṇṇanābhūto kathāmaggo pacchā tambapaṇṇiyehi mahātherehi sīhaḷabhāsāya ṭhapito. Attanomati theravādo. Samentameva gahetabbanti yathā pāḷiyā saṃsandati[M 0.0057], evaṃ mahāpadesato atthā uddharitabbāti dasseti. Pamādapāṭhavasena ācariyavādassa kadāci pāḷiyā asaṃsandanāpi siyā, so na gahetabboti dassento āha – “suttena samento eva gahetabbo”ti.

Although the commentary is the miscellaneous teachings of the Blessed One that occurred here and there, yet because the Dhamma-reciters, after chanting the Tipiṭaka, the word of the Buddha, established a path of recitation in conformity with the explanation of its meaning, it is called the 'doctrine of the teachers.' Therefore it is said: 'The doctrine of the teachers is the commentary.' For the path of discourse that constitutes the explanation of the meaning of the Buddha's word, which had indeed been included in the three Councils, was later established in the Sinhala language by the great elders of Tambapaṇṇi. The Theravāda is one's own opinion. He shows that 'only what agrees should be accepted' means that meanings should be extracted from the great references in such a way that they collate with the Pāli. Showing that sometimes, through error or a textual mistake, there might be a non-collation of the teachers' doctrine with the Pāli, and that this should not be accepted, he says: 'Only what agrees with the Sutta should be accepted.'

Catūhi mahāpadesehi yujjatīti catūhi mahāpadesehi na virujjhati. Idāni taṃ avirujjhanākāraṃ dassento “yena yenā”tiādi vuttaṃ. Suttotaraṇādi eva hettha kāraṇaṃ. Tassa ca anekākāratāya “pakārenā”ti vutto. Saṃvaṇṇiyamāne sutte saṃvaṇṇanāvasena gahetabbanti [V 0.0054] sambandho. Ābhatenāti ānītena. Suttatoti suttantarato. Ayañhettha attho – kenaci pasaṅgena suttantarato uddharitvā ānītena suttapadena suttotaraṇādinā, kāraṇappakārena ca catumahāpadesāvirodhena saṃvaṇṇiyamāne sutte saṃvaṇṇanāvasena atthajātaṃ gahetabbanti. Tenāha “tena…pe… kātabbā”ti. Tattha yuttihārayojanā kātabbāti yuttiniddhāraṇavasena ayaṃ yuttihāro yojetabbo. Atha vā yuttihārayojanā kātabbāti iminā hārena vakkhamānanayena yuttigavesanaṃ katvā tāya yuttiyā sabbahārayojanā kātabbāti attho. Lakkhaṇañhetaṃ yuttigavesanāya, yadidaṃ yuttihāro. Tenāha “sabbesaṃ hārānaṃ, yā bhūmī, yo ca gocaro tesaṃ. “Yuttāyuttaparikkhā”ti (netti. 4), “imāya yuttiyā aññamaññehi kāraṇehi gavesitabba”nti (netti. 20) ca.

`It is consistent with the four great references` means it does not conflict with the four great references. Now, showing that manner of non-conflict, 'by whatever…' etc., is said. Bringing a sutta to bear, etc., is the reason here. And because of its manifold nature, 'manner' is stated. The connection is: in a sutta that is being commented upon, the meaning should be grasped by way of the commentary. `Brought` means adduced. `From the sutta` means from another sutta. The meaning here is this: in a sutta that is being commented upon, the collection of meanings should be grasped by way of the commentary, without conflict with the four great references, by means of a sutta passage brought and adduced from another sutta on some occasion, by bringing a sutta to bear, etc., and by a manner of reasoning. Therefore he says, 'by that… should be done.' Therein, 'the application of the mode of reasoning should be made' means this mode of reasoning should be applied by way of determining what is suitable. Or, 'the application of the mode of reasoning should be made' means that having investigated for suitability by the method to be explained by this mode, the application of all the modes should be made with that suitability. For this—the mode of reasoning—is the characteristic of the investigation for suitability. Therefore he says: 'of all the modes, what is their ground and what their domain,' and 'the examination of the suitable and unsuitable,' and 'by this suitability one should investigate with various reasons.'

19. Yadi vā sabbāni padāni ekaṃ atthaṃ abhivadantīti yojanā.

19. Or, the application is: if all the terms express a single meaning.

20. Jarāyaṃ ṭhitassa aññathattanti ṭhitassa yaṃ aññathattaṃ aññathābhāvo, ayaṃ jarā nāma. Khaṇikamaraṇaṃ khaṇikanirodho. Samucchedamaraṇaṃ khīṇāsavānaṃ khandhaparinibbānaṃ.

20. The alteration of what is standing is aging; that is, the alteration or change of what is standing. This is called aging. Momentary death is momentary cessation. Death by complete cutting off is the passing away of the aggregates of those with destroyed taints.

Kevalassāti jarāya amissassa. Aññāva jarā, aññaṃ maraṇanti “paṭiññātassa kevalassa maraṇassa diṭṭhattā”ti hetu. Yathā taṃ devānanti sadisūdāharaṇaṃ, visadisūdāharaṇaṃ pana iddhipādādayo, anvayabyatirekā gahetvā yojetabbā.

“Kevalassa” means not mixed with aging. Aging is one thing, death is another—thus the reason is “because the mere death that is declared is seen.” Just as with the gods—this is a similar example, but dissimilar examples such as the bases of psychic power should be taken and applied through correlation and contrast.

Tehīti [M 0.0058] jarāmaraṇehi.

“Tehi” means by aging and death.

“Jīraṇabhijjanasabhāvā”ti iminā lesena taṇhājarāmaraṇānaṃ anaññattaṃ yojeti. Yadipi “aññā taṇhā, aññā jarā, aññaṃ maraṇa”nti siddhovāyamattho, yaṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ “na ida”ntiādi, tathāpi sakkuṇeyyaparihārāyaṃ codanāti ajjhāruḷhaṃ tattha dosaṃ dassetuṃ “yadi ca yathā jarāmaraṇa”nti pāḷipavattāni dassento “yadi…pe… dassetī”ti āha. Bhāvoti adhippāyo. Etesanti taṇhājarāmaraṇānaṃ.

“Jīraṇabhijjanasabhāvā” (of the nature to decay and break up)—by way of this indication, he connects craving, aging, and death as non-different. Even though this meaning—that “craving is one thing, aging another, and death yet another”—is established, in reference to which it was said “not this” and so forth, still, to show the fault in that entrenched view, which is an objection that can be refuted, he presents the scriptural passage “if indeed, just as aging and death,” and so forth, saying “if... (etc.)... shows.” “Bhāva” means intention. “Etesaṃ” refers to craving, aging, and death.

“Imāya yuttiyā aññamaññehi kāraṇehi gavesitabba”nti ca keci paṭhanti, byañjanatopi gavesitabbaṃ, aññattha atthato aññatthampīti adhippāyo. Tameva byañjanato aññatthaṃ dassetuṃ pāḷiyaṃ “salloti vā”tiādi vuttaṃ. Imesanti salladhūpāyanānaṃ. Icchāvipariyāyeti [V 0.0055] icchitālābhe, appaccayasamavāye vā. Idampi samatthanaṃ hoti yathādhippetassa aññatthassa byatirekadassanabhāvato. Jarāmaraṇavipariyāyeti jarāmaraṇe asati. Na hi yathādhippetajarāmaraṇābhāve taṇhā na hotīti.

Some read it as: “It should be sought by this reasoning, by mutual causes,” and also by way of expression; the intention is that it should be sought elsewhere in meaning and also elsewhere in expression. To show that same meaning elsewhere by way of expression, it is stated in the Pāli: “or a dart,” and so forth. “Imesaṃ” refers to the dart, smoke, and tribute. “Icchāvipariyāye” means when what is desired is not obtained, or when conditions are not met. This too is a substantiation, due to the showing of the absence of the intended other meaning. “Jarāmaraṇavipariyāye” means when aging and death are absent. For it is not that craving is absent just because the intended aging and death are absent.

Dvidhā vuttāti dvippakārena vuttā, dvikkhattuṃ vā vuttā. Yaṃ idaṃ…pe… ārammaṇakaraṇavasena vā abhilapananti evaṃ kiriyāparāmasanaṃ yojetabbanti veditabbaṃ. Visesoti ayaṃ etāsaṃ icchātaṇhānaṃ pakatisaṅkhāto viseso. “Dvīhi nāmehī”tipi pāḷi. Yadipi evanti kāmaṃ visayavisesesu evaṃ yathāvuttaavatthāvisesena icchātaṇhānaṃ atthi kāci bhedamattāti attho. Sabhāvato pana bhedo natthīti dassento “tathāpī”ti āha.

“Dvidhā vuttā” (stated in two ways) means stated in two kinds, or stated twice. It should be understood that this—(etc.)—or speaking by way of making an object, thus the grasping at action should be connected. “Viseso” (distinction) means this is the distinction of these cravings born of desire, conventionally designated as such. The Pāli also says “by two names.” Even if it is so—meaning, although there is some mere differentiation of cravings born of desire regarding the distinctions of sense objects in the aforementioned distinct states—still, showing that there is no difference in reality, he says “nevertheless.”

Icchantīti kāmenti. Taṇhāyanā pātukāmatā. Santāpanaṭṭhenāti paridahanabhāvena. Ākaḍḍhanaṭṭhenāti avaharaṇaṭṭhena. Saritānīti rāgavasena allāni. Taṃsampayuttapītivasena siniddhāni sinehitāni. Visattikāti vitthatā rūpādīsu tebhūmakadhammesu byāpanavasena. Visaṭāti purimavevacanameva ta-kārassa ṭa-kāraṃ katvā vuttaṃ. Visālāti vipulā. Visakkatīti parisakkati sahati. Ratto hi rāgavatthunā pādena tāḷiyamānopi sahati. “Osakkanaṃ, vipphandanaṃ vā visakkana”nti vadanti. Aniccādikaṃ niccādito [M 0.0059] gaṇhantī visaṃvādikā hoti. Visaṃharatīti tathā tathā kāmesu ānisaṃsaṃ dassentī vividhehi ākārehi nekkhammābhimukhappavattito cittaṃ saṃharati saṃkhipati. Visaṃ vā dukkhaṃ, taṃ harati, vahatīti attho. Dukkhanibbattakassa kammassa hetubhāvato visamūlā, visaṃ vā dukkhādibhedā vedanā mūlaṃ etāyāti visamūlā, dukkhasamudayattā visaṃ phalaṃ etissāti visaphalā. Rūpādidukkhasseva paribhogo etāya, na amatassāti visaparibhogā. Sabbattha niruttivasena padasiddhi veditabbā. Yo panettha padhāno attho, taṃ dassetuṃ puna “visatā vā panā”tiādi vuttaṃ.

“Icchanti” means they crave. “Taṇhāyanā” is the longing to manifest. “Santāpanaṭṭhena” means by way of scorching. “Ākaḍḍhanaṭṭhena” means by way of pulling away. “Saritāni” means wet with passion. “Siniddhāni sinehitāni” means moistened and beloved, by way of the joy associated with it. “Visattikā” means widespread, pervading forms and other phenomena in the three realms of existence through pervasiveness. “Visaṭā” is just a synonym for the previous term, with the ‘ṭ’ sound replacing the ‘t’ sound. “Visālā” means extensive. “Visakkati” means it struggles, it tolerates. For one who is lustful tolerates even when struck by the foot of the object of desire. They say, “‘visakkana’ means retreating or writhing.” “Visaṃvādikā” means it deceives by taking the impermanent, etc., as permanent, etc. “Visaṃharati” means it draws the mind away in various ways, showing the benefits of sensual pleasures, diverting it from inclining towards renunciation. Or, it carries poison, which is suffering; that is the meaning. “Visamūlā” (rooted in poison) because it is the cause of actions that produce suffering. Or, “visamūlā” because suffering and other types of feelings are its root. “Visaphalā” (bearing poison as fruit), because its fruit is poison, since it is the origin of suffering. “Visaparibhogā” (indulging in poison) because by it one indulges only in the suffering of forms, etc., not in the deathless. Everywhere, the derivation of the words should be understood according to etymology. But to explain the primary meaning here, it is further said, “or again, spreading out,” and so forth.

Sinehanaṃ pemakaraṇaṃ. Bandhanaṭṭhenāti saṃyojanaṭṭhena. Āsīsanaṭṭhenāti icchanaṭṭhena. Abhinandanaṭṭhenāti assādanaṭṭhena, sampaṭicchanaṭṭhena vā.

“Sinehanaṃ” means the act of making dear. “Bandhanaṭṭhena” means in the sense of a fetter. “Āsīsanaṭṭhena” means in the sense of wishing. “Abhinandanaṭṭhena” means in the sense of savoring or acceptance.

21. Anabhiratīti ukkaṇṭhā. Ñāṇanibbidāti nibbidānupassanā. Yathā ca dukkhū…pe… cāresu yutti vuttāti yojanā.

21. “Anabhirati” means weariness. “Ñāṇanibbidā” means insight into disenchantment. And the connection is stated as the fitting of suffering—(etc.)—in the practices.

Sukhāpaṭipadādandhābhiññā [V 0.0056] sukhāpaṭipadākhippābhiññā sukhāpaṭipadādayo. Yo dukkhāya paṭipadāya visesaṃ adhigantuṃ bhabbo, tassa sukhāpaṭipadāyogyassa viya kariyamānā dhammadesanā visesāvahā na hoti, tasmā sā na yuttāti imamatthaṃ dasseti “rāgacarito”tiādinā. Rāgacaritassa tathā tathā kāmānaṃ ādīnavaṃ, okāraṃ, saṃkilesaṃ, nekkhamme ānisaṃsañca avibhāvetvā ādito vipassanākathāva kariyamānā na visesāvahā hoti āsayassa asodhitattāti etamatthaṃ dassento pāḷiyaṃ “vipassanā…pe… desanā”ti āhāti veditabbaṃ. Sesapadesupīti yathā “rāgacaritassā”tiādinā rāgacaritakoṭṭhāsavasena pāḷiyaṃ desanāya ayutti vuttā, iminā nayena sesapadesupi dosacaritakoṭṭhāsādīsupi “dosacaritassa puggalassa asubhaṃ deseyyā”tiādinā pāḷiyaṃ avuttopi yathāsambhavamattho niddhāretvā vattabbo. Kasmā pana yuttihāre ayuttiniddhāraṇā katāti codanaṃ manasi katvā āha “ettha cā”tiādi. Sesesupi eseva nayoti sesesupi dosacaritādivasena niddhāritesu ayuttigavesanesu ayameva upāyo. Anulomappahāna”ntipi pāḷi, so evattho.

The pleasant path with slow insight, the pleasant path with quick insight, the pleasant path, and so on. For one who is capable of attaining distinction through the difficult path, the teaching of the Dhamma, given as if to one suited for the pleasant path, does not bring distinction; therefore it is not appropriate—this meaning is shown by “one of lustful temperament,” and so on. For one of lustful temperament, unless the drawbacks of sensual pleasures, their wretchedness, defilement, and the benefits of renunciation are thoroughly considered from the beginning, merely discussing insight meditation does not bring distinction because the disposition has not been purified—this meaning is shown in the Pāli passage “insight... teaching,” it should be understood. In the remaining cases as well—just as in the passage “for one of lustful temperament,” where the unsuitability of the teaching is stated in relation to the category of lustful temperament—in the same way, in the remaining cases such as the category of hateful temperament, etc., though not stated in the Pāli, the meaning should be determined and stated appropriately, such as “for a person of hateful temperament, the unattractive should be taught,” and so on. But why, when applying reasoning, is the determination of unsuitability made? Considering this objection in mind, it is said: “Here also...” and so on. In the remaining cases as well, this is the method for searching for unsuitability in terms of hateful temperament, etc., once they have been determined. “Anulomappahāna” (gradual abandonment) is also a Pāli phrase, and that is its meaning.

“Yāvatikā [M 0.0060] ñāṇassa bhūmī”ti etena yuttihārassa mahāvisayataṃ dasseti. Kasmā panāyaṃ mahāvisayoti? Yuttivicārabhāvato, saṃvaṇṇetabbassa ca dhammassa nānānayanipuṇādiguṇavisesayogatoti dassento “taṃ kissa hetū”ti āha.

“Yāvatikā ñāṇassa bhūmī” (as far as the ground of knowledge extends)—by this, he shows the extensive scope of the logical application. “But why is this so extensive?” Showing that it is so because of its nature as reasoned investigation, and because the Dhamma to be described is endowed with the special qualities of skill in various approaches and so forth, he says, “For what reason?”

Aparabhāgeti pacchābhāge. Mettāvihāribhāgassa aparihīnatāvacanato ayogo vutto. Tenāha “sato”ti. Yathāvuttakāraṇato evāti paṭipakkhattā eva.

“Aparabhāge” means in the latter portion. The unsuitability is stated from the statement of the non-diminishment of the loving-kindness dwelling portion. Therefore he says, “mindful.” “Yathāvuttakāraṇato eva” means indeed, because of the reason stated, due to its being the opposite.

Pahānekaṭṭhabhāvato diṭṭhimaññitassa. Ādīnavadassanena vitakkaṃ jigucchantā dutiyajjhānassa āsannaupacārajjhānadhammāpi vitakkārammaṇā na honti, pageva dutiyajjhānadhammāti adhippāyenāha “ārammaṇakaraṇattho hettha sahagatasaddo”ti.

For one conceited with views, due to the singleness of meaning in terms of abandonment. Seeing the danger and being disgusted with thought, even the factors of the near-access jhāna to the second jhāna do not have thought as their object, how much less so the factors of the second jhāna itself. With this intention, he says: “Here, the term ‘associated’ refers to the function of making an object.”

Evaṃ yuttihāralakkhaṇaṃ āgamato yuttāyuttavicāraṃ dassetvā idāni āgamānugatāya yuttiyāpi taṃ dassetuṃ guṇamukhena dosavibhajanaṃ vibhajanto “apicetthā”tiādimāha. Taṃ uttānatthameva.

Having thus shown the characteristic of logical application and the examination of what is suitable and unsuitable according to tradition, now, in order to demonstrate that very thing by reasoning which follows tradition, he distinguishes faults by way of virtues, saying “furthermore, here,” and so forth. Its meaning is self-evident.

Yuttihāravibhaṅgavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Analysis of Logical Application is completed.

4. Padaṭṭhānahāravibhaṅgavaṇṇanā

4. Commentary on the Analysis of the Mode of the Basis

22. Tesaṃ [V 0.0057] tesanti anavasesapariyādānaṃ, tena ye sutte vuttā ca dhammā, ye ca tesaṃ kāraṇabhūtaṃ, tesaṃ sabbesampīti vuttaṃ hoti. Sabbadhammayāthāvaasampaṭivedhoti imamatthaṃ dassetuṃ “kattha pana so”tiādi vuttaṃ.

22. The phrase “tesaṃ tesaṃ” (of them, of them) signifies complete inclusion without remainder; hence, it is said that all phenomena mentioned in the Sutta, and their causes, are included. To show the meaning that one has not fully penetrated the true nature of all phenomena, it is said, “But where is that?” and so on.

Piyāyitabbajātiyanti pemanīyasabhāvaṃ. Micchāpaṭipadāti pamādāpatti, micchābhiniveso vā. Ekavāraṃ uppannāpi pāṇātipātacetanā verappasavanato dosassa, ekavāraṃ uppannāpi pamādāpatti, micchābhiniveso vā mohassa uppattikāraṇanti pāḷiyaṃ avuttampi nayato niddhāretabbanti dassento “dosassa…pe… imināva nayena attho veditabbo”ti āha. Nimittatthaggahaṇalakkhaṇaṃ vaṇṇasaṇṭhānaṃ, anubyañjanatthaggahaṇalakkhaṇaṃ anubyañjananti “nimittānubyañjanaggahaṇalakkhaṇā”ti vuttaṃ. Tattha tattha nimittaṃ itthipurisanimittaṃ. Anubyañjanaṃ hatthapādahasitakathitādi. Phasso paccayo etissāti [M 0.0061] phassapaccayā, taṃbhāvo phassapaccayatā. Assādeti etāyāti assādo, taṇhā.

The phrase 'piyāyitabbajāti' means the state of being lovable. 'Wrong practice' (micchāpaṭipadā) is either an offense due to negligence or wrong adherence. Even a single instance of the intention to kill living beings is a cause for the arising of hatred because it generates enmity; and even a single instance of an offense due to negligence or wrong adherence is a cause for the arising of delusion. To show that even what is not stated in the Pāḷi should be determined by implication, it is said, 'Of hatred... and so on... the meaning should be understood in this same way.' The characteristic of grasping the sign is color and shape; the characteristic of grasping the secondary feature is the secondary feature. Hence it is said, 'having the characteristic of grasping signs and secondary features.' Therein, the sign is the sign of a woman or a man. The secondary feature is the hands, feet, laughter, speech, and so on. 'Contact is a condition for it' (phasso paccayo etissā), thus it is 'conditioned by contact' (phassapaccayā); its state is 'contact-conditionality' (phassapaccayatā). 'One savors with it' (assādeti etāya), hence 'savoring' (assādo), which is craving (taṇhā).

Vatthūni ñeyyadhammoti āha “vatthuavippaṭipatti visayasabhāvapaṭivedho”ti. Idhādhippetaṃ sammāpaṭipattiṃ dassetuṃ “sīlasamādhisampadāna”nti vuttaṃ. Ekadesupalakkhaṇavasena, vaṇṇagandharāgisappāyavasena vā pāḷiyaṃ “vinīlakavipubbakaggahaṇalakkhaṇā asubhasaññā”ti vatvā “tassā nibbidāpadaṭṭhāna”nti vuttaṃ nibbidaṃ dassento “nibbidā”tiādi vuttaṃ. Tattha paritassanato visesetuṃ “ñāṇenā”ti visesitaṃ. Tathā pavattanti nibbidanākārena pavattanaṃ.

He says, 'Objects are phenomena to be known,' meaning, 'non-deviation from the object is the penetration of the nature of the domain.' To show the right practice intended here, it is said, 'accomplishment in virtue and concentration.' By way of indicating a part, or by way of color, smell, and suitability for lust, it is said in the Pāḷi, 'The perception of foulness has the characteristic of grasping the bluish and festering,' and having said, 'Its proximate cause is disenchantment,' to show disenchantment, 'disenchantment,' and so on is said. Therein, to distinguish it from agitation, it is specified as 'with knowledge.' 'They proceed thus' means proceeding in the mode of disenchantment.

Yoniso ummujjantiyā videharañño dhītāya rucāya jātissarañāṇaṃ kammassakataññāṇassa kāraṇaṃ ahosi, na pana asappurisūpanissayato, ayoniso ummujjantassa tasseva rañño senāpatino alātassa bījakassa dāsassāti imamatthaṃ dassento “imassa ca…pe… udāharitabbo”ti āha. Soti pasādo. Avatthāvisesoti sampayuttadhammānaṃ anāvilabhāvalakkhito avatthābhedo. Āyatanagatoti ṭhānagato, ratanattayavisayoti attho. “Kāyo”tiādinā avatthāvisesena vinā sabhāvasiddhameva padaṭṭhānaṃ dasseti.

For Rucā, the daughter of King Videha, who was emerging through wise attention, the knowledge of past lives became a cause for the knowledge of kamma as one's own, but not from reliance on an unworthy person. To show this meaning—that it was not so for General Alāta and the slave Bījaka of that same king, who were emerging through unwise attention—he says, 'And for this... and so on... should be cited.' That is clarity. 'A particular state' is a different state characterized by the unclouded nature of associated phenomena. 'Gone to the sphere' means gone to the place; the meaning is, having the Three Jewels as its object. By 'body,' and so on, it shows the proximate cause as simply established by its own nature, without a particular state.

Imasmiṃ ca ṭhāne pāḷiyaṃ pubbe yesaṃ dhammānaṃ padaṭṭhānaṃ niddhāritaṃ, te dhammā yesaṃ dhammānaṃ [V 0.0058] padaṭṭhānāni honti, te dassetuṃ “aparo nayo”tiādi āraddhanti veditabbaṃ. Assādamanasikāro ayonisomanasikāralakkhaṇo vutto nidassanamattaatthoti veditabbo, yebhuyyena sattānaṃ lobhavasena ayonisomanasikārā saṃvattantītti dassanatthaṃ vā evaṃ vuttaṃ. Upapatti eva opapaccayaṃ, tassa bhāvo opapaccayikanti āha “upapattibhavabhāvenā”ti. Vavatthitabhāvoti vavatthitabhāvo rūpassa dassanādipaṭiniyatārammaṇakiccatā. Bhavassa aṅgānīti bhavassa kāraṇāni. Dutiye aṅgānīti avayavā, kammavaṭṭampi vā kāraṇaṅgabhāvena yojetabbaṃ.

And in this place, it should be understood that 'another method,' and so on, was begun in order to show those phenomena that become the proximate causes for other phenomena, for which phenomena the proximate cause was previously determined in the Pāḷi. 'Attention to gratification' is said to have the characteristic of unwise attention; this should be understood as merely for the sake of illustration. Or, it is said thus to show that for the most part, unwise attention arises for beings due to greed. Arising itself is the condition for arising, and its state is being conditioned by arising; hence he said, 'by way of the state of the becoming of arising.' 'Determined state' is the determined state of form, which is its function as a specific object for seeing and so on. 'The limbs of existence' are the causes of existence. In the second instance, 'limbs' are parts; or, the round of kamma should be connected as a causal limb.

Kammaṭṭhānassāti bhāvanāya brūhanā vaḍḍhanā. Tesūti titthaññutādīsu. Kalyāṇamittassa sammadeva payirupāsanāyapīti taṃ nissāya laddhena sabbāya dhammassavanena dhammupasaṃhitaṃ pāmojjaṃ hotīti titthaññutā pītaññutāya padaṭṭhānaṃ. Evaṃ yāya vimuttiyā sati vimuttiñāṇadassanaṃ hotīti [M 0.0062] sā tassa padaṭṭhānanti ayamattho pākaṭoti āha “purimānaṃ…pe… suviññeyyo evā”ti. Saha adhiṭṭhānenāti ñātapariññāya saddhiṃ. Ñātapariññā hi tīraṇapariññāya adhiṭṭhānaṃ. Sesaṃ suviññeyyameva.

'Of the meditation subject' means its cultivation, growth, and development. 'Among these' refers to knowing the fords, and so on. Indeed, through proper association with a good friend, joy connected with the Dhamma arises from hearing the complete Dhamma, being supported by that friend. Thus, knowing the fords is the proximate cause for knowing what is imbibed. In this way, when there is a certain liberation, there is knowledge and vision of liberation; that liberation is the proximate cause of this knowledge and vision. This meaning is clear, so he says, 'Of the former... and so on... is very easily understood.' 'With determination' means together with the full understanding of the known. For the full understanding of the known is the basis for the full understanding by scrutiny. The rest is very easily understood.

Padaṭṭhānahāravibhaṅgavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Explanation of the Analysis of the Proximate Cause Method is concluded.

5. Lakkhaṇahāravibhaṅgavaṇṇanā

5. The Explanation of the Analysis of the Characteristic Method.

23. “Lakkhaṇahārassa visayaṃ pucchatī”ti vuttaṃ, “ko pana tassa visayo”ti vutte samānalakkhaṇā avuttadhammā. Kāyānupassanāya samāraddhāya vedanānupassanādayo sukheneva sijjhantīti tabbacanena vedanāgatāsatiādīnaṃ vuttabhāvo dassito satipaṭṭhānabhāvena ekalakkhaṇattāti kāyānupassanāsatipaṭṭhānassa saddhānuggahitāni vīriyasatisamādhipaññindriyāni sādhanaṃ, evaṃ itaresampīti katvā vuttaṃ. Ayaṃ attho aṭṭhakathāyameva (netti. aṭṭha. 51) parato āgamissati. Iminā nayena sesesupi ekalakkhaṇatāniddesesu attho veditabbo. Paratoti catubyūhahāravaṇṇanāyaṃ (netti. aṭṭha. 20).

23. It was said, 'He asks about the domain of the Characteristic Method.' When asked, 'But what is its domain?' the answer is: unmentioned phenomena that have a common characteristic. When the contemplation of the body is undertaken, the contemplations of feeling and so on succeed easily. By this statement it is shown that mindfulness directed to feelings and so on has been mentioned, because they have a single characteristic in being foundations of mindfulness. For the foundation of mindfulness that is the contemplation of the body, the faculties of energy, mindfulness, concentration, and wisdom, supported by faith, are the means of accomplishment; thus it is said for the others as well. This meaning will come later in the commentary itself (Netti. Aṭṭha. 51). In this way, the meaning should be understood in the remaining explanations of the single characteristic. 'Later' refers to the explanation of the Fourfold Array Method (Netti. Aṭṭha. 20).

Asammissatoti vedanādayopi ettha sitā ettha paṭisandhāti kāye vedanādianupassanāpasaṅgepi āpanne tadasammissatoti attho.

'Unmixedly' means that although feelings and so on are based here and rebirth-linking occurs here, even when the occasion for the contemplation of feelings and so on in the body arises, the meaning is 'unmixed with that'.

Avayavigāhasamaññātidhāvanasārādānābhinivesanisedhanatthaṃ [V 0.0059] kāyaṃ aṅgapaccaṅgehi, tāni ca kesādīhi, kesādike ca bhūtupādāyarūpehi vinibbhujituṃ “tathā na kāye”tiādimāha. Pāsādādinagarāvayavasamūhe avayavivādinopi avayavigāhaṇaṃ karonti. Nagaraṃ nāma koci attho atthīti pana kesañci samaññātidhāvanaṃ siyāti itthipurisādisamaññātidhāvane nagaranidassanaṃ vuttaṃ. Añño koci sattādiko. Yaṃ passati itthiṃ, purisaṃ vā. Nanu cakkhunā itthipurisadassanaṃ natthīti? Saccaṃ natthi, “itthiṃ passāmi, purisaṃ passāmī”ti pana pavattasamaññāvasena “yaṃ passatī”ti vuttaṃ. Micchādassanena vā diṭṭhiyā yaṃ passati, na taṃ diṭṭhaṃ rūpāyatanaṃ hoti, rūpāyatanaṃ vā taṃ na hotīti attho. Atha vā taṃ kosādibhūtupādāyasamūhasaṅkhātaṃ [M 0.0063] diṭṭhaṃ na hoti, diṭṭhaṃ vā yathāvuttaṃ na hotīti attho. Yaṃ diṭṭhaṃ taṃ na passatīti yaṃ rūpāyatanaṃ, kesādibhūtupādāyasamūhasaṅkhātaṃ vā diṭṭhaṃ, taṃ paññācakkhunā bhūtato na passatīti attho.

In order to prevent adherence to grasping a substance and running after concepts by analyzing the whole, he says, 'Thus, not in the body,' and so on, in order to dissect the body into its major and minor limbs, these into hair and so on, and the hair and so on into primary and derived matter. Even those who maintain there is a whole analyze its parts, such as the components of a palace or a city. The example of a city is given in the context of running after designations such as 'woman' or 'man,' because for some there might be a running after the concept that 'there is some entity called a city.' Another is some being, and so on. What one sees is a woman or a man. But is it not so that there is no seeing of a woman or a man with the eye? Truly, there is not. But 'what one sees' is said by way of current convention, as in 'I see a woman,' 'I see a man.' Or, what one sees with wrong seeing, with a view, that which is seen is not the visible-form-sphere, or that is not the visible-form-sphere; this is the meaning. Or, that which is seen, designated as the aggregate of derived matter beginning with hair, is not seen; or what is seen is not as stated; this is the meaning. 'What is seen, one does not see' means that what is seen as the visible-form-sphere, or as the aggregate of derived matter beginning with hair, one does not see as it really is with the eye of wisdom.

Na aññadhammānupassīti na aññasabhāvānupassī, asubhādito aññākārānupassī na hotīti vuttaṃ hoti.

'Not contemplating other phenomena' means not contemplating other natures; it is said that one does not contemplate modes other than foulness and so on.

Pathavīkāyanti kesādiṃ pathavīdhammasamūhattā “kāyo”ti vadati, lakkhaṇapathavimeva vā anekabhedabhinnaṃ sakalasarīragataṃ pubbāpariyabhāvena pavattamānaṃ samūhavasena gahetvā “kāyo”ti vadati. Evaṃ aññatthāpi.

'Earth-body' refers to hair and so on, which is called a 'body' because it is a collection of earth-element phenomena. Or, taking the earth element itself—which is characterized by its nature, divided into many kinds, pervading the entire body, and proceeding continuously—as a collection, it is called a 'body.' The same applies elsewhere.

Ākārasamūhasaṅkhātassāti aniccatādiākārasamudāyapariyāyassa.

'Of that which is designated as a collection of modes' means a synonym for the collection of modes such as impermanence and so on.

Tīsu bhavesu kileseti bhavattayavisayakilese. Sabbatthikanti sabbattha līne, uddhate ca citte icchitabbatthā, sabbe vā līne, uddhate ca bhāvetabbā bojjhaṅgā atthikā etāyāti sabbatthikā. Anto saṅkocoti anto olīyanā, kosajjanti attho.

'Defilements in the three existences' means defilements having the three realms of existence as their domain. 'Sabbatthikā' means that which is needed in all circumstances: objects to be desired in a mind that is sluggish or restless. Or, because all the enlightenment factors to be developed in a sluggish or restless mind are needed by it, it is 'sabbatthikā.' 'Internal contraction' means internal shrinking; the meaning is sloth.

24. Gahitesūti bhāvanāggahaṇena gahitesu, bhāvitesūti attho, vacanena vā gahitesu. Bhāvanāggahaṇadīpanatthattā pana vacanena gahaṇassa bhāvanāggahaṇamettha padhānaṃ. Yassa satipaṭṭhānā bhāvitā, tassa sammappadhānādayo bodhipakkhiyadhammā na bhāvitāti netaṃ ṭhānaṃ vijjatīti ca samānalakkhaṇatāpadesena imamatthaṃ dassetuṃ pāḷiyaṃ “catūsu satipaṭṭhānesu bhāviyamānesu cattāro sammappadhānā bhāvanāpāripūriṃ gacchantī”tiādi vuttaṃ.

24. 'Gahitesu' means taken up through cultivation, that is, developed; or, taken up through recitation. However, since the purpose is to clarify the taking up through cultivation, the taking up through cultivation is primary here. For one who has developed the foundations of mindfulness, it is impossible that the factors of enlightenment such as the right efforts remain undeveloped. To show this meaning by way of indicating their similar characteristics, the Pāli text states: 'When the four foundations of mindfulness are being developed, the four right efforts reach the fulfillment of development,' etc.

Vipallāsā [V 0.0060] pahīyanti ujuvipaccanīkabhāvato. “Āhārasamudayā kāyassa samudayo, phassasamudayā vedanānaṃ samudayo (saṃ. ni. 5.408), saṅkhārapaccayā viññāṇaṃ, viññāṇapaccayā nāmarūpa”nti (saṃ. ni. 1.1, 39; ma. ni. 3.126; mahāva. 1; udā. 1; vibha. 225) vacanato kāyādīnaṃ samudayabhūtā kabaḷīkārāhāraphassamanosañcetanāviññāṇāhārākāyādīnaṃ parijānanena pariññātā honti tappaṭipakkhappahānatoti dassento “cattāro [M 0.0064] āhārā”tiādimāha. Sabbatthāti “upādānehi anupādāno bhavatī”ti evamādīsu.

Distortions are abandoned because they are the direct opposite of what is right. By the statement, 'With the arising of nutriment, the body arises; with the arising of contact, feelings arise (Saṃ. Ni. 5.408); with formations as condition, consciousness; with consciousness as condition, name-and-form' (Saṃ. Ni. 1.1, 39; Ma. Ni. 3.126; Mahāva. 1; Udā. 1; Vibha. 225), he shows that the nutriments—physical food, contact, mental volition, and consciousness—which are the origin of the body and so forth, are fully understood by fully understanding the body and so forth, through the abandonment of their opposites, and says, 'the four nutriments,' and so on. 'In all cases' refers to such statements as 'by means of the clingings, one becomes without clinging,' and so forth.

Tattha yasmā pañca kāmaguṇā savisesā kāye labbhantīti visesena kāyo kāmupādānassa vatthu, sukhavedanassādavasena paralokanirapekkho “natthi dinna”ntiādi (dī. ni. 1.171; ma. ni. 1.445; 2.94-95, 225; 3.91, 116; saṃ. ni. 3.210; dha. sa. 1221; vibha. 938) parāmāsaṃ uppādetīti diṭṭhupādānassa vedanā, citte niccaggahaṇavasena sassatassa “attano sīlādivasena parisuddhaparāmasanaṃ hotī”ti sīlabbatupādānassa cittaṃ, nāmarūpaparicchedena bhūtaṃ bhūtato apassantassa “attābhiniveso hotī”ti attavādupādānassa dhammā vatthu, tasmā “catūsu satipaṭṭhānesu bhāviyamānesu upādānehi anupādāno bhavatī”ti vuttaṃ.

Herein, because the five strands of sensual pleasure are especially obtained in the body, the body is the basis for clinging to sensual pleasures. Due to relishing pleasant feeling, being heedless of the next world, one generates the adherence, 'There is no fruit of giving,' etc. (Dī. Ni. 1.171; Ma. Ni. 1.445; 2.94–95, 225; 3.91, 116; Saṃ. Ni. 3.210; Dha. Sa. 1221; Vibha. 938); thus feeling is the basis for clinging to views. By grasping the mind as permanent, an eternalist has the adherence that 'there is a grasping at purification through one’s own virtue, etc.'; thus the mind is the basis for clinging to rites and rituals. For one who does not see what has come to be as it has come to be by delimiting name-and-form, 'adherence to self arises'; thus phenomena are the basis for clinging to a doctrine of self. Therefore, it is said: 'When the four foundations of mindfulness are developed, one becomes without clinging by means of the clingings.'

Yasmā pana vuttanayeneva kāyo kāmayogassa vatthu, bhavesu sukhaggahaṇavasena bhavassādo hotīti bhavayogassa vedanā, santatighanaggahaṇavasena citte attābhiniveso hotīti diṭṭhiyogassacittaṃ, dhammavinibbhogassa dukkarattā, dhammānaṃ dhammamattatāya ca duppaṭivijjhattā sammoho hotīti avijjāyogassa dhammā, vatthu, tasmā catusatipaṭṭhānabhāvanāya tesu tesaṃ pahānasiddhito yogehi visaṃyuttatā vuttā. Eteneva āsavehi anāsavatā, oghehi nittiṇṇatā ca saṃvaṇṇitā hoti kāmarāgādīnaṃ eva kāmayogakāmāsavakāmoghādibhāvato.

Moreover, in the way already stated, the body is the basis for the bond of sensuality. Because there is enjoyment of existence by way of grasping pleasure in existences, feeling is the basis for the bond of existence. Because adherence to self arises in the mind by way of grasping it as a solid continuity, the mind is the basis for the bond of views. Because delusion arises due to the difficulty of analyzing phenomena and the difficulty of penetrating their mere phenomenality, phenomena are the basis for the bond of ignorance. Therefore, because their abandonment is accomplished in these through the development of the four foundations of mindfulness, disengagement from the bonds is spoken of. By this same means, freedom from the taints and having crossed over the floods are also explained, since sensual lust and so on are themselves the bond of sensuality, the taint of sensuality, the flood of sensuality, and so on.

Vuttanayeneva kāyo abhijjhākāyaganthassa vatthu, “dukkhāya vedanāya paṭighānusayo anusetī”ti (ma. ni. 1.465) dukkhadukkhavipariṇāmadukkhasaṅkhāradukkhabhūtā vedanā visesena byāpādakāyaganthassa vatthu, citte niccābhinivesavasena sassatassa “attano sīlena suddhī”tiādiparāmasanaṃ hotīti sīlabbataparāmāsassa cittaṃ vatthu, sappaccayanāmarūpadassanābhāvato bhavavibhavadiṭṭhisaṅkhāto idaṃsaccābhiniveso hotīti tassa dhammā vatthūti catusatipaṭṭhānāti yojetabbaṃ.

In the way already stated, the body is the basis for the bodily bond of covetousness. Because 'the underlying tendency to aversion lies latent in painful feeling' (MN 1.465), feeling—which is suffering as suffering, suffering due to change, and suffering of conditioned formations—is especially the basis for the bodily bond of ill will. Because for an eternalist, due to adherence to permanence in the mind, there arises the adherence to such things as 'purity is through one's own virtue,' the mind is the basis for the bodily bond of adherence to rites and rituals. Because of the absence of seeing name-and-form with its conditions, there arises adherence to this as truth, which is reckoned as the view of existence and non-existence; for this bond, phenomena are the basis. This should be connected to the four foundations of mindfulness.

Vuttanayeneva [V 0.0061][M 0.0065] visesato kāyo rāgasallassa vatthu, vedanā dosasallassa, “cittaṃ niccaggahaṇavasena attābhinivesaṃ attānaṃ seyyādito dahatī”ti cittaṃ mānasallassa, vuttanayeneva dhammā mohasallassa vatthūti catusatipaṭṭhānāti yojetabbaṃ.

In the way already stated, the body is specifically the basis for the dart of lust, and feeling is the basis for the dart of hatred. Because 'the mind, by way of grasping permanence, burns oneself with adherence to self by considering oneself superior, etc.,' the mind is the basis for the dart of conceit. In the way already stated, phenomena are the basis for the dart of delusion. This should be connected to the four foundations of mindfulness.

Yasmā pana kāyānupassanādīhi kāyavedanācittadhammesu pariññātesu rūpavedanāsaññāsaṅkhārakkhandhā pariññātā honti, citte hi pariññāte saññāpi pariññātāva hoti, tasmā “viññāṇaṭṭhitiyo cassa pariññaṃ gacchantī”ti vuttaṃ.

Because when the body, feeling, mind, and phenomena are fully understood through contemplation of the body and so forth, the aggregates of form, feeling, perception, and formations are also fully understood; for when the mind is fully understood, perception is likewise fully understood. Therefore, it is said: 'And his stations of consciousness go to full understanding.'

Tathā visesato kāye sāpekkhā chandāgatiṃ gacchatīti kāyo chandāgatiyā vatthu, vuttanayeneva vedanā byāpādassa nimittanti sā dosāgatiyā vatthu, santatighanaggahaṇavasena sarāgādicitte sammoho hotīti mohāgatiyā cittaṃ, dhammasabhāvānavabodhena bhayaṃ hotīti bhayāgatiyā dhammā vatthūti catusatipaṭṭhānabhāvanāya agatigamanappahānaṃ hotīti āha “agatigamanehi ca na agatiṃ gacchatī”ti.

Likewise, because one, having longing for the body, goes the biased course of desire, the body is the basis for bias due to desire. In the way already stated, because feeling is the sign of ill will, it is the basis for bias due to hatred. Because delusion arises in a mind affected by lust, etc., through grasping it as a solid continuity, the mind is the basis for bias due to delusion. Because fear arises from not understanding the intrinsic nature of phenomena, phenomena are the basis for bias due to fear. He says that through the development of the four foundations of mindfulness there is the abandonment of going on biased courses, stating: 'And he does not go a biased course by way of the biased courses.'

“Akusalassa somanassassa vasenā”ti idaṃ “ayampi attho sambhavatī”ti katvā vuttaṃ. “Sukhāya vedanāya rāgānusayo anusetī”ti (ma. ni. 1.465) pana vacanato sukhavedanāggahaṇena tatthānusayanena samudayasaccaṃ desitanti veditabbaṃ. Desitaṃ dukkhaṃ ariyasaccanti dukkhadukkhaggahaṇena sātisayaṃ dukkhaṃ ariyasaccaṃ pakāsitaṃ hotīti pāḷiyaṃ “dukkhaṃ ariyasaccaṃ desita”nti vuttaṃ. Sahacaraṇādīsu yaṃ vattabbaṃ, taṃ heṭṭhā niddesavāravaṇṇanāyaṃ (netti. aṭṭha. 5 ādayo) vuttaṃ.

This—'by means of unwholesome joy'—was said considering that 'this meaning is also possible.' However, from the statement, 'The underlying tendency to lust lies latent in pleasant feeling' (MN 1.465), it should be understood that by the grasping of pleasant feeling, the truth of the origin is taught through the underlying tendency therein. 'The noble truth of suffering has been taught' is said in the Pāli because by grasping suffering-as-suffering, the noble truth of suffering is revealed superlatively. What should be said regarding co-arising and so on has been explained previously in the commentary on the section of exposition (Nettippakaraṇa Aṭṭhakathā 5, etc.).

Lakkhaṇahāravibhaṅgavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Analysis of the Conveyor of Characteristics is concluded.

6. Catubyūhahāravibhaṅgavaṇṇanā

6. The Commentary on the Analysis of the Conveyor of the Fourfold Array

25. Hārānanti niddhāraṇe sāmivacanaṃ. Hāresu imassa catubyūhahārassa visesato suttassa byañjanavicayabhāvatoti yojanā. Tena vuttaṃ “byañjana…pe… dassetī”ti. Yāyāti niruttiyā.

25. The word `hārānaṃ` is a genitive of specification. The construction is that among the conveyors (`hāresu`), this conveyor of the fourfold array is specifically an investigation into the phrasing of a sutta. Therefore it was said: 'phrasing... pe... it shows.' The word `yāyā` is by way of etymology.

Yathārahanti [V 0.0062][M 0.0066] saṃvaṇṇiyamāne sutte yaṃ yaṃ arahati nibbacanaṃ vattuṃ, taṃtaṃlokasamaññānurodheneva. Pubbabhāgapaṭipadā sampādetvā pacchā saccābhisamayaṃ pāpuṇātīti āha “sammuti…pe… hotī”ti, taṃtaṃpaññattiggahaṇamukhena paramatthaggahaṇaṃ hotīti evaṃ vā iminā sambandho.

'As is fitting' means: when a sutta is being explained, whatever interpretation is appropriate to state is stated in accordance with worldly convention. One accomplishes the preliminary practice and later attains the penetration of the truths—thus it is said, 'by convention... pe... it is.' Or, the connection here is thus: by means of grasping the various designations, there is the grasping of the ultimate meaning.

Yamidaṃ anindriyabaddharūpasantānaṃ sandhāya “ubhayamantarenā”ti idha vuttaṃ. Otaraṇahāre (netti. aṭṭha. 42 ādayo) panassa dvārappavattaphassādidhamme sandhāya vuttabhāvaṃ dassetuṃ “ubhayamantarenāti phassasamuditesu dhammesū”ti attho vutto. Aṭṭhakathācariyā panāhu “antarenāti vacanaṃ pana vikappantaradīpana”nti. Tasmā ayamettha attho – na imaṃ lokaṃ, na huraṃ lokaṃ, atha kho ubhayamantarenāti. Aparo vikappo – ubhayamantarenāti vā vacanaṃ vikappantarābhāvadīpanaṃ. Tassattho – na imaṃ lokaṃ, na huraṃ lokaṃ nissāya jhāyati jhāyī, ubhayamantarena pana aññaṃ ṭhānaṃ atthīti.

This which is stated here, 'between both,' is with reference to the continuum of form not bound to the sense faculties. In the Otaraṇahāra (Netti. Aṭṭha. 42 ff.), however, to show that this was stated with reference to phenomena such as contact occurring at the sense doors, the meaning is given as: '“Between both” means among phenomena arisen from contact.' But the commentary masters say: 'The word “between,” however, indicates another alternative.' Therefore, the meaning here is: not this world, not the other world, but rather between both. Another alternative: or, the expression 'between both' indicates the absence of another alternative. Its meaning is: the meditator does not meditate relying on this world or the other world; but between the two there is another state.

Yepi ca “antarāparinibbāyī, sambhavesī”ti ca imesaṃ suttapadānaṃ atthaṃ micchā gahetvā atthi eva antarābhavoti vadanti, tepi yasmā avihādīsu tattha tattha āyuvemajjhaṃ anatikkamitvā antarā aggamaggādhigamena anavasesakilesaparinibbānena parinibbāyantīti antarāparinibbāyī, na antarābhavabhūtoti purimassa suttapadassa attho. Pacchimassa ca ye bhūtā eva, na puna bhavissanti, te hi (kathā. anuṭī. 507) khīṇāsavā, purimapadehi “bhūtā”ti vuttā. Tabbiparītatāya sambhavaṃ esantīti sambhavesino. Appahīnabhavasaṃyojanattā sekkhā, puthujjanā ca. Catūsu vā yonīsu aṇḍajajalābujasattā yāva aṇḍakosaṃ, vatthikosañca na bhindanti, tāva sambhavesī nāma. Aṇḍakosato, vatthikosato ca bahi nikkhantā bhūtā nāma. Saṃsedajaopapātikā ca paṭhamacittakkhaṇe sambhavesī nāma, dutiyacittakkhaṇato paṭṭhāya bhūtā nāma. Yena vā iriyāpathena jāyanti, yāva tato aññaṃ na pāpuṇanti, tāva sambhavesī, tato paraṃ bhūtāti attho, tasmā natthīti paṭikkhipitabbaṃ. Sati hi ujuke pāḷianugate atthe kiṃ aniddhāritasāmatthiyena antarābhavena attabhāvaparikappitena payojananti.

And as for those who, having wrongly grasped the meaning of the sutta words antarāparinibbāyī (one who attains parinibbāna in the interval) and sambhavesī (one seeking to become), say that an intermediate state does indeed exist, they too should be refuted. For the meaning of the first sutta word is this: one is an antarāparinibbāyī because, while in the Aviha realm and so on, without transgressing the midpoint of the lifespan, one attains parinibbāna in the interval through the attainment of the supreme path and the parinibbāna of the defilements without remainder; it does not mean one is a being in an intermediate state. As for the latter word: those who are 'beings' (bhūtā) and will not come to be again are those whose taints are destroyed, called 'beings' in the preceding words. In contrast, those who seek becoming (sambhavaṃ esanti) are 'seekers of becoming' (sambhavesino); they are trainees and worldlings because they have not abandoned the fetter of existence. Alternatively, among the four kinds of birth, egg-born and womb-born beings are called 'seekers of becoming' as long as they have not broken the eggshell or the womb; once they have emerged from the eggshell or the womb, they are called 'beings.' Moisture-born and spontaneously arisen beings are called 'seekers of becoming' at the first moment of consciousness, but from the second moment of consciousness onwards they are called 'beings.' Or, as long as they have not changed from the posture in which they were born, they are 'seekers of becoming'; after that, they are 'beings.' Therefore, it must be rejected, saying, 'It does not exist.' For when there is a straightforward meaning that accords with the Pāli canon, what is the use of a self-conceived intermediate existence whose capability has not been ascertained?

Yaṃ [M 0.0067] pana ye “santānavasena pavattamānānaṃ dhammānaṃ avicchedena desantaresu pātubhāvo diṭṭho. Yathā taṃ vīhiādiaviññāṇakasantāne, evaṃ saviññāṇakasantānepi avicchedena desantaresu pātubhāvena bhavitabbaṃ. Ayañca nayo sati antarābhave yujjati, nāññathā”ti yuttiṃ [V 0.0063] vadanti. Tehi iddhimato cetovasippattassa cittānugatikaṃ kāyaṃ adhiṭṭhahantassa khaṇena brahmalokato idhūpasaṅkamane, ito vā brahmalokagamane yutti vattabbā. Yadi sabbattheva vicchinnadese dhammānaṃ pavatti na icchitā, yadipi siyā “iddhivisayo acinteyyo”ti, taṃ idhāpi samānaṃ “kammavipāko acinteyyo”ti vacanato, tasmā taṃ tesaṃ matimattameva. Acinteyyasabhāvā hi sabhāvadhammā, te katthaci paccayavisesena vicchinnadese pātubhavanti, katthaci avicchinnadese ca. Tathā hi mukhaghosādīhi aññasmiṃ dese ādāsapabbatappadesādike paṭibimbapaṭighosādikaṃ paccayuppannaṃ nibbattamānaṃ dissati, tasmā na sabbaṃ sabbattha upanetabbanti ayamettha saṅkhepo. Vitthārato pana paṭibimbassa udāharaṇabhāvasādhanādiko antarābhavavicāro kathāvatthuppakaraṇassaṭīkāyaṃ (kathā. anuṭī. 507) gahetabbo.

As for those who advance this argument: 'The manifestation of phenomena that proceed by way of a continuum is seen to occur without interruption in other locations. Just as this is so in the case of the continuum of non-conscious things like rice, so too in the case of a conscious continuum there must be manifestation without interruption in other locations. And this principle is tenable if there is an intermediate state, but not otherwise.' They should be asked to state their reasoning regarding one with psychic power who has attained mastery over the mind, who, having resolved upon a body that follows the mind, can in an instant come here from the Brahmā world or go from here to the Brahmā world. If the occurrence of phenomena in a disconnected location is not accepted at all, even if one should say, 'The domain of psychic power is inconceivable,' the same applies here too, according to the saying, 'The result of kamma is inconceivable.' Therefore, that is merely their opinion. For intrinsic phenomena are inconceivable by nature; due to specific conditions they sometimes arise in a disconnected location and sometimes in an uninterrupted one. Thus, a reflection or an echo, produced by conditions such as the sound from the mouth, is seen to arise in another place, such as on the surface of a mirror or a mountain. Therefore, not everything can be applied to everything. This is the summary here. For a detailed treatment, the discussion of the intermediate state, including the demonstration of the reflection as an illustration, should be consulted in the Subcommentary to the Kathāvatthu.

Apare pana “idhāti kāmabhavo, huranti arūpabhavo, ubhayamantarenāti rūpabhavo vutto”ti vadanti, “idhāti paccayadhammā, huranti paccayuppannadhammā, ubhayamantarenāti paṇṇattidhammā vuttā”ti ca vadanti, taṃ sabbaaṭṭhakathāsu natthi, tasmā vuttanayeneva attho veditabbo. Avasiṭṭhaṃ rūpanti āpodhātuākāsadhātūhi saddhiṃ lakkhaṇarūpāni, ojañca sandhāyāha anindriyabaddharūpassa adhippetattā. Tassa khīṇāsavassa taṃ nibbānārammaṇaṃ cittaṃ na jānanti na ñāyanti “jhāyamānā”ti vuttattā. Sesaṃ suviññeyyameva.

But others say: '“Here” means the sense-sphere existence, “yonder” means the formless existence, and “between both” means the fine-material existence.' And they also say: '“Here” means conditions, “yonder” means conditionally arisen phenomena, and “between both” means concepts.' This is found in none of the commentaries; therefore, the meaning should be understood only in the way stated. 'The remaining form': this is said with reference to the characteristic forms along with the water element and the space element, and nutriment, because what is intended is form not bound to the sense faculties. Because it is said 'meditating,' they do not know or understand that mind of the one whose taints are destroyed, which has Nibbāna as its object. The rest is easy to understand.

Catubyūhahāravibhaṅgavaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The Commentary on the Exposition of the Fourfold Array is concluded.

7. Āvaṭṭahāravibhaṅgavaṇṇanā

7. Commentary on the Analysis of the Mode of Turning Back

29. Akusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ viddhaṃsanasabhāvattā, akusalānaṃ vā pajahane kusalānaṃ sampādane paṭṭhapanasabhāvattā ārambhadhātu. Tathābhūtāti sīlādīhi [M 0.0068] samaṅgībhūtā. Kāmadhātuādikā tidhātuyova tedhātu. Tassa abhibhavanato tedhātuissaro maccurājā. Anādimatisaṃsāre cirakālaṃ laddhapatiṭṭhāpi acirakālaṃ bhāvitehi kusalehi dhammehi samucchindanīyattā abalā kilesāti vuttaṃ “abalaṃ dubbala”nti. Tenāha “abalā naṃ balīyantī”ti.

29. Because it has the nature of destroying unwholesome states, or because it has the nature of establishing the abandoning of the unwholesome and the accomplishment of the wholesome, it is the element of initiative. 'Being thus' means being endowed with morality and so on. The three realms, beginning with the sensuous realm, are indeed the three realms. The king of death is the lord of these three realms because he overcomes them. Though the defilements have long been established in beginningless saṃsāra, they are weak because they can be eradicated by wholesome states developed in a short time; thus it is said, 'weak and feeble.' Therefore, it is stated, 'The weak are indeed overpowered.'

Idaṃ vacanaṃ ayaṃ gāthāpādo. Samādhissa padaṭṭhānanti ettha samādhissa kāraṇaṃ samatānuyoge niyojanatoti [V 0.0064] yojetabbaṃ. Esa nayo sesesupi. Papañcāti rāgādayova. Tathā ceva saṃvaṇṇitanti desanāya padaṭṭhānabhāveneva atthasaṃvaṇṇanā katāti attho.

This statement is this verse line. 'The foundation of concentration'—here, it should be connected as 'the cause of concentration is the application to balanced effort.' This method applies to the rest as well. 'Proliferation' means lust and so on. 'In this way, it is explained' means the explanation of the meaning has been done precisely by being the foundation of the teaching.

Nti taṃ desanaṃ. Tassāti sabhāgādivasena āvaṭṭanassa. Paripakkañāṇānaṃ visesādhigamāya. Lābhavinicchayapariggahamacchariyānītiādīsu lābhoti rūpādiārammaṇappaṭilābho. So pariyesanāya sati hotīti pariyesanāggahaṇena gahito. Vinicchayoti “ettakaṃ me rūpārammaṇatthāya bhavissati, ettakaṃ saddādiārammaṇatthāya, ettakaṃ mayhaṃ, ettakaṃ parassa, ettakaṃ paribhuñjissāmi, ettakaṃ nidahissāmī”ti evaṃ pavatto vitakko vinicchayo. So lāpitahetukattā pariyesanamūlakatāya pariyesanāggahaṇeneva gahito, tathā pariggahamacchariyāni. Tattha pariggaho “mama ida”nti pariggaṇhanaṃ. Macchariyaṃ “mayheva hotū”ti parehi sādhāraṇabhāvāsahanaṃ. Tenevassa porāṇā evaṃ vacanatthaṃ vadanti “mayhevidamacchariyaṃ hotu, mā aññesaṃ acchariyaṃ hotūti pavattattā macchariyanti vuccatī”ti (dī. ni. aṭṭha. 2.103; a. ni. aṭṭha. 3.9.23). Paribhogatthānaṃ pana vinicchayādīnaṃ paribhogantogadhatā veditabbā. Chandarāgo dubbalarāgo. Ajjhosānaṃ “mama ida”nti taṇhāvasena balavasanniṭṭhānanti āha “chandarāgaajjhosānā taṇhā evā”ti. Ārakkhanimittaṃ dvārapidahanamañjūsāgopanādinā suṭṭhu rakkhaṇanimittaṃ. Pāpāni karonto paribhoganimittaṃ ratto giddho gadhito mucchito hutvā migova paribhuñjananimittaṃ pamādaṃ āpajjatīti evaṃ pariyenārakkhā paribhoganimittaṃ. Pamādo tividho taṇhāya vasena kathitoti dassento “tividho taṇhāyāti vutta”nti āha.

‘N’ refers to that teaching. ‘Of that’ refers to the turning back by way of similarity and so on. This is for the attainment of distinction by those with fully matured knowledge. In phrases such as ‘acquisition, decision-making, possessiveness, and stinginess,’ ‘acquisition’ refers to the obtaining of objects such as forms. This occurs when there is seeking, and thus it is included by the term ‘seeking.’ ‘Decision-making’ is the thought process that proceeds thus: ‘This much will be for the object of form, this much for the objects of sound and so on, this much for me, this much for others, this much I will use, this much I will store.’ Because it is caused by what is obtained and is rooted in seeking, it is included by the term ‘seeking.’ Similarly with possessiveness and stinginess. There, ‘possessiveness’ is the grasping, ‘This is mine.’ ‘Stinginess’ is the inability to tolerate sharing with others, thinking, ‘Let it be mine alone.’ Therefore, the ancients explain the meaning of the word thus: ‘Because it proceeds as “Let this wonder be mine, let it not be a wonder for others,” it is called stinginess.’ However, decision-making and so on, when for the purpose of enjoyment, should be understood as included within ‘enjoyment.’ ‘Desire and lust’ is a weaker form of lust. ‘Clinging’ is the strong determination through craving, thinking, ‘This is mine.’ Thus, it is said, ‘desire, lust, and clinging are indeed craving.’ The sign of guarding is the means of thorough protection, such as locking doors, protecting chests, and so on. One who does evil, on account of enjoyment, being attached, greedy, infatuated, and deluded, like a deer, falls into heedlessness. Thus, heedlessness is threefold by way of seeking, guarding, and enjoyment. To show that it is stated as ‘threefold by way of craving,’ it is said, ‘it is stated as threefold by way of craving.’

Avisesena vuttanti “katamena upādānena saupādānā”ti vibhāgena pucchitvāpi “avijjāya ca taṇhāya cā”ti avinibbhujitvā vuttaṃ. Taṇhañca avijjañca caturupādānaṃ [M 0.0069] vasenāti kāmupādānādīnaṃ catunnaṃ upādānānaṃ vasena vibhajitvā khandhānaṃ dukkhabhāvena dukkhasaccabhāvena saha pariññeyyabhāvaṃ, upādānānaṃ samudayabhāvena samudayasaccabhāvena saha pahātabbabhāvaṃ dassetīti yojanā.

‘It is said without distinction’ means that even when asked with a distinction, ‘By what clinging are the aggregates subject to clinging?,’ it is stated without differentiating, ‘By ignorance and by craving.’ The construction is as follows: ‘By distinguishing craving and ignorance by way of the four clingings—that is, by way of the four kinds of clinging beginning with sensual clinging—it shows the characteristic of suffering in the aggregates, along with their nature as the truth of suffering and what should be fully understood; and the characteristic of origination in the clingings, along with their nature as the truth of origination and what should be abandoned.’

30. “Yo”tiādinā vutto tividho pamādo pariyesati, ārakkhaṇañca karoti, paribhoganimittañcāti sambandho. Pamādo hi pamajjantassa puggalassa bhogānaṃ pariyesanāya, ārakkhaṇāya ca hetubhūto kattubhāvena upacarito, paribhogassa pana nimittaṃ. “Tappaṭipakkhenā”ti padassa atthaṃ vivarati “appamādānuyogenā”ti, tena samathabhāvaṃ dasseti[V 0.0065]. Khepanāti khayapāpanā. Vodānapakkhavisabhāgadhammavasenāti vodānapakkho ca so pamādassa visabhāgadhammo cāti vodāna…pe… dhammo, samatho, tassa vasena.

30. The connection is that the threefold heedlessness, stated by ‘who’ and so on, seeks, guards, and is the condition for enjoyment. Indeed, heedlessness is figuratively spoken of as the agent, being the cause for a heedless person in seeking and guarding possessions, while it is the condition for enjoyment.
