Now, to explain in detail that very meaning stated in brief, it is said, 'For from the first method,' etc. There, having divided persons into two kinds based on their behavior of craving and views, and having drawn out the Nandiyāvaṭṭa Method based on them, then those same persons with craving and views are further divided by the fourfold classification of practices, and the possibility of the Sīhavikkīḷita Method is shown. Those same persons, distinguished by the fourfold division of practices, are again divided into three kinds by the classification of quick-understanding, etc., and the possibility of the Tipukkhala Method is shown. Referring to this, it is said, 'From the first method... is pointed out'.

Yasmā subhasukhasaññāhi lobho, niccasaññāya doso “iminā me anattho kato”ti āghātuppattito[V 0.0115], attasaññāya moho gahito hoti. Tathā asubhasaññādīhi alobhādayo, tasmā dhammādhiṭṭhānavasena tatiyanayato dutiyanayassa sambhavo. Yasmā pana lobhe sati sambhavato lobhaggahaṇeneva doso gayhati. Lobho ca taṇhā, moho avijjā, tappaṭipakkhato alobhādosehi samatho gayhati, amohena vipassanā, tasmā dhammādhiṭṭhānavaseneva dutiyanayato paṭhamanayassa sambhavoti imaṃ visesaṃ dīpetuṃ uddesānukkamena niddeso na katoti dassento “dhammādhiṭṭhānavasena panā”tiādimāha.

Because greed is grasped through perceptions of beauty and pleasure; hatred through perceptions of permanence, arising from the thought 'This harm has been done to me'; and delusion through perceptions of self. Likewise, non-greed, etc., are grasped through perceptions of ugliness, etc. Therefore, the arising of the second method from the third method is through the basis of phenomena. But because, when greed exists, hatred is consequently grasped by the very grasping of greed—and greed is craving, delusion is ignorance—from their opposites, serenity is grasped by non-greed and non-hatred, and insight by non-delusion. Therefore, since the first method arises from the second through the basis of phenomena, to explain this distinction and to show that 'the exposition is not made in the order of enumeration,' he begins with 'But through the basis of phenomena...'.

Tenevāti tatiyanayato dutiyanayassa viya dutiyanayato paṭhamanayassapi sambhavato. Evaṃ pāḷiyaṃ puggalādhiṭṭhānavasena āgataṃ nissāya aṭṭhakathāyaṃ dhammādhiṭṭhānavaseneva nayaniggamo niddhāritoti ayameva viseso. Yadi evanti pāḷiyaṃ āgatappakārato aññenapi pakārena nayā niddhāretabbā, evaṃ sante yathā puggalādhiṭṭhānavasena paṭhamanayato tatiyanayassa, tatiyanayato dutiyanayassa [M 0.0122] sambhavo dassito, evaṃ dhammādhiṭṭhānavaseneva paṭhamanayato tatiyanayadutiyanayānaṃ, dhammādhiṭṭhānavaseneva dutiyanayato tatiyanayassa sambhavo dīpetabboti imamatthamāha “dve hutvā…pe… siyā”ti.

'Therefore' means because of the possibility of the first method arising from the second method, just as the second method arises from the third method. Thus, relying on what is presented in the Pāli texts based on the standpoint of individuals, the commentary determines the derivation of the methods based on the standpoint of the Dhamma itself—this is the distinction. If it is so—that methods should be determined in another way than that presented in the Pāli texts—then, just as the arising of the third method from the first method and the second method from the third method is shown based on the standpoint of individuals, so too, based on the standpoint of the Dhamma itself, the arising of the third and second methods from the first method, and based on the standpoint of the Dhamma itself, the arising of the third method from the second method should be elucidated. He states this meaning with 'Being two... it may be'.

Tattha nayoti pacchā vuttadutiyanayo. Atthatoti atthāpattito, atthato labbhamānattā eva sarūpena na kathitoti attho. Idāni taṃ atthāpattiṃ ekantikaṃ katvā dassetuṃ “yasmā”tiādi vuttaṃ. Anuppaveso icchito taṃtaṃnayamūlapadānaṃ nayantaramūlapadesu samavarujjhanato. Tathā hi “yattha sabbo akusalapakkho saṅgahaṃ samosaraṇaṃ gacchati, yattha sabbo kusalapakkho saṅgahaṃ samosaraṇaṃ gacchatī”ti (netti. 3) ca vuttaṃ. Ayañca atthoti “nayānaṃ aññamaññaanuppaveso niggamo”ti ayaṃ duvidho attho. Piṭakānaṃ atthakathanaṃ peṭakaṃ, so eva upadesoti peṭakopadeso, upadesabhūtā pariyattisaṃvaṇṇanāti attho.

Here, 'method' refers to the second method mentioned later. 'In meaning' refers to the implication from meaning; the meaning is that it is not spoken of in its own form because it is obtained from the meaning itself. Now, to show that implication of meaning as definitive, 'Because...' etc., is stated. Entry is desired because the root terms of each respective method are included in the root terms of other methods. For it is said: 'Where all the unwholesome division gathers and converges, where all the wholesome division gathers and converges' (Netti. 3). And this is the meaning: 'The mutual entry and derivation of the methods'—this is the twofold meaning. The explanation of the Piṭakas is the Peṭaka; that itself is the instruction, hence Peṭakopadesa, meaning the description of the scriptures which is instructional.

Ādito paṭṭhāyāti nayānaṃ aññamaññaanuppavesaniggamamattameva avibhāvetvā nayavicārassa paṭhamāvayavato pabhuti vibhāvanā dīpanā pakāsanā.

'From the beginning' means that, without explaining merely the mutual entry and derivation of the methods, the clarification, elucidation, and exposition proceed from the first component of the consideration of methods.

Dosadiṭṭhīti appassādatādidosagāhikadiṭṭhī, dosadassinoti attho. Te hi asamūhatānusayā, kāmesu ca ādīnavadassino. Idañhi nesaṃ aṅgadvayaṃ attakilamathānuyogassa kāraṇaṃ vuttaṃ. Natthi atthoti yo rāgābhibhūtehi andhabālehi parikappito diṭṭhadhammiko kāmehi attho, so madhubindugiddhassa madhulittasatthadhārāvalehanasadiso appassādo bahudukkho bahupāyāso bahuādīnavo [V 0.0116] savighāto sapariḷāho samparāyiko tathevāti sabbadāpi viññūjātikassa kāmehi payojanaṃ na vijjati. Anajjhositāti anabhibhūtā viharanti. Tena vuccati sukhā paṭipadāti tena mandakilesabhāvena tesaṃ puggalānaṃ akicchena sijjhamānā vipassanā paṭipadā “sukhā paṭipadā”ti vuccati. Ajjhositāti abhiniviṭṭhā. Ime sabbe sattāti ime taṇhādiṭṭhicaritabhāvena dvidhā vuttā aparimāṇappabhedā sabbepi paṭipajjantā sattā.

'View of aversion' means a view that grasps faults such as lack of gratification; the meaning is 'one who sees fault'. For they have latent tendencies that are not uprooted, and they are seers of danger in sensual pleasures. Indeed, these two factors are stated as the cause of the practice of self-mortification. 'There is no benefit' refers to the benefit in this life from sensual pleasures imagined by those blinded and foolish, overpowered by lust; that benefit is like licking a drop of honey from the edge of a sword—it offers little satisfaction, much suffering, much distress, many dangers, is full of vexation and torment, and likewise in the life to come. Thus, for one of a discerning nature, there is never any purpose in sensual pleasures. 'They are not attached' means they dwell un-overcome. Therefore, it is called 'the pleasant practice'—because of their weak defilements, the insight practice that succeeds without difficulty for such individuals is called 'the pleasant practice'. 'Attached' means 'deeply committed'. 'All these beings' refers to all beings, of countless variety, who proceed in two ways, as stated, through the conduct of craving and views.

Sukhena [M 0.0123] paṭinissajjantīti kilese akicchena pajahanti. “Imā catasso paṭipadā”tiādi paṭipadānaṃ ettāvatāyaṃ, visayabhāvakiccesu ca byabhicārābhāvadassanaṃ. Ayaṃ paṭipadāti nigamanaṃ, ayaṃ paṭipadā yāya vasena sīhavikkīḷitassa nayassa bhūmidassanatthaṃ cattāro puggalā niddhāritāti adhippāyo. Catukkamaggena kilese niddisatīti anantaraṃ vakkhamānena āhārādicatukkamaggena dasavatthuke kilesasamūhe niddisati. Catukkamaggena ariyadhammesu niddisitabbāti tappaṭipakkhena paṭipadādicatukkamaggena ariyadhammesu bodhipakkhiyesu visayabhūtesu niddhāretvā kathetabbā.

'They relinquish with ease' means they abandon defilements without difficulty. 'These four practices,' etc.—this is the extent of the practices, and it demonstrates the absence of deviation in their functions regarding their objects. 'This is the practice' is the conclusion; the intent is: 'This is the practice by which four types of individuals are distinguished for the purpose of showing the ground of the Sīhavikkīḷita Method.' 'He indicates the defilements by the fourfold way' means that, by the fourfold way of nutriment and so on which will be explained immediately after, he indicates the group of defilements based on ten items. 'They should be indicated in the noble qualities by the fourfold way' means that, by the opposing fourfold way of practice and so on, they should be determined and explained in relation to the noble qualities, which are the factors of enlightenment that have become the objects.

Idañca pamāṇaṃ cattāro āhārāti imesaṃ vipallāsānaṃ pavattiyā pamāṇaṃ, yadidaṃ cattāro āhārā. Idaṃ vuttaṃ hoti – yāvadeva cattāro āhārā pariññaṃ na gacchanti, tāvadeva cattāro vipallāse vibhajanti. Yāvadeva cattāro vipallāsā appahīnā, tāvadeva cattāri upādānāni paribrūhantīti. Evaṃ sabbattha yathārahaṃ vattabbaṃ. Tenāha “evaṃ imāni sabbāni dasa padānī”ti. “Yojetabbānī”ti ca vacanaseso.

'And this is the measure: the four nutriments' means that this, namely the four nutriments, is the measure for the occurrence of these perversions. This is what is said: as long as the four nutriments are not fully understood, so long do the four perversions manifest. As long as the four perversions are not abandoned, so long do the four kinds of clinging increase. Thus, it should be stated everywhere as is fitting. Therefore, he said: 'Thus, all these ten factors.' And the remainder of the statement is: 'should be connected.'

“Abhijjhāya ganthatī”ti iminā abhijjhāyanameva ganthananti dasseti. Esa nayo sesesupi. Papañcentoti diṭṭhābhinivesaṃ vitthārento.

'He binds with covetousness'—by this it is shown that coveting itself is the binding. This principle applies to the remaining factors as well. 'Proliferating' means expanding on the adherence to views.

Vippaṭisāruppattihetubhāvo kilesānaṃ āsavananti āha “āsavantī”ti. Kiṃ vippaṭisārāti tena kilesānaṃ vītikkamavatthuṃ vadati. Yasmā appahīnānusayasseva vippaṭisārā, na itarassa, tasmā “ye vippaṭisārā, te anusayā”ti vuttaṃ. Padadvayenapi phalūpacārena kāraṇaṃ vuttaṃ.

The fact that defilements are a cause for the arising of remorse is their 'outflowing'; hence it is said, 'they flow out' (āsavanti). What is remorse? It refers to the basis of transgression by those defilements. Since remorse arises only in one whose latent tendencies are not abandoned, and not in another, therefore it is said, 'What remorses are, those are latent tendencies.' By the two terms, the cause is stated by way of the result.

Paṭhamena [V 0.0117] padenāti yathāvuttesu dasasu suttapadesu paṭhamena padena. Paṭhamāya disāyāti tadatthasaṅkhātāya sīhavikkīḷitassa saṃkilesapakkhe paṭhamāya disāya.

'By the first term' means by the first term among the ten sutta passages mentioned above. 'By the first direction' means by the first direction on the defilement side of the lion's play, which is designated by that meaning.

Itīti evaṃ, vuttanayenāti attho. Kusalākusalānanti yathāvuttaanavajjasāvajjadhammānaṃ. Pakkhapaṭipakkhavasenāti vodānapakkhatappaṭipakkhavasena. Yojanāti paṭhamadisādibhāvena yutte katvā manasānupekkhanā. “Manasā volokayate”ti (netti. 4) hi vuttaṃ.

'Thus' means in this way, by the method stated. 'Of wholesome and unwholesome' refers to the blameless and blameworthy phenomena as previously described. 'By way of side and opposing side' means by way of the side of purification and its opposing side. 'Application' means mental contemplation after having connected them as the first direction and so on. For it is said, 'He surveys with the mind' (Netti. 4).

Tassāti [M 0.0124] disālokanassa. Sotāpattiphalādīnaṃ pariyosānatā indriyavasena veditabbā. Yesañhi saddhādīnaṃ indriyānaṃ vasena satipaṭṭhānādīni sijjhanti, tesaṃ vasena sotāpattiphalādīnaṃ pariyosānatā. Tattha sotāpattiphale saddhindriyaṃ pāripūriṃ gacchati. Sotāpanno hi saddhāya paripūrikārī. Sakadāgāmiphale vīriyindriyaṃ pāripūriṃ gacchati. Sakadāgāmī hi āraddhavīriyo uparimaggādhigamāya. Anāgāmiphale samādhindriyaṃ pāripūriṃ gacchati. Anāgāmī samādhismiṃ paripūrikārī. Aggaphale arahatte satindriyañca paññindriyañca pāripūriṃ gacchati. Arahā hi sativepullappatto, paññāvepullappatto cāti.

'Of that' means of surveying the directions. The culmination of the fruits of stream-entry and so on should be understood in terms of the faculties. For the culmination of the fruits of stream-entry and so on is by way of those very faculties of faith and so on, through which the foundations of mindfulness and other factors are accomplished. Therein, in the fruit of stream-entry, the faculty of faith reaches fulfillment. A stream-enterer is indeed one who brings faith to fulfillment. In the fruit of once-returning, the faculty of energy reaches fulfillment. A once-returner is indeed one of aroused energy for the attainment of the higher path. In the fruit of non-returning, the faculty of concentration reaches fulfillment. A non-returner is indeed one who brings concentration to fulfillment. In the final fruit, arahantship, the faculties of mindfulness and wisdom reach fulfillment. An arahant is indeed one who has attained an abundance of mindfulness and an abundance of wisdom.

Apare panāhu – saddhābalena subhasaññāya pahānaṃ. Saddahanto hi paṭikkūlamanasikāre kammaṃ karoti. Vīriyabalena sukhasaññāya pahānaṃ. Vīriyavā hi sukhassādaṃ abhibhavitvā yonisomanasikāramanuyuñjati. Samādhibalena niccasaññāya pahānaṃ. Samāhito hi saṅkhārānaṃ udayabbayaṃ pariggaṇhanto aniccasaññaṃ paṭilabhati. Paññābalena attasaññāya pahānaṃ. Paññavā hi saṅkhārānaṃ avasavattitaṃ sallakkhento attasuññataṃ paṭivijjhati. Sati pana sabbatthāpi icchitabbā. Tenāha “satiṃ ca khvāhaṃ, bhikkhave, sabbatthikaṃ vadāmī”ti (saṃ. ni. 5.234; mi. pa. 2.1.13). Evaṃ catuvipallāsappahāyīnaṃ catunnaṃ indriyānaṃ pāripūriṭṭhānaṃ cattāri sāmaññaphalāni catuvipallāsamukhānaṃ catunnaṃ disānaṃ pariyosānāni vuttānīti.

Others, however, say: The abandoning of the perception of beauty is through the power of faith. For one who has faith applies himself to the contemplation of the repulsive. The abandoning of the perception of pleasure is through the power of energy. For one who is energetic overcomes the enjoyment of pleasure and applies appropriate attention. The abandoning of the perception of permanence is through the power of concentration. For one who is concentrated, comprehending the rise and fall of formations, attains the perception of impermanence. The abandoning of the perception of self is through the power of wisdom. For one who is wise, discerning the uncontrollability of formations, penetrates selflessness. Mindfulness, however, is to be desired in all circumstances. Hence it is said, 'Indeed, monks, I declare mindfulness as useful in all circumstances' (Saṃ. Ni. 5.234; Mi. Pa. 2.1.13). Thus, the four fruits of recluseship are said to be the fulfillment of the four faculties that abandon the four perversions, and the culminations of the four directions that are the starting points of the four perversions.

“Lobho akusalamūla”ntiādi lobhādīnaṃ hetuphalabhāvena sampayuttatāya dassanaṃ.

'Greed is an unwholesome root,' etc., shows the connection of greed and the like as cause and effect due to their association.

Tattha manāpikenāti yebhuyyavasena vuttaṃ. Amanāpikenāpi hi ārammaṇena vipariyesavasena lobho uppajjati. Manāpikenāti vā manāpikākārena. Phassavedanūpavicārarāgavitakkapariḷāhā [V 0.0118] sahajātāpi labbhanti, asahajātāpi. “Uppādo”ti etena uppajjamānasaṅkhāraggahaṇanti “uppajjatī”ti vuttaṃ. Uppādalakkhaṇasseva pana gahaṇe “uppajjatī”ti na vattabbaṃ siyā. Na hi uppādo uppajjati, rāgajapariḷāhahetukatā ca tesaṃ rāgassa taṇhāsabhāvattā. Taṇhā hi dukkhassa samudayo, yaṃ kiñci samudayadhammaṃ, sabbaṃ taṃ dukkhanti. Tathā ca vuttaṃ “taṇhāsahajātavedanāya [M 0.0125] pana lobho sahajātādipaccayehi ca paccayo”ti. Evaṃ iṭṭhārammaṇe uppannalobhasahagatasukhavedanāya udayo idha “uppādo saṅkhatalakkhaṇa”nti vutto, tassā vipariṇāmo “vipariṇāmadukkhatā”ti. Vipariṇāmāvatthā ca udayāvatthaṃ vinā na hotīti sā taṃ nissāya uppajjantī viya vuttā “uppādaṃ…pe… dukkhatā”ti.

Here, 'with what is agreeable' is stated as a generalization. For greed can also arise through a disagreeable object by way of inverse perception. Or 'with what is agreeable' can mean in the manner of what is agreeable. Contact, feeling, investigation, lust, thought, and feverishness are found both as co-nascent and not co-nascent. By 'arising', the grasping of formations as they are arising is meant; thus, it is said, 'it arises.' If, however, only the characteristic of arising were grasped, it would not be fit to say 'it arises.' For arising does not arise. And their being caused by the feverishness born of lust is because lust has the nature of craving. For craving is the origin of suffering, and whatever has the nature of arising is suffering. And so it is said: 'Greed is a condition for feeling co-arisen with craving, by way of co-nascence and other conditions.' Thus, the arising of pleasant feeling accompanied by greed toward a desirable object is here called 'arising as a conditioned characteristic,' and its change is called 'the suffering of change.' And since the state of change does not occur without the state of arising, it is spoken of as if arising dependent on that: 'arising... suffering.'

Doso akusalamūlantiādīsupi vuttanayānusārena attho veditabbo. Ayaṃ pana viseso – ṭhitassa aññathattaṃ nāma jarā, taṃ nissāya domanassassa uppajjanato vuttaṃ “ṭhitassa…pe… dukkhadukkhatā”ti. Dosajapariḷāhahetukatā jarāya dosabahulassa puggalassa nacirena jīraṇato veditabbā.

In the case of 'hatred is an unwholesome root,' and so forth, the meaning should be understood in accordance with the method explained above. This, however, is the distinction: aging is the alteration of what is stable; and because displeasure arises in dependence on that, it is said, 'the alteration of what is stable... is suffering as suffering.' That aging is caused by the feverishness born of hatred should be understood from the fact that a person abundant in hatred ages quickly.

Vayoti saṅkhārānaṃ nirodho. Aniccatāvasena ca saṅkhatadhammānaṃ saṅkhāradukkhatāti vuttaṃ “vaya…pe… saṅkhāradukkhatā”ti. Tenāha bhagavā “yadaniccaṃ taṃ dukkha”nti (saṃ. ni. 3.15, 45, 76; paṭi. ma. 2.10). Mohajapariḷāhahetukatā vayalakkhaṇassa yebhuyyena sammohanimittattā, maraṇassa avijjāpaccayattā ca saṃsārappavattiyā veditabbā.

'Passing away' is the cessation of formations. And by way of impermanence, it is said of conditioned phenomena that they are the suffering of formations: 'passing away... is the suffering of formations.' Therefore, the Blessed One said: 'What is impermanent, that is suffering.' That this is caused by the feverishness born of delusion should be understood because the characteristic of passing away is for the most part a sign of delusion, and because death, for the continuation of saṃsāra, is conditioned by ignorance.

Alobhādīnaṃ paññādipāripūrihetukatā yathārahaṃ upanissayakoṭisahajātakoṭiyā ca paccayabhāvena veditabbā. Sabbe hi kusalā dhammā sabbesaṃ kusalānaṃ dhammānaṃ yathāsambhavaṃ paccayavisesā honti evāti. Abyāpādavitakkasannissayo upavicāro abyāpādūpavicāro. Avihiṃsūpacārepi eseva nayo.

That non-greed and so forth are a cause for the fulfillment of wisdom and so forth should be understood as their being a condition by way of the categories of decisive support and co-nascence, as is suitable. For all wholesome states are indeed particular conditions for all wholesome states, according to possibility. Investigation supported by the thought of non-ill-will is investigation of non-ill-will. This same principle applies to the investigation of non-harming.

Ayaṃ tipukkhalo nāma dutiyo nayo saddhiṃ disālokananayena niddiṭṭhoti vacanaseso. “Ime cattāro”tiādi puggalādhiṭṭhāneneva nandiyāvaṭṭassa nayassa bhūmidassanatthaṃ āraddhaṃ. Ime yathāvuttapaṭipadācatukkassa vasena catubbidhā. Visesenāti diṭṭhitaṇhāsannissayatāvisesena. Diṭṭhicarito hi tikkhapañño, mandapañño ca sukhāya paṭipadāya khippābhiññāya [V 0.0119] ca dandhābhiññāya ca niyyātīti dvidhā vuttoti. Tathā taṇhācarito dukkhāya paṭipadāya khippābhiññāya ca dandhābhiññāya ca niyyātīti dvidhā vuttoti dassito cāyamattho. Tenāha “dve honti diṭṭhicarito ca taṇhācarito cā”ti.

This second method, called the 'Threefold Person,' together with the 'Illumination of Directions' method, has been explained—this is the remainder of the statement. The passage beginning 'These four' was started for the purpose of showing the ground of the 'Cycle of Delight' method, with the individual as its basis. These are fourfold by way of the fourfold practice mentioned above. 'By distinction' means by the distinction of being supported by views or craving. For a person of view-based character, whether of sharp or dull wisdom, is said to be liberated in two ways: by the pleasant practice with quick direct knowledge, or with slow direct knowledge. Similarly, a person of craving-based character is said to be liberated in two ways: by the painful practice with quick direct knowledge, or with slow direct knowledge. And this meaning has been shown. Therefore, it is said: 'There are two—one of view-based character and one of craving-based character.'

Cattāro [M 0.0126] hutvāti sīhavikkīḷitassa nayassa bhūmidassane cattāro hutvā ṭhitā, catuppaṭipadāvasena cattāro katvā vuttāti attho. Tayo hontīti tipukkhalassa nayassa bhūmidassane ugghaṭitaññuādivasena tayo bhavanti. Tayo hutvāti tathā tayo hutvā ṭhitā tayo katvā kathitā. Dve hontīti idāni nandiyāvaṭṭassa nayassa bhūmidassane dve bhavanti. Ajjhosānanti diṭṭhiajjhosānaṃ. Abhinivesoti taṇhābhiniveso. Ahaṃkāroti ahaṃmāno “aha”nti vā karaṇaṃ ahaṃkāro. Diṭṭhimānamaññanānaṃ vasena “ahamasmī”ti samanupassanā mamaṃkāro, mamāyanaṃ taṇhāggāho.

'Being four' means that in showing the ground of the 'Lion's Play' method, they stand as four; the meaning is that they are spoken of as made four by way of the fourfold practice. 'Being three' means that in showing the ground of the 'Threefold Person' method, they become three by way of the one of quick understanding, and so forth. 'Being three' means they thus stand as three and are spoken of as made three. 'Being two' means that now, in showing the ground of the 'Cycle of Delight' method, they become two. 'Adherence' is adherence to views. 'Insistence' is the insistence of craving. 'I-making' is the conceit 'I am,' or the act of making 'I' is I-making. 'Mine-making' is the consideration 'I am' by way of views, conceit, and conceiving; possessiveness is the grasping of craving.

Dasapadāni “paṭhamā disā”ti kātabbānīti nandiyāvaṭṭassa nayassa “paṭhamā disā”ti karaṇīyāni, “paṭhamā disā”ti vavatthapetabbānīti attho. Saṃkhittena…pe… pakkhassāti anekappabhedassapi kaṇhapakkhassa saṃkilesapakkhassa atthaṃ saṃkhittena saṅkhepena paṭipakkhe vattamāne vodānadhamme uddissa ñāpenti pakāsenti, paṭhamā kātabbāti yojanā. Dasa padāni dutiyakānīti taṇhādikā dasa koṭṭhāsā “dutiyā disā”ti kātabbā. “Saṃkhittena…pe… kaṇhapakkhassā”ti ānetvā yojetabbaṃ.

The ten terms are to be made 'the first direction.' This means that according to the 'Cycle of Delight' method, they are to be made 'the first direction'; the meaning is that they are to be established as 'the first direction.' 'In brief... of the side' means that, referring to the purifying qualities existing on the opposing side, they make known and declare in brief, in summary, the meaning of the dark side, the side of defilement, even though it has many divisions. The connection is that 'the first is to be made.' 'The ten terms are the second' means the ten portions beginning with craving are to be made 'the second direction.' The phrase 'In brief... of the dark side' should be brought in and connected here.

Yonisoti upāyaso. Yoniso manasikāro aniccādivasena paṭhamamanasikāro. Paññāti sutacintāmayī paññā, jhānābhiññā ca. Nibbidāti nibbedhañāṇaṃ. Somanassadhammūpasañhitaṃ pamodādisahagataṃ cetasikasukhaṃ.

'Wisely' means by skillful means. 'Wise attention' is the initial attention by way of impermanence and so forth. 'Wisdom' is the wisdom consisting of learning and reflection, and that of meditative absorption and direct knowledge. 'Disenchantment' is penetrating knowledge. Mental pleasure accompanied by gladness and so forth, connected with states of mental joy.

Kusalapakkhe cāti ca-saddo samuccayattho, tena ubhayapakkhato samuccayavasena catasso disā, na paccekanti dasseti.

In the phrase 'and in the wholesome side,' the word 'ca' (and) has the meaning of conjunction. It thereby shows that the four directions are by way of combination from both sides, not individually.

Tesanti taṇhādīnaṃ, taṇhāya, taṇhāpakkhikānañcāti attho. Satipi anavasesato rāge pahīyamāne anavasesato avijjāpi pahīyateva, rāgassa pana cetovimutti ujupaṭipakkhoti dassanatthaṃ “rāgavirāgā”ti vuttaṃ. Avijjāvirāgāti etthāpi eseva nayo. Ayañca attho “āsavānaṃ khayā anāsavaṃ cetovimuttiṃ paññāvimutti”ntiādinā (ma. ni. 1.438) āgatapāḷiyā atthavaṇṇanāvasena vuttā, idha pana “rāgavirāgā cetovimutti sekkhaphalaṃ, avijjāvirāgā [V 0.0120] paññāvimutti asekkhaphala”ntiādinā (netti. 51) vevacanasamāropane [M 0.0127] āgatattā purimā anāgāmiphalaṃ. Tañhi kāmarāgassa ujuvipaccanīkato samādhipāripūriyāva visesato “rāgavirāgā cetovimuttī”ti vuccati, pacchimā arahattaphalaṃ taṇhāya, avijjāya ca anavasesappahānato, paññāpāripūriyā ca “avijjāvirāgā paññāvimuttī”ti vuccati.

'Of these' means of craving and so forth; that is, of craving and of things allied with craving. Although when lust is abandoned without remainder, ignorance is also abandoned without remainder, it is said 'through dispassion for lust' to show that liberation of mind is the direct antidote to lust. This same principle applies to 'through dispassion for ignorance.' And this meaning is stated by way of commentary on the Pāli passage that begins, 'With the destruction of the taints, the taintless liberation of mind, liberation by wisdom...' (MN 1.438). Here, however, since it is given in the application of synonyms as 'dispassion for lust is liberation of mind, a trainee's fruit; dispassion for ignorance is liberation by wisdom, a fruit of one beyond training' (Netti. 51), the former is the fruit of non-returning. For that, because it is the direct opposite of sensual lust and due to the fulfillment of concentration, is specifically called 'liberation of mind through dispassion for lust.' The latter is the fruit of arahantship, and it is called 'liberation by wisdom through dispassion for ignorance' because of the abandoning without remainder of craving and ignorance, and due to the fulfillment of wisdom.

Tatthāti nandiyāvaṭṭanaye. Tesūti “cattāri padānī”ti vuttesu taṇhādīsu catūsu mūlapadesu. Idha samosaraṇanti saṅgaho vutto, so ca sabhāvato, sabhāgato ca hotīti taṇhādīni cattāri dassetvā “tesu aṭṭhārasa mūlapadāni samosarantī”ti vuttaṃ. Samathaṃ bhajanti sabhāvato, sabhāgato cāti adhippāyo. Vipassanaṃ bhajantīti etthāpi eseva nayo. Nayādhiṭṭhānānaṃ nayādhiṭṭhāne anuppaveso nayānaṃ nayesu anuppaveso eva nāma hotīti āha “tipukkhalo…pe… anuppavisantī”ti.

'Therein' means in the 'Cycle of Delight' method. 'Among them' refers to the four root terms beginning with craving, which were spoken of as the 'four terms.' Here, 'converging' is said to be inclusion, and this occurs by nature and by commonality. Having shown the four beginning with craving, it is said, 'In them, eighteen root terms converge.' The intention is that they partake of tranquility by nature and by commonality. This same principle applies to 'they partake of insight.' The entry of the bases of a method into the basis of a method is indeed what is called the entry of methods into methods. Therefore, he said: 'the Threefold Person... enter...'

Alobhāmohapakkhaṃ abhajāpetvā adosapakkhaṃ bhajāpetabbassa nandiyāvaṭṭasīhavikkīḷitamūlapadassa abhāvato adoso ekasuttakoṭiyā ekakova hotīti dassento āha “adoso adoso evā”ti. Doso doso evāti etthāpi eseva nayo. Samosaranti sabhāgato ca sabhāvato ca saṅgahaṃ gacchantīti attho.

Showing that non-hatred stands alone as a single sutta category because there is no root term of the 'Lion's Play of the Cycle of Delight' that would cause one to cultivate the side of non-hatred while not causing one to cultivate the side of non-greed and non-delusion, he said: 'Non-hatred is just non-hatred.' This same principle applies to 'Hatred is just hatred.' 'They converge' means they are included by way of commonality and by way of nature.

Bhūmi gocaroti ca mūlapadāni eva sandhāya vadati. Ekekaṃ nayaṃ anuppavisati taṃtaṃmūlapadānuppavesato. Kusale vā viññāte akusalo paṭipakkho, akusale vā kusalo paṭipakkho anvesitabbo saṃvaṇṇiyamānasuttapadānurūpato upaparikkhitabbo. Anvesanā upaparikkhā “disālokana”nti vuccati. So nayo niddisitabboti tathā anvesitvā tehi dhammehi disā vavatthapetvā so so nayo niddhāretvā yojetabbo. Yathā mūlapadesu mūlapadānaṃ anuppaveso saṃvaṇṇito, imināva nayena mūlapadato mūlapadānaṃ niddhāraṇāti veditabbāti dassento “yathā ekamhi…pe… niddisitabbānī”ti āha. “Ekekasmiñhī”tiādi kāraṇavacanaṃ.

And when he speaks of 'ground' and 'range,' he does so with reference to the root terms themselves. It enters each method through the entry of those respective root terms. When a wholesome state is known, its unwholesome opposite should be sought; or when an unwholesome state is known, its wholesome opposite should be sought; it should be examined in accordance with the sutta terms being explained. This seeking and examination is called the 'Illumination of Directions.' That method should be pointed out. Thus, having sought in that way, having established the directions by means of those phenomena, and having determined the respective method, it should be applied. To show that just as the entry of root terms into root terms has been described, so by this same method the extraction of root terms from a root term should be understood, he said: 'as in one... should be pointed out...' The phrase beginning 'For in each...' is a statement of reason.

Tattha tatthāti ekekasmiṃ naye. Ekasmiṃ dhamme viññāteti taṇhādike ekasmiṃ mūlapadadhamme sarūpato, niddhāraṇavasena vā viññāte. Sabbe [M 0.0128] dhammā viññātā hontīti tadaññamūlapadabhūtā sabbe lobhādayo viññātā nayassa bhūmicaraṇāyogyatāya pakāsā pākaṭā honti[V 0.0121]. “Imesa”ntiādi nayattayadisābhūtadhammānaṃ matthakapāpanena tiṇṇaṃ nayānaṃ kūṭaggahaṇaṃ, taṃ heṭṭhā vuttanayameva.

‘Here and there’ means in each single method. ‘When one phenomenon is understood’ means that when a single root-principle phenomenon, such as craving and so forth, is understood either according to its own nature or by way of determination. ‘All phenomena are understood’ means that all other root-principle phenomena, such as greed and so forth, become understood, being manifest and evident due to the method's suitability for proceeding on its ground. The phrase ‘of these,’ and so on, by bringing to a culmination the phenomena that constitute the direction of the three methods, is the grasping of the pinnacle of the three methods, which is simply the method explained previously.

Puna “imesū”tiādi kammanayadvayassa vibhāgavibhāvanaṃ, taṃ viññeyyameva.

Again, the phrase ‘in these,’ and so on, is a clarification of the twofold division of the method of action; that should be understood.

Nayasamuṭṭhānavāravaṇṇanā niṭṭhitā.

The explanation of the chapter on the arising of methods is concluded.

Sāsanapaṭṭhānavāravaṇṇanā

The Explanation of the Section on the Foundation of the Teaching

89. Saṅgahavārādīsūti saṅgahavārauddesaniddesavāresu. Sarūpato na dassitaṃ, atthato pana dassitamevāti adhippāyo. Tameva hi atthato dassanatthaṃ udāharaṇabhāvena nikkhipati, yathā mūlapadehi paṭṭhānaṃ niddhāretabbanti. “Aññamaññasaṅgaho”ti idaṃ mūlapadapaṭṭhānānaṃ aññamaññato niddhāretabbatāya kāraṇavacanaṃ “sati anuppavese tato viniggāmo siyā”ti. Paṭṭhānanti ettha pa-iti upasaggapadaṃ, taṃ pana “vibhattesu dhammesu yaṃ seṭṭhaṃ, tadupāgamu”ntiādīsu viya pakāratthajotakanti dassento “pakārehi ṭhāna”ntiādīsu viya pakāratthajotakanti dassento “pakārehi ṭhāna”nti āha. Idhāti imasmiṃ nettippakaraṇe. Tassāti desanāsaṅkhātassa pariyattisāsanassa. Tathābhāvadīpananti veneyyajjhāsayānurūpena pavattitattā saṃkilesabhāgiyatādippakārehi ṭhitabhāvena dīpetabbattā “dīpissatīti dīpana”nti katvā. Patiṭṭhahanti adhisīlasikkhādayo samudāyarūpena gahitā. Etehi saṃkilesadhammādīhi, saṃkilesadhammādīnaṃ adhisīlasikkhādīnaṃ pavattanupāyatā anupubbikathāya sāmukkaṃsikāya dhammadesanāya dīpetabbā. Tesanti saṃkilesadhammādīnaṃ. Puna tesanti suttāni sandhāyāha.

89. ‘In the sections on inclusion and so forth’ refers to the sections on inclusion, exposition, and explanation. The intention is that it is not shown in its form, but it is indeed shown in its meaning. Indeed, to show that very meaning, he sets it forth by way of example, so that the Paṭṭhāna might be determined from the root principles. The phrase ‘mutual inclusion’ is a causal statement for the mutual determination of the root principles and the Paṭṭhāna, meaning ‘if there is entry, then there would be emergence from it.’ Here, in the word ‘Paṭṭhāna,’ ‘pa-’ is a prefix. To show that this prefix illuminates the meaning of ‘mode,’ as in such phrases as ‘what is supreme among the classified phenomena, that is approached,’ he says ‘establishment by modes.’ ‘Here’ means in this Nettippakaraṇa. ‘Of that’ refers to the scriptural teaching designated as the discourse. ‘Illuminating such a state’ means that because it proceeds in accordance with the inclinations of those to be trained, it is to be illuminated as abiding in states with various modes, such as that partaking of defilement, and is thus called ‘illumination’ (dīpana) because ‘it will illuminate’ (dīpissati). ‘They are established’ refers to the training in higher virtue and so forth, taken as a collection. By these phenomena of defilement and so forth, the way of proceeding for the phenomena of defilement and so forth, and for the training in higher virtue and so forth, should be illuminated through the gradual instruction and the supreme Dhamma discourse. ‘Of those’ refers to the phenomena of defilement and so forth. Again, ‘of those’ refers to the suttas.

Goṭṭhāti vajā. Paṭṭhitagāvoti gatagāvo. Āgataṭṭhānasminti sīhanādasuttaṃ (ma. ni. 1.156) vadati. Pavattagamanattā etthāti vacanaseso. Atha vā gacchati etthāti gamanaṃ, desanāñāṇassa nissaṅgavasena pavattagamanadesabhāvato paṭṭhānaṃ nāmāti attho. Vomissāti “saṃkilesabhāgiyañca vāsanābhāgiyañcā”tiādinā dukatikacatukkabhāvena missitā.

‘Goṭṭha’ means a cattle-pen. ‘Paṭṭhitagāvo’ means cows that have gone. ‘In the place where it is found’ refers to the Sīhanāda Sutta (MN 1.156). The remainder of the phrase is ‘here, because of the proceeding of the movement.’ Alternatively, ‘one goes in this, thus it is a going’; the meaning is that it is called ‘Paṭṭhāna’ because it is the sphere for the unattached proceeding and movement of the knowledge of the teaching. ‘Vomissā’ means mixed in the manner of couplets, triplets, and quadruplets, as in ‘partaking of defilement and partaking of penetration,’ and so on.

Saṃkilesabhāve [M 0.0129] ñāpetabbe pavattaṃ, taṃ visayaṃ katvā desitanti attho, atthamattavacanañcetaṃ[V 0.0122], saṃkilesabhāge bhavanti saddanayena attho veditabbo. “Saṃkilesabhāgika”ntipi pāṭho, tassa saṃkilesabhāgo etassa atthi, saṃkilesabhāge vā niyuttaṃ, saṃkilesabhāgassa vā pabodhanasīlaṃ saṃkilesabhāgikaṃ, tadeva saṃkilesabhāgiyanti attho veditabbo. Padālanaṃ samucchindanaṃ, padālanasannissayatā cettha padālanaggahaṇena gahitāti daṭṭhabbaṃ. Asekkheti asekkhadhamme. Tesaṃ vomissakanayavasenāti tesaṃ saṃkilesabhāgiyādīnaṃ catunnaṃ paṭikkhepāpaṭikkhepavomissakanayavasena.

The meaning is that it proceeds to make known the state of defilement, and is taught having made that its object. This is merely a statement of meaning; the meaning should be understood by the grammatical principle as ‘occurring in the portion of defilement.’ There is also the reading ‘saṃkilesabhāgika.’ Its meaning should be understood as ‘saṃkilesabhāgiya,’ that is, that to which the portion of defilement belongs, or that which is connected with the portion of defilement, or that which has the nature of awakening the portion of defilement. ‘Padālanaṃ’ means complete cutting off. Here, it should be seen that by the term ‘padālana,’ dependence on cutting off is understood. ‘Asekkhe’ refers to the qualities of one beyond training. ‘By way of their mixed method’ means by way of the mixed method of rejection and non-rejection for those four, namely, that which partakes of defilement and so on.

“Tāni pana cha dukā”tiādinā padānaṃ gahaṇaparicchedato vavatthāpanataṃ vatvā parato “sādhāraṇāni katānī”ti padassa atthasaṃvaṇṇanāya sayameva sarūpato dassessati. “Anuddharaṇe kāraṇaṃ natthī’ti vatvā uddharaṇe pana kāraṇaṃ dassento “tathā hi vakkhatī”tiādinā pāḷimāhari. Vodānaṃ nāma saṃkilesato hoti saṃkiliṭṭhasseva vodānassa icchitattā. Yasmā vodānaṃ tadaṅgādivasena saṃkilesato visujjhanaṃ, tasmā “taṃ pana atthato vāsanābhāgiyādi eva hotī”ti vuttaṃ. Tattha tadaṅgavikkhambhanehi vodānaṃ vāsanābhāgiyādivasena hoti, samucchedapaṭippassaddhīhi vodānaṃ nibbedhabhāgiyavasena, asekkhabhāgiyavasena vodānaṃ paṭippassaddhiyā eva veditabbaṃ. Yāyaṃ desanā rāgādibhāginī siyā, sā saṃkilesabhāgiyā. Yāyaṃ desanā cāgādibhāginī siyā, sā vāsanābhāgiyā. Yā pana āpattivicchedanī sāvasesaṃ, anavasesañca, sā nibbedhabhāgiyā, asekkhabhāgiyā ca.

Having stated the determination of the terms by way of their inclusion and delimitation with the phrase ‘Now, regarding the six pairs,’ and so on, he will later explain the meaning of the phrase ‘made common’ by its own nature through an explanation of its meaning. Having stated, ‘There is no reason for not extracting,’ he then showed the reason for extracting by quoting the Pāḷi, ‘For thus it will be said,’ and so on. Purification occurs from defilement, for purification is desired only for what is defiled. Since purification is the cleansing from defilement by way of substitution and so on, therefore it is said, ‘But in meaning, it pertains to the partaking of penetration and so on.’ Therein, purification through substitution and suppression pertains to the partaking of penetration and so on; purification through eradication and tranquillization pertains to the partaking of penetration; purification by way of the partaking of the state of one beyond training should be understood as pertaining to tranquillization alone. That teaching which partakes of lust and so on, partakes of defilement. That teaching which partakes of generosity and so on, partakes of penetration. But that teaching which cuts off offenses—whether with or without remainder—partakes of penetration and of the state of one beyond training.

“Taṇhāsaṃkilesabhāgiyaṃ sutta”ntiādinā paṭhamameva saṃkilesabhāgassa dassitattā vuttaṃ “saṃkileso tividho…pe… visayadassanatthaṃ āraddha”nti. Bhavarāgo bhavapatthanā. Uppajjatīti na vigacchati. Tatra tatra bhaveti yadi vā kāmabhave, yadi vā rūpabhave, yadi vā arūpabhave. Padantarasaṃyojanavasenāti dukanayeneva padantarena yojanavasena. Missitāni katānīti saṃsaṭṭhāni katāni.

Because the portion of defilement was shown at the very beginning with the phrase ‘the sutta partaking of the defilement of craving,’ and so on, it is said, ‘Defilement is threefold… undertaken for the purpose of showing the domain.’ Lust for existence is the desire for existence. ‘It arises’ means it does not disappear. ‘In this or that existence’ means whether in the desire-realm existence, the form-realm existence, or the formless-realm existence. ‘By way of connection with another term’ means by way of connection with another term according to the method of couplets. ‘Made mixed’ means made combined.

Ekakacatukkavasena dassitabbāni padāni eva gahetvā āvuttinayadassanavasena missetvā avasiṭṭhadukavasena, tikacatukkavasena ca itare aṭṭha paṭṭhānabhāgā dassitāti āha “tāniyeva yathāvuttāni aṭṭha suttānī”tiādi[M 0.0130]. Cattāro ekakāyeva pāḷiyaṃ ādito dassitā. Chadukā pāḷiyaṃ āgatā cattāro, aṭṭhakathāyaṃ dveti. Cattāro tikā pāḷiyaṃ āgatā dve, aṭṭhakathāyaṃ dveti. Dve catukkā pana aṭṭhakathāyameva āgatā. “Pāḷiyaṃ anāgatā”ti idaṃ sarūpato [V 0.0123] anāgamanaṃ sandhāya vuttaṃ, nayato pana āgatabhāvo dassito eva. Ye panettha pāḷiyaṃ anāgatā, tesaṃ udāharaṇāni parato dassayissāma.

He says, ‘Those very eight suttas as stated,’ and so on, to show that having taken only the terms to be shown by way of singles and quadruplets, and having mixed them by way of showing the method of recurrence, the other eight portions of the Paṭṭhāna are shown by way of the remaining couplets, and by way of triplets and quadruplets. The four singles are shown in the Pāḷi text from the beginning. Of the six couplets, four are found in the Pāḷi text and two in the commentary. Of the four triplets, two are found in the Pāḷi text and two in the commentary. But the two quadruplets are found only in the commentary. The phrase ‘not found in the Pāḷi text’ is said with reference to their not being found in their exact form; however, their presence by way of method has indeed been shown.

Soḷasahīti soḷasavidhehi. Na hi tāni suttāni soḷaseva, atha kho soḷasappakārānīti mūlagaṇanaṃ ṭhapetvā kāraṇasuttaladdhena saṅkhāragabbhena tadanurūpo yo gaṇanavitthāro, tassa pattharaṇavidhi paṭṭhānanayo. Iminā…pe… natthīti yathāvuttapaṭṭhānavinimutto pariyattisāsanappadeso na vijjati yathārahaṃ taṃtaṃpaṭṭhānabhāvena pavattattāti dasseti. Yadi suttageyyādi navavidhaṃ pariyattisāsanaṃ yathāvuttapaṭṭhānavaseneva pavattaṃ, tattha kathamidha anidassitānaṃ gāthādīnaṃ saṃkilesabhāgiyādibhāvo gahetabboti pañhaṃ sandhāya “gāthāya gāthā anuminitabbā”tiādipāḷi pavattāti dassetuṃ “kathaṃ panā”tiādi vuttaṃ.

‘By sixteen’ means by sixteen kinds. For those suttas are not just sixteen in number, but are of sixteen types. Setting aside the root number, the Paṭṭhāna method is the procedure for laying out the corresponding numerical expansion, which is contained within the formations obtained from the causal sutta. By this… he shows that there is no portion of the scriptural teaching devoid of the aforesaid Paṭṭhāna, because it proceeds appropriately as this or that portion of the Paṭṭhāna. To show that the Pāḷi text beginning with ‘A verse should be inferred from a verse’ proceeds in response to the question, ‘If the ninefold scriptural teaching—sutta, geyya, and so on—proceeds only by way of the aforesaid Paṭṭhāna, then how is the state of partaking of defilement, and so on, to be understood for the verses and other parts not shown here?’, the phrase ‘But how…?’ and so on, is stated.

Tattha ayaṃ gāthā viyāti “kāmandhā jālasañchannā, manopubbaṅgamā dhammā, uddhaṃ adho sabbadhi vippamutto, yassa selūpamaṃ citta”ntiādinā idha udāhaṭagāthā viya. Gāthāti aññāpi tepiṭake buddhavacane āgatā idha anudāhaṭā. Saṃvaṇṇanākāle sammukhībhāvena “ayaṃ gāthā viyā”ti vuttā yā kāci gāthā “saṃkilesabhāgiyā”ti vā “saṃkilesavāsanānibbedhaasekkhabhāgiyā”ti vā anuminitabbā nayaggāhena ñāpetabbāti dassetuṃ vuttaṃ “saṃkilesa…pe… jānitabbāti attho”ti. Vā-saddo hi idha avuttavikappanattho. Sesapadesūti veyyākaraṇasuttapadesu.

Therein, 'like this verse' means like the verse cited here, beginning with 'Blinded by sensual pleasure, covered by a net,' 'Phenomena are preceded by mind,' 'Above, below, everywhere released,' 'Whose mind is like a rock,' and so on. 'Verse' also refers to other verses found in the word of the Buddha in the Tipiṭaka that are not cited here. To show that at the time of commenting, whatever verse is spoken of directly as 'like this verse' should be inferred and understood by grasping the method as being either 'partaking of defilement' or 'partaking of the penetration of the tendencies of defilement and of the trainee,' it is said, 'defilement... and so on... should be known, is the meaning.' For the word 'or' here has the meaning of an unstated alternative. 'In the remaining passages' means in the passages of the discourses in mixed prose and verse.

90. Ariyānaṃ dhammanti cārittavārittabhedaṃ sīlācāraṃ. Ekantakaraṇīyassa akaraṇampi vītikkamo eva.

90. 'The Dhamma of the noble ones' means the virtuous conduct of morality, which is divided into conduct to be practiced and conduct to be avoided. Not doing what should absolutely be done is indeed a transgression.

Avijjādike saṃkilesadhamme tadaṅgādivasena dhunātīti dhonā vuccati paññā. Paccavekkhitvā paribhuñjanapaññāti pana pakaraṇena avacchinnattā vuttaṃ. Taṃ atikkamitvā carantoti paccayānaṃ apaccavekkhitvā paccayaparibhoge ādīnavaṃ apassanto iṇaparibhogavasena paribhuñjanto na parimuccati nirayādidukkhato, vaṭṭadukkhato ca.

Because it shakes off defiling states, such as ignorance, by means of substitution of opposing factors and so on, wisdom is called 'dhonā' (purified). However, it is said to be 'the wisdom of consuming after reflection' because it is defined by the context. 'One who transgresses that and fares on' means one who, not reflecting on the requisites and not seeing the danger in the use of requisites, consumes them as if incurring a debt, and is not freed from the suffering of hell and so on, nor from the suffering of the round of existence.

Kukkujanakaṃ [M 0.0131] nāma kadaliyā pupphanāḷi. Parābhavāyāti vināsāya. Tathāti yathā phalapākantā kadalī, evaṃ veḷunaḷāpi osadhijātikattāti upasaṃhārattho tathā-saddo. Tenāha “phalaṃ veḷuṃ phalaṃ naḷa”nti.

'Kukkujanaka' is the name for the flower-stalk of a plantain tree. 'For ruin' means for destruction. The word 'so' has the meaning of a conclusion: just as the plantain tree perishes when its fruit ripens, so too do the bamboo and the reed, because they are a species of annual plant. Therefore, it is said, 'fruit for the bamboo, fruit for the reed.'

Sukhettepīti [V 0.0124] pi-saddena ko pana vādo ūsarādidosaduṭṭhesu khettesūti dasseti. “Chakaṇa…pe… attho”ti etena yathāvuttaabhisaṅkharaṇābhāvena bījadosaduṭṭhanti dasseti.

In 'even in a good field,' the word 'even' shows: what then to say of fields corrupted by faults such as being saline? By this, 'six moments... and so on... is the meaning,' it shows that they are corrupted by the fault of the seed, due to the absence of the aforementioned preparation.

91. Sajjitanti sañjitaṃ. Aparikkhateti paṭipakkhehi dhammehi avikkhambhite aroge.

91. 'Prepared' means produced. 'Uninvestigated' means not suppressed by opposing states, healthy.

Yāya samannāgato puggalo “kiṃ sutaṃ mayā, kiṃ vā suṇāmī”ti kusalaṃ gavesī carati, sā dhammojapaññā kissavā nāma. Dubbhāsitāti duṭṭhu bhāsitā, issāmacchariyadosādīhi duṭṭhā vā bhāsitā.

That wisdom of the essence of the Dhamma, endowed with which a person fares on seeking the wholesome, thinking, 'What have I heard, or what am I hearing?' is of what kind? 'Ill-spoken' means badly spoken, or spoken corrupted by faults such as envy and avarice.

92. Vicinātīti visesato cināti pasavati.

92. 'Investigates' means it especially accumulates, it produces.

Vigatabhūtāti vigatasacca. Tenāha “alīkavādī”ti.

'Devoid of what is real' means devoid of truth. Therefore, it is said, 'a speaker of falsehood.'

Avajātaputtāti lāmakaputta. Bhagavato sāsane pabbajitvā nihīnavuttitaṃ sandhāya vadati. Nerayikoti niraye nibbattanako. Pāpakammino papatanti etthāti papataṃ, narakaṃ.

'An inferior son' means a base son. This is said with reference to one who, having gone forth in the Dispensation of the Blessed One, has a base livelihood. 'Bound for hell' means one who is reborn in hell. 'Chasm' is so called because evil-doers fall into it; it means hell.

Taṇhādīnaṃ sabhāvabhedatoti taṇhādiṭṭhiduccaritānaṃ taṇhāyanaviparītadassanaduṭṭhacaritatāsaṅkhātasabhāvavibhāgato. Avatthābhedatoti taṇhāya chandapemalobharāganandīpipāsāmucchādayo, diṭṭhiyā gāhaparāmāsamicchābhinivesavisukavipphanditaviparītadassanādayo, duccaritassa tiracchānapettivisayaasurayonigāmitādayo avatthāvisesā. Ca-saddena tesaṃ kāmataṇhādirūpataṇhādiattānudiṭṭhādisassatagāhādikāyaduccaritādi- pāṇātipātādippakārabhedo saṅgayhati.

'By the division of the intrinsic nature of craving and so on' means by the division of the intrinsic nature of craving, views, and misconduct, which are characterized as craving, perverse seeing, and corrupt conduct, respectively. 'By the division of states': for craving, there are desire, affection, greed, lust, delight, thirst, fainting, and so on; for views, there are grasping, adherence, wrong resolve, writhing in views, perverse seeing, and so on; for misconduct, there are particular states such as leading to the animal realm, the realm of ghosts, and the realm of asuras. By the word 'and,' the division of their types is included, such as craving for sensual pleasures, craving for form, and so on; self-view, eternalist grasping, and so on; bodily misconduct, killing living beings, and so on.

93. Vipulanti uḷāraṃ, telādīhi ceva dhanadhaññādīhi ca pahūtasannicayanti attho. Sambādhāti janasaṃmaddasaṅghaṭā.

93. 'Vast' means magnificent; the meaning is a great accumulation of oil and so on, and of wealth, grain, and so on. 'Crowded' means a dense throng of people.

Daṇḍena na hiṃsatīti ettha vuttaṃ yaṃ daṇḍanidhānaṃ, taṃ vaṭṭavivaṭṭanissitaṃ. Tadubhayassāpi phalaṃ dassento “so puggalo”tiādimāha.

Here, in 'does not harm with a rod,' the laying down of the rod that is mentioned is dependent on both the round of existence and the ending of the round. Showing the result of both of these, he said, 'that person,' and so on.

94. Kiñcati [M 0.0132] taṃsamaṅginaṃ vimaddatīti kiñcanaṃ, rāgādi, palibundhati kusalappavattiṃ nivāretīti [V 0.0125] palibodho, rāgādiyeva, kiñcanameva palibodho kiñcanapalibodho. Atha vā kiñcanañca palibodho ca kiñcanapalibodho, āmisakiñcikkhañca rāgādisaṃkileso cāti attho.

94. It is an 'impediment' (kiñcana) because it crushes one who possesses it; this refers to lust and so on. It is an 'obstruction' (palibodha) because it obstructs, it prevents the arising of the wholesome; this also refers to lust and so on. An impediment itself is an obstruction, hence 'impediment-obstruction.' Or else, an impediment and an obstruction are an 'impediment-obstruction'; the meaning is both a material trifle and a defilement such as lust.

Visesitanti vilomaṃ, visamaṃ kiriyanti attho. Rājabhaṇḍanti orodhe sandhāya vadanti.

'Distinguished' means contrary; the meaning is an uneven action. 'The king's property' is said with reference to the harem.

Yācayogoti yācanayogo, yācakānaṃ manorathaparipūraṇato. Tenāha “yācitabbayutto”ti. Dānayuttoti satataṃ dānakiriyāsamaṅgī. Dānasaṃvibhāgaratoti ettha dānaṃ nāma atthikānaṃ yathādhippāyapaṭiyattapariccāgo, saṃvibhāgo attanā paribhuñjitabbato appamattakatopi saṃvibhajanaṃ. Imehi kho…pe… hotīti ettha hotisaddena “samannāgato”ti padaṃ sambandhitabbaṃ, na “sotāpanno”ti dassetuṃ “sotāpanno…pe… hotī”ti vuttaṃ. Tehi dhammehi samannāgamo hi idha vidhīyati, na sotāpannabhāvo, tena sotāpannalakkhaṇamete dhammā, na sotāpannabhāvalakkhaṇanti dasseti. Tathā hi “sotāpannena…pe… labbhamānataṃ dassetī”ti vuttaṃ.

'Open to requests' means fit to be requested, because of fulfilling the wishes of supplicants. Therefore, it is said, 'fit to be requested.' 'Devoted to giving' means constantly endowed with the act of giving. In 'delighting in giving and sharing,' 'giving' is the relinquishing of what has been prepared according to the intention of those in need; 'sharing' is the distribution of even a small amount from what is to be consumed by oneself. Here, in 'with these... and so on... he is,' the word 'endowed' should be connected with the word 'is,' not 'a stream-enterer.' To show this, it is said, 'a stream-enterer... and so on... is.' For here, being endowed with those qualities is prescribed, not the state of a stream-enterer. Thereby it shows that these qualities are a characteristic of a stream-enterer, not the characteristic of the state of a stream-enterer. For so it is said, 'by a stream-enterer... and so on... showing that it is obtainable.'

95. Lapati katheti etenāti lapanaṃ, oṭṭhaṃ.

95. One speaks, one talks with this, thus it is 'the speaker'; it means the lip.

97. Muditoti dibbasampattiyā pamudito.

97. 'Rejoicing' means rejoicing in divine attainment.

99. Kiñcāpi udatārīti taraṇakiriyā atītabhāvena vuttā, taraṇameva pana gahetvā āha “oghataraṇassa ariyamaggakiccattā”ti. Evaṃ vippamutto, vimuttoti ca ettha muccanakiriyāyapi vattabbaṃ.

99. Although in 'he has crossed over,' the action of crossing is spoken of in the past tense, taking just the crossing itself, it is said, 'because the crossing of the flood is the task of the noble path.' Similarly, here in 'fully released' and 'released,' this should also be said of the action of being released.

100. Pātu-saddapubbako bhavanti-saddo siyā uppādapariyāyo siyā āvibhāvapariyāyoti “pātubhavantī”ti padassa “uppajjanti, pakāsenti cā”ti attho vutto. Pātubhūtadhammassāti uppannabodhipakkhiyadhammassa, vibhūtacatusaccadhammassa vā. No kalloti na yutto. Sahetudhammanti ettha paccayuppannadhammāva gahitā, na paccayadhammāti? Nayidamevaṃ daṭṭhabbaṃ paccayadhammānampi paccayuppannabhāvānativattanato. Atha vā sahetudhammanti paccayuppannadhammo padhānabhāvena vutto, paccayadhammo pana guṇabhāvenāti evamettha ubhayesaṃ vuttabhāvo veditabbo.

100. The word 'bhavanti' preceded by the word 'pātu' can be a synonym for arising or a synonym for manifestation; thus, the meaning of the word 'pātubhavanti' is said to be 'they arise and they are manifest.' 'Of the Dhamma that has appeared' means of the factors of enlightenment that have arisen, or of the Dhamma of the Four Truths that has been made manifest. 'Not proper' means not fit. In 'the Dhamma with its cause,' are only conditionally arisen phenomena taken, and not the condition-phenomena? It should not be seen thus, because condition-phenomena also do not transcend the state of being conditionally arisen. Or else, in 'the Dhamma with its cause,' the conditionally arisen phenomenon is spoken of as primary, while the condition-phenomenon is spoken of as secondary; thus it should be understood here that both are spoken of.

Āraññakanti [V 0.0126][M 0.0133] āraññakaṅgasamannāgataṃ. Aññātoti paricayavasena na ñāto, asaṃsaṭṭhoti attho. Tenāha “niccanavo”ti.

'A forest-dweller' means one endowed with the forest-dweller's ascetic practice. 'Unknown' means not known by way of familiarity; the meaning is unassociated. Therefore, it is said, 'always new.'

Byāpādavihiṃsāvitakkavirahe veriparisaṅkāya abhāve akittiparimuttīti evamādīhipi kāraṇehi kodhappahānena sukhaṃ supati. Kodhapariḷāhābhāvo pana pākaṭataroti āha “kodha…pe… sayatī”ti. Visamūlassāti ettha visasarikkhatāya “visa”nti dukkhaṃ adhippetanti āha “dukkhavipākassā”ti. Sukhanti cetasikasukhaṃ. Akkuṭṭhassa paccakkositvā ca paccakkosanahetu uppajjatīti yojanā.

By abandoning anger, one sleeps happily, due to the absence of thoughts of ill will and violence, the absence of apprehension of danger from enemies, being released from infamy, and for such reasons. However, the absence of the burning fever of anger is more evident, thus it is said, 'anger... and so on... he lies down.' In 'of the poisoned root,' here, because of its similarity to poison, by 'poison' suffering is intended; thus it is said, 'of that which has a painful result.' 'Sweet' means mental happiness. The construction is: for one who has been reviled, a cause for being reviled in return arises by reviling in return.

101. Sallubbāhanaṃ salluddharaṇaṃ.

101. 'The pulling out of the dart' is the extraction of the dart.

Visayabhedena, pavattiākārabhedena ca anekabhedattā kāmasaññāya vuttaṃ “yāya kāyacī”ti.

Because the perception of sensual desire is of many kinds due to the diversity of its objects and the diversity of its modes of occurrence, it is said, 'whatever.'

Dānamukhenāti dānena mukhabhūtena, dānaṃ pamukhaṃ katvāti attho.

'With giving as the foremost' means with giving as the principal thing; the meaning is, having made giving primary.

“Ariyamaggasampāpanavasenā”ti iminā anukampānuddayānaṃ ekantānavajjatameva vibhāveti. “Anukampā”ti padassatthavivaraṇaṃ “karuṇāyanā”ti, itarassa “mettāyanā”ti.

"By way of bringing about the Noble Path"—this clarifies that compassion and kindness are wholly faultless. The explanation of the term "compassion" is "the act of being compassionate," while for the other it is "the act of being friendly."

102. Pakatiādīti ādisaddena aṇuissarapajāpatipurisakālādhiṭṭhāyakāriādike saṅgaṇhāti.

102. "Nature, etc."—by the word "etc." are included minor lords, progenitors, persons, time, determiners, agents, and others.

Kāmesūti kāmaguṇesu rūpādivisayesu.

"In sensual pleasures" means in the strands of sensual pleasure, in objects such as forms, etc.

Bahalakilesatāyāti bahulakilesabhāvena. Pubbahetumandatāyāti vivaṭṭūpanissayassa kusalassa akatattā.

"Due to the abundance of defilements" means due to the state of having abundant defilements. "Due to the weakness of past causes" means due to not having performed wholesome actions that are a decisive support for turning away from rebirth.

Cittavūpasamabhāvanāyāti cittavūpasamakarabhāvanāya samathavipassanāya.

"For the development of mental tranquility" means for the cultivation that produces mental tranquility, that is, for serenity and insight.

Parissayā [V 0.0127] sīlādiparipūraṇassa paribandhabhūtā kilesā eva. Anariyā paññāsīsaṃ ukkhipitvā ṭhātumeva na sakkontīti vuttaṃ “ñāṇasirena adhosirā hutvā”ti.

Dangers are the defilements that are obstacles to the fulfillment of virtue, etc. The ignoble are unable even to stand holding their heads up with wisdom; thus it is said, "with heads down in respect to the head of wisdom."

103. Bhagavato, bhikkhusaṅghassa ca vasanayogyabhāvo, tehi nivutthabhāvo ca tassa sātisayo vaṇṇoti vuttaṃ “paṭhamagāthāya jetavanassa vaṇṇaṃ kathetvā”ti. Vuttañhetaṃ –

103. Its suitability as a dwelling for the Blessed One and the community of monks, and the fact that they have dwelt there, is its surpassing quality; this is what is meant by 'having described the quality of the Jetavana in the first verse.' For it is said:

“Gāme [M 0.0134] vā yadi vāraññe, ninne vā yadi vā thale;

Yattha arahanto viharanti, taṃ bhūmirāmaṇeyyaka”nti. (dha. pa. 98; theragā. 991);

"Whether in a village or in the forest, in a low-lying place or on high ground; wherever Arahants dwell, that spot of earth is delightful."

Idha dhammasaddo samādhipariyāyo “evaṃdhammā te bhagavanto”tiādīsu (dī. ni. 2.13; ma. ni. 3.198; saṃ. ni. 5.378) viyāti āha “dhammoti samādhī”ti samādhipakkhikā dhammā sativāyāmā.

Here, the word "dhamma" is a synonym for concentration, as in such passages as "those Blessed Ones are of such a nature"; therefore, it is said, "Dhamma means concentration." The dhammas that are allied with concentration are mindfulness and effort.

Nānugaccheyyāti nānutaseyya. Anutasanameva hi taṇhādiṭṭhīhi anugamanaṃ. Paṭivipasseyyāti vipassanāsammasanamāha. Yamakato, hi khaṇikato, paṭipāṭito ca sammasanaṃ vipassanāyapi sammasanato paṭivipassanā nāma. Sā hi vipassanāya diṭṭhiugghāṭanamānasamugghāṭananikantipariyādānahetutāya visesato paṭipakkhena asaṃhīraasaṃkuppanahetubhūtā paribrūhanā hoti. “Punappunaṃ…pe… appento”ti etena nibbānārammaṇadhammānubrūhanaṃ yathā “brūhetā suññāgārāna”nti (ma. ni. 1.64) dasseti.

"Should not follow" means one should not be anxious. For anxiety itself is following by means of craving and views. "Should thoroughly investigate" refers to insight contemplation. Contemplation by way of pairs, by way of moments, or by way of sequence is called 'thorough investigation' (paṭivipassanā) because it is a contemplation even of insight itself. This thorough investigation, by being a cause for uprooting views, dispelling conceit, and mastering delight through insight, becomes a special cultivation that is a cause for being unshakeable and imperturbable, an antidote. By "Again and again... applying..." it shows the augmentation of states that have Nibbāna as their object, as in "a cultivator of empty dwellings" (Majjhima Nikāya 1.64).

Yaṃ kiñci apadisitvā paṭiññādānaṃ saṅgaro. So pana attano kiccavisesaṃ apadisitvā mittasanthavavasena vā kālāgamanaṃ apadisitvā kiñcikkhānuppadānena vā paṭibāhakaraṇaṃ apadisitvā balaggabodhavasena vā siyāti tassa mittakaraṇādipariyāyataṃ sandhāyāha “saṅgaroti…pe… nāma”nti. Evaṃ paṭipannattāti evaṃ aniccasaññāmukhena tiyaddhakesu saṅkhāresu appamādappaṭipattiyā paṭipannattā.

An entanglement (saṅgara) is the making of a pledge by citing some pretext. This may be done by citing one's own special duty, or by way of friendship and intimacy, or by citing the passage of time while giving nothing, or by citing an impediment, or by way of asserting one's superior strength; referring to its function as a way of making friends and so on, it is said, 'an entanglement... is the name'. "Because of having practiced thus" means because of having practiced with diligence regarding conditioned things in the three time periods, by way of the perception of impermanence.

Dibbacakkhu suvisuddhanti sāvasesā desanāti āha “yaṃ sacchikarotī”ti. Rūpāyatanañhettha adhippetaṃ.

To show that the statement, 'The divine eye is perfectly pure,' is a teaching with something left unsaid, the text adds, 'that which one realizes.' For here, the form-base is intended.

104. Antanti [V 0.0128] saṅkhārānaṃ pārimantabhūtaṃ. Vedānanti maggañāṇavedānameva. Arahattādhigamena antaṃ pariyosānaṃ gatattā. Kammavipākavaṭṭānaṃ, kilesavaṭṭassāpi ca ussadena upacayena ussadā, rāgādayo.

104. "The end" means the ultimate limit of formations. "Knowledges" refers specifically to the knowledges of the path, because by attaining Arahantship, one has reached the end, the final conclusion. Lust and so on are 'abundant' (ussadā) because of the swelling and accumulation of the rounds of kamma and its results, and also of the round of defilements.

Sukkobhāsatāya sukkā, abhivisiṭṭhaggahā. Sabbāni vā tārakarūpāni sukkā. Vindatīti upalabhati, paṭivijjhatīti attho.

They are bright due to their bright lustre, being pre-eminent celestial bodies. Or, all star-forms are bright. "One finds" (vindati) means one comprehends; one penetrates—that is the meaning.

“Ajjhattaṃ vipassanābhiniveso hotī”ti idaṃ “sakesu dhammesū”ti pāragubhāvassa visesitattā vuttaṃ, tañca kho abhiniveseneva desitaṃ. “Sabbaṃ[M 0.0135], bhikkhave, abhiññeyya”nti (saṃ. ni. 4.46; paṭi. ma. 1.3) vuttaṃ. Pāragutā ca tesaṃ khandhānaṃ pariññābhisamayavasena hoti. Tato ca nesaṃ hetubhūtasamudaye, tadappavattilakkhaṇe nirodhe, nirodhagāminiyā paṭipadāya ca pahānasacchikiriyābhāvanābhisamayapāripūrivasena itarasaccesupi pāragubhāvo vutto eva hoti. Sabbaso hi sakaattabhāvabodhenapi catusaccābhisamayo hotiyeva. Vuttañhetaṃ “imasmiṃyeva byāmamatte kaḷevare sasaññimhi samanake lokañca paññapemi, lokasamudayañcā”tiādi (saṃ. ni. 1.107; a. ni. 4.45). Atha vā sakesu dhammesūti attano dhammesu. Attadhammā nāma atthakāmassa kulaputtassa sīlādidhammā. Sīlasamādhipaññādayo hi vodānadhammā ekantahitasukhasampādanato purisassa sakadhammā nāma, na anatthāvahā saṃkilesadhammā viya paradhammā. Tesaṃ sīlādīnaṃ pāripūriyā pāraṃ pariyantaṃ gatoti pāragū. “Akkula pakkula”iti evaṃ vihiṃsanakapayogaṃ. Ajakalāpena (udā. 7) hi tadā bhagavantaṃ bhīsāpetukāmena kataṃ yakkhagajjitaṃ “akkula pakkula” iti iminā ākārena sattānaṃ sotapathaṃ agamāsi, tasmā taṃ “akkulaṃ pakkulakaraṇa”nti vuttaṃ.

"There is an inclination towards insight internally"—this is said because the state of having reached the far shore is specified by 'in one's own qualities,' and this is taught precisely as an inclination. It is said, "Monks, all should be fully understood" (Saṃyutta Nikāya 4.46; Paṭisambhidāmagga 1.3). And having gone beyond those aggregates occurs by means of the full understanding through direct knowledge. From that, having gone beyond is also spoken of in the other truths, by means of fulfilling the full understanding through abandoning, realizing, and developing with regard to their cause, the origin; with regard to cessation, which has the characteristic of their non-occurrence; and with regard to the path leading to cessation. For in every way, there is indeed a full understanding of the four truths even through the awakening to one's own personal existence. As it is said: "In this very fathom-long body, with its perception and mind, I make known the world, the origin of the world..." and so on (Saṃyutta Nikāya 1.107; Aṅguttara Nikāya 4.45). Alternatively, "in one's own qualities" means in one's own dhammas. One's own dhammas are the qualities such as morality, etc., of a clansman who seeks what is beneficial. For the purifying qualities—morality, concentration, wisdom, and so on—are called a person's 'own dhammas' because they bring about ultimate welfare and happiness, unlike the defiling qualities that bring harm, which are like 'another's dhammas.' Having reached the far shore, the limit, through the fulfillment of these—morality and so on—one is called "one who has gone beyond." "Akkula, pakkula"—this is a terrorizing expression. For at that time, the yakkha's roar, made by the goatherd Ajakalāpa with the desire to frighten the Blessed One, entered the ears of beings in the form of "akkula, pakkula." Therefore, it is said to be "the making of akkula, pakkula."

Nābhinandatīti “ayaṃ maṃ daṭṭhuṃ āgatā”ti na tussati. Yasmā pana “bhagavato bhāsitaṃ abhinandī”tiādīsu (ma. ni. 1.88) viya sampaṭicchanatthopi abhinandasaddo hoti, tasmā vuttaṃ “cittena na sampaṭicchatī”ti. Na socatīti “mayā asammoditā gacchatī”ti na cittasantāpaṃ āpajjati. “Saṅgā saṅgāmajiṃ mutta”nti idaṃ abhinandasocanānaṃ abhāvassa kāraṇavacanaṃ.

"He does not delight" means he is not pleased, thinking, "She has come to see me." However, since the word abhinandati can also mean acceptance, as in "he accepted the Blessed One’s words" (Majjhima Nikāya 1.88), it is said, "he does not accept with his mind." "He does not grieve" means he does not feel mental distress, thinking, "She is leaving without my having conversed with her." "Freed from attachments, victorious in battle" is a statement explaining the reason for the absence of delight and grief.

Tenāti udake nhānena. Tenevāha “na udakena sucī hotī”ti. Tassattho – udakummujjanādinā neva sattānaṃ suci pāpato suddhi nāma hotīti. Udakummujjanādīni hi idha [V 0.0129] uttarapadalopena “udaka”nti vuttaṃ. Udakenāti vā ummujjanādikiriyāsādhanabhūtena udakena sattānaṃ suci pāpasuddhi na hotīti. Atha vā sucitena yathāvuttena udakena pāpamalato suddho nāma satto na hotīti. Yadi siyā, sabbesameva macchabandhānaṃ pāpasuddhi siyā. Tenāha “bahvettha nhāyatī jano”ti. Mātughātādipāpakammakārīnaṃ, aññesañca gomahiṃsādīnaṃ udakaṃ orohantānaṃ antamaso macchakacchape upādāya sabbesampi pāpasuddhi siyā[M 0.0136], na panevaṃ hoti. Kasmā? Nhānīyapāpahetūnaṃ appaṭipakkhabhāvato. Yañhi yaṃ vināseti, so tassa paṭipakkho. Yathā āloko andhakārassa, vijjā avijjāya, na evaṃ nhānaṃ pāpassa, tasmā niṭṭhamettha gantabbaṃ “na udakena sucī hotī”ti. Yena pana suci hoti, taṃ dassetuṃ “yamhi saccañcā”tiādi vuttaṃ. Tattha saccanti vacīsaccañca viratisaccañca. Atha vā saccanti ñāṇasaccañceva paramatthasaccañca. Dhammoti seso ariyadhammo. Saccassa panettha visuṃ gahaṇaṃ tassa bahukāratādassanatthaṃ. Sesaṃ suviññeyyameva.

"By that" means by bathing in water. Hence it is said, "One does not become pure by water." The meaning is this: by immersion in water and the like, beings do not become pure, that is, purified from evil. For here "immersion in water and so on" is stated as "water" by elision of the final words. Alternatively, "by water" means that by water, which is the instrument for actions like immersion, beings do not become pure, do not become purified from evil. Or else, no being becomes purified from the stain of evil by the aforementioned water, which is considered pure. If it were so, all fishermen would be purified from evil. Hence it is said, "Many people bathe here." Those who commit evil deeds like matricide, and others who descend into the water, such as those who harm cows and buffaloes, down to the last fish and turtle—all would be purified from evil, but this is not the case. Why? Because bathing is not the counteragent for the causes of evil. For whatever destroys something is its counteragent. Just as light is the counteragent of darkness, and knowledge of ignorance, so bathing is not the counteragent of evil. Therefore, the conclusion here must be: "One does not become pure by water." To show by what one does become pure, it is said, "In whom there is truth," etc. Herein, "truth" means verbal truth and the truth of abstinence. Alternatively, "truth" means the truth of knowledge and ultimate truth. "Dhamma" refers to the rest of the noble Dhamma. The separate mention of "truth" here is to show its great efficacy. The rest is easily understood.

Jātibalanisedhakanti jātimattabrāhmaṇānaṃ bhovādikānaṃ paṭisedhakaṃ. Jātivādassa vā nisedhakaṃ, “na jaccā brāhmaṇo hotī”ti (su. ni. 655) hi vuttaṃ. Thero hi tathāvādena te aniggaṇhantopi niggaṇhanto viya hotīti katvā vuttaṃ.

'Denying the power of birth' means refuting those brahmins who are such merely by birth and address others with 'bho'. Or it denies the doctrine of birth, for it is said, 'Not by birth is one a brahmin' (Snp 655). For the Elder, by speaking in such a way, is as if rebuking them, even while not directly rebuking them; for this reason it is said.

105. Vimuttiyanti anupādisesanibbānadhātuyaṃ.

105. ‘In liberation’ means in the Nibbāna-element without residue.

Savāsananti ettha khīṇāsavassāpi akhīṇāsavasadisakāyavacīpayogahetubhūtā santāne kilesabhāvanā vāsanā nāma āyasmato pilindavacchassa (a. ni. aṭṭha. 1.1.215; dha. pa. aṭṭha. 2.pilindavacchattheravatthu) vasalavohāro viya, saha vāsanāyāti savāsanaṃ, bhāvanapuṃsakañcetaṃ “visamaṃ candimasūriyā parivattantī”tiādīsu (a. ni. 4.70) viya. Yathāvuttavāsanampi asesetvāti attho. Kummaggapariharaṇavasena maggasampaṭipattīti magge kusalo amaggepi kusalo eva hoti. Bhagavā pana sabbaññutāya sabbattheva kusaloti āha “magge ca amagge ca kovido”ti.

‘With latent tendencies’ (savāsana) here means that even for one whose taints are destroyed, there is a latent tendency (vāsanā), which is the perfuming of defilements in the mental continuum that is the cause of bodily and verbal conduct similar to that of one whose taints are not destroyed—like the venerable Pilindavaccha’s habit of addressing others as ‘outcaste’ (AN Aṭṭh. 1.1.215; Dhp-A 2, Pilindavacchattheravatthu). ‘With the latent tendency’ is savāsana, and this is an abstract noun in the neuter gender, as in phrases like ‘unevenly do the sun and moon revolve’ (AN 4.70). The meaning is that even the aforementioned latent tendency is entirely abandoned. By avoiding the wrong path and correctly practicing the Path, one who is skilled in the path is also skilled in what is not the path. The Blessed One, however, through his omniscience, is skilled in all things; thus he said: ‘skilled in the path and in what is not the path.’

106. Tamena yuttoti yathāvuttatamo tassa atthīti tamo, puggalo. Appakāsabhāvena ṭhitā khandhāva tamo. Ālokabhūtoti jātiguṇāloko, pākaṭaguṇoti attho.

106. ‘Endowed with darkness’ means he possesses the darkness described above; he is a person with darkness. The aggregates that persist due to their nature of non-radiance are themselves the darkness. ‘Become light’ means the light of innate qualities; the meaning is that his qualities are manifest.

Kilesamayaṃ [V 0.0130] bandhanaṃ “daḷha”nti vadanti. Yato saccāni paṭivijjhantā buddhāva naṃ chindanti, na aññe.

The bondage consisting of defilements is called ‘strong’ because only Buddhas, having penetrated the truths, cut it—no others.

Ducchedanatthena [M 0.0137] satipi daḷhabhāve sithilavuttitaṃ tassa dīpetuṃ “bandhanabhāvampī”tiādimāha. Tena “aho sukhumataraṃ kho, bhikkhave, mārabandhana”nti vuttaṃ.

To show its loose nature, even though it is firm in the sense of being difficult to cut, he said, ‘even the state of being a bond’ and so on. Therefore, it is said: ‘Indeed, monks, very subtle is the bond of Māra.’

107. Yadipi cetanā kusalākusalasādhāraṇā, apuññābhisaṅkhāro idhādhippetoti tassa vasena atthaṃ dassento “akusalacetanāvasena cetetī”ti āha. Cetanaṃ abhisandahanaṃ, cittassa byāpārāpattibhāvena pavattīti attho. Yasmā pana cetanā yadā viññattiṃ samuṭṭhāpeti, tadā diguṇussāhādiguṇavāyāmā viya hutvā pākaṭaṃ payogaṃ nipphādeti, tasmā “pakappetī”ti vuttā. Pākaṭappayogakappanañhettha pakappanaṃ adhippetaṃ. Tenāha “tameva pakappetī”ti. Paccayaṭṭho idha ārammaṇatthoti vuttaṃ “pavattiyā paccayo hotī”ti.

107. Although volition is common to both wholesome and unwholesome, demeritorious formation is what is intended here. Therefore, to explain its meaning in this respect, it is said: ‘It intends by way of unwholesome volition.’ Volition is coordination; the meaning is its occurrence by way of the mind's activity and attainment. Moreover, because when volition generates intimation, it becomes like double effort and exertion, producing a manifest application, therefore it is said: ‘It plans.’ Here, planning is intended to mean the contriving of a manifest application. Therefore, it is said: ‘It plans that very thing.’ The meaning of ‘condition’ here is ‘object,’ as stated: ‘It is a condition for occurring.’

108. Yathā jalasamuddassa vīcisamuṭṭhānavasena labbhamāno vego “vīcimayo”ti vuccati, evaṃ cakkhusamuddassāpi rūpāvabhāsanavasena labbhamāno vego “rūpamayo”ti vutto. Eseva nayo sesesupi. Āviñchanatoti ākaḍḍhanato, ākaḍḍhanañcettha santānassa tanninnabhāvahetutāya daṭṭhabbaṃ.

108. Just as the force arising by way of the arising of waves in the ocean of water is called ‘wave-made,’ so too the force arising by way of the appearance of forms in the eye-ocean is called ‘form-made.’ This same principle applies to the rest. ‘From drawing in’ means from attracting; and here, attraction should be understood as the cause for the continuum's state of inclining toward that.

Samudanaṃ kilesatemanaṃ, avassavahetutā, kilesānaṃ ūmiādisadisatā samāvaṭṭanena sattānaṃ anatthāvahatāya veditabbā. Uparūpariveguppattiyā upagatassa uṭṭhātuṃ appadānena, guṇasāravināsanena ca kodhupanāhādīnaṃ ūmiādisadisatā daṭṭhabbā.

The flowing together is the moistening by defilements, due to being the cause of outflows. The similarity of the defilements to waves and so on should be understood by their whirling around and bringing harm to beings. The similarity of anger, resentment, and the like to waves and so on is to be seen in their arising with successively increasing force, in not allowing one who has encountered them to rise up, and in destroying the essence of virtue.

Abhimukho nandatīti tadārammaṇaṃ sukhaṃ somanassaṃ sādiyanto sampaṭicchati. Abhivadatīti taṇhābhinivesavasena abhinivissa vadati. Tañhissa abhinivesaṃ dīpetuṃ “aho sukha”ntiādi vuttaṃ. Ajjhosānaṃ adhimuccanabhūtāya taṇhāya taṇhāvatthukassa anupavisitvā āveṇikatākaraṇanti āha “ajjhosāya tiṭṭhatīti gilitvā pariniṭṭhapetvā”ti.

‘He delights facing it’ means he accepts it, relishing the happiness and joy related to that object. ‘He speaks facing it’ means he speaks, having clung firmly by way of the adherence of craving. To reveal this adherence of craving, phrases like ‘Ah, happiness!’ are spoken. Clinging (ajjhosāna) is the making something one's own by entering into the object of craving through craving that is of the nature of conviction; thus he says, ‘he dwells clinging’ means ‘having swallowed, having completely finished.’

109. “Kasmā”tiādinā santāpadukkhānaṃ asuppatikārataṃ āha “yena vā pakārenā”ti. Yenāti yena vā kāmajjhosānadiṭṭhijjhosānabhūtena micchābhinivesappakārena. Gahaṭṭhapabbajitā tathārūpaṃ katvā [M 0.0138] attano vaḍḍhiñca maññanti. Avaḍḍhi eva pana hoti tassa pakārassa [V 0.0131] vaḍḍhiyaṃ anupāyabhāvato ca upāyabhāvato ca avaḍḍhiyaṃ. Tathāpīti tattha tattha icchāvighātaṃ pāpuṇantopi. Yasmā ito bāhirakā sabbena sabbaṃ bhavanissaraṇaṃ appajānanto mandakilesaṃ dīghāyukaṃ sukhabahulaṃ ekaccaṃ bhavaṃ teneva mandakilesādibhāvena “nibbāna”nti samanupassanti, tasmā bhavena bhavavippamokkhaṃ vadantīti.

109. By ‘Why?’ and so on, he states the irremediable nature of torment and suffering, with the words ‘by whatever kind.’ ‘By whatever kind’ means by whatever kind of wrong adherence that consists of obsession with sensual desires and obsession with views. Householders and renunciants, having acted in such a way, consider it their own growth. But it is only decline, since that kind of method is a non-means for growth and a means for decline. ‘Even so’ means: even while encountering the frustration of their desires here and there. Since those outside this teaching, not understanding at all the complete escape from existence, regard a certain existence—long-lasting, full of pleasure, with mild defilements—as ‘Nibbāna’ precisely because of that mildness of defilements and so forth, therefore they speak of release from existence through existence.

Bhavadiṭṭhisahagatā taṇhā purimapade uttarapadalopena bhavataṇhāti vuttāti āha “bhavataṇhātiādīsu viyā”ti.

Craving accompanied by the view of existence is called ‘craving for existence’ in the first term through the elision of the latter term; thus he says, ‘as in “craving for existence” and so on.’

Yatthāti yasmiṃ bhave.

‘Yattha’ means in whichever existence.

Tato evāti bhūtaratiyā eva. Aññamaññañhi sattānaṃ chandarāgo balavā hoti. Anavasesatoti anavasesena, na kiñci sesetvā.

‘Tato eva’ means precisely due to delight in beings. For the desire and lust of beings for one another is indeed strong. ‘Anavasesato’ means without remainder, leaving nothing behind.

Saṃsārasotassa anukūlabhāvena gacchatīti anusotagāmī. Tasseva paṭikkūlavasena nibbidānupassanādīhi pavattatīti paṭisotagāmī, acalappasādādisamannāgamena ṭhitasabhāvoti attho.

Because he goes in accordance with the stream of saṃsāra, he is one who goes with the stream (anusotagāmī). Because he proceeds against that very stream by means of disenchantment, insight-contemplation, and so on, he is one who goes against the stream (paṭisotagāmī); the meaning is one whose nature is steadfast, being endowed with unwavering confidence and so on.

110. “Palabbhati, nikhajjatī”tiādīsu viya upasaggo padavaḍḍhanamattanti āha “abhijātikoti jātiyo”ti. Kaṇhadhammasamannāgatattā vā kaṇho. Paṭhamavayepi majjhimavayepi pāpasamaṅgī hutvā ṭhito kaṇhadhamme abhijāyati, pacchāpi pāpaṃ pasavatīti attho. Sukkoti vā ettha vuttavipariyāyeneva attho veditabbo.

110. Just as in phrases like ‘palabbhati, nikhajjatī,’ and so on, the prefix is a mere extension of the word; thus he says, ‘abhijātiko means jātiyo (births).’ Or, he is ‘kaṇho’ (dark) because he is endowed with dark qualities. The meaning is: being associated with evil in his youth and middle age, he is born into dark states and later produces evil. Alternatively, ‘sukko’ (bright) here should be understood in the sense opposite to what has been stated.

111. Purimasminti purimasmiṃ pade. Visaye bhummaṃ tattha deyyadhammassa patiṭṭhāpanato. Dutiye adhikaraṇe, tadadhikaraṇañhi nibbānanti. Gahaṭṭhapabbajitakiccesu vā visiṭṭhadhammadassanatthaṃ paccayadānārahattānaṃ samadhuratāniddeso. Atha vā yena yena pana vatthunāti rūpārūpanirodhādinā taṇhāvatthunā. Amarāvikkhepavatthuādināti ettha ādi-saddena subhasukhādimicchābhinivesavatthuṃ saṅgaṇhāti. Yathā vā taṇhādiṭṭhiduccaritānaṃ vasena saṃkilesabhāgiyassa suttassa vibhāgo, evaṃ samathavipassanāsucaritavasena taṇhāvodānabhāgiyādisuttavibhāgoti dassetuṃ pāḷiyaṃ “taṇhā…pe… niddisitabba”nti vuttaṃ.

111. ‘In the first’ refers to the first term. The locative case is used in the sense of sphere because of the establishment of a gift there.
